# Four-wave mixing with output amplification

The feed-forward design uses frequency-multiplexed four-wave mixing (FWM). Products from several input and weight teeth add as optical fields at each selected idler frequency. A separate amplifier applies a common gain to the selected idlers before the next layer. The simulation question is how accurately the amplified fields represent the target matrix–vector product at the required output power. The recurrent investigation prioritizes optical inputs and optical weights. [Spatial and recirculating multiplier footprints](#footprint-of-a-64-channel-optical-multiplier) · [Earlier electronically addressed memory](#recurrent-memory-with-two-optical-waveguides)

The working target is above −10 dB from one full-scale input tooth to its intended full-scale output. Report gain before and after the amplifier, total useful output power, and vector field error at the same pump power. The 64-input Hankel example below is a structured test case. Its −24.3 dB per selected idler is a waveguide estimate with one lit input tooth, a 50 mW reference, and 50 mW spread across the weight comb.

## Four-wave mixing

Bragg-scattering FWM acts as a tunable beam splitter between optical frequencies. An optical weight and a fixed pump control the fraction of input light sent to a new frequency. With one data input and undepleted pumps, the output power equals that fraction times the data power. Calibrating the fraction gives a multiply even at high conversion. If only two tones may enter, degenerate FWM is the alternative. At weak conversion its idler power scales as data power times **squared** pump power, so a square-root encoding of a positive weight recovers the product. [Bragg-scattering theory](https://journals.aps.org/prresearch/pdf/10.1103/PhysRevResearch.7.023108) · [Measured quadratic pump dependence, Fig. 4](https://doi.org/10.1063/5.0239950)

**Single-pass length and power.** For weak, phase-matched degenerate FWM, idler conversion scales approximately as `(γ P L_eff)²`, where γ is waveguide nonlinearity, P is pump power, and L_eff is the effective interaction length. Lei et al. measured peak on-chip conversion of **−14.5 dB at 0.71 cm** and **−7.3 dB at 2.12 cm** in nonresonant AlGaAs-on-insulator spirals at the same **≈63 mW CW pump**. Their gain metric is output idler power divided by input signal power. The 2.12 cm result leaves 2.7 dB for other losses before reaching −10 dB. Fiber coupling in that experiment was about 3.2 dB per facet. [Lei et al., Fig. 2 and Eq. 2](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447)

**Resonant conversion.** Zhao et al.'s **71% on-chip transfer to one telecom idler (−1.5 dB)** used a silicon nitride microring, two pumps at **138 and 148 mW**, and a **21 µW signal**. Repeated circulation, resonance tuning, and gratings that suppressed unwanted idlers enabled that operating point. The 71% measures signal transfer. Pump consumption is a separate cost. A different silicon experiment with unequal pumps and a 55 mW peak control gave **5% total conversion (−13 dB)**. [Zhao et al., experimental details](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10347252) · [Unequal-pump measurement](https://opg.optica.org/ol/abstract.cfm?uri=ol-41-15-3651)

**Frequency multiplexing.** A high-Q ring can serve several comb teeth if each carrier and its modulation sidebands fit a resonance. Ye et al. enhanced only the pump, letting the signal and idler pass once. They measured −20 dB at 25 mW on-chip pump power. In a nonresonant guide, Kaminski et al. converted seven simultaneous 25 GHz-spaced channels with a shared 100 mW pump. The center channel’s signal-to-noise ratio peaked near 20 mW **total** signal input. Interchannel mixing lowered the optimum power per channel as more channels were added. [Ye et al., Fig. 5](https://doi.org/10.1063/5.0239950) · [Kaminski et al., Figs. 10–11](https://backend.orbit.dtu.dk/ws/files/192313703/HKKR_jlt_2933226_pp.pdf)

**Which matrices.** On a uniform, broadly phase-matched grid, a Bragg-scattering pump pair couples signal teeth a fixed frequency interval apart. The coupling matrix has shared diagonals (Toeplitz). In the weak-conversion limit, the generated field in a separate output band is a convolution. With a strong reference and independently set weight tones, a single selected output can collect a dot product, one distinct pump offset per input. Adjacent output rows reuse those offsets. At strong conversion the full transfer follows the coupled-mode equations. [Wang et al., Eqs. 1–2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41377-020-0299-7) · [Basani et al., Sec. II](https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevApplied.22.014009)

**Independently programmable rows.** For data on frequencies fₖ, choose the weight tone for column k at fᵣ + fₒ − fₖ. With a fixed reference R at fᵣ, the phase-matched products xₖwₖR* add as fields at one idler fₒ. Paquot et al. demonstrated a field dot product with up to three channels at 40 Gb/s. For a 64 × 64 design, hold or replay the vector across 64 row slots and load 64 new weight values per slot, or distribute the same vector to 64 separately weighted mixers. A fully spectral alternative in weak conversion spaces selected output idlers by 64 input-tooth spacings. The required weight offsets then have distinct indices 64i − k for all 4,096 entries. This construction assumes sufficient phase-matching bandwidth, suppression of other FWM products, and adequate power per tone. [Paquot et al.](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.22.002609) · [Bragg-scattering frequency rule](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41377-020-0299-7)

For two dense matrices, hold A and present the columns of B as successive vectors. Gu, Campbell and Yeh also demonstrated a spatial 4 × 4 matrix–matrix product in a photorefractive crystal. Their geometry makes all terms for one output pixel write the same grating, so they sum before optical readout. [Gu et al., Figs. 1–5](https://doi.org/10.1364/OL.18.000146)

**Hankel read.** Use a fixed reference tone R and a shaped weight comb h. For equally spaced input, weight and idler bands, choose the origins so `f_R + f_h[0] = f_x[0] + f_y[0]`. The phase-conjugating FWM term at `f_y[j] = f_R + f_h[i+j] − f_x[i]` gives `y_j ∝ R Σ_i h[i+j] conj(x_i)` in weak conversion. This reproduces one archived Hankel factor, with 127 programmable anti-diagonals and 64 selected outputs. The 4,096 paths need sufficiently uniform complex coupling along each anti-diagonal and isolation from other mixing products. [Classical FWM field equation, Eq. S5](https://journals.aps.org/prresearch/supplemental/10.1103/PhysRevResearch.7.023108/Supplemental_Material.pdf) · [Multi-pump sum-index coupling, Eq. 1](https://arxiv.org/html/2406.10715)

**Example carrier placement.** The data and weight values are the coherent field amplitudes on two separate combs. The sum of the reference frequency and each weight frequency plays the role of the old DFG pump frequency. An illustrative 10 GHz grid uses `f_x[i] = 199.20 + 0.010i` THz for 64 input teeth, `f_h[s] = 197.20 + 0.010s` THz for 127 weight teeth, and a single reference at 195.00 THz. Select the 64 idlers at `f_y[j] = 193.00 + 0.010j` THz. With only weight tooth 63 active, its 197.83 THz field couples each input i to output 63−i. [Dual-pump idler field, Eq. 16](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6115365/#Sec2)

Counting first-order products of the launched carrier centers gives 64 intended contributions per selected idler. The closest product from a different mixing family is 110 GHz from the selected band. The closest discarded idler from the intended family is 10 GHz away. These are frequency separations. Efficient operation also depends on phase matching across the occupied bands and filtering at the chosen modulation bandwidth. At the archived 100 GHz spacing, the 64 data teeth span 6.3 THz and the 127 weights span 12.6 THz, so that case needs its own band placement.

**Power retained by the filter.** For one lit input tooth, flat weak conversion, and fixed reference and total weight powers, split the weight power equally across the active tones. The Hankel read collects 64/127 (50.4%) of the generated idler power, with 1/127 per useful tooth. A single row collects 1/64 (1.56%). The fully spectral design with independent weights collects 64/4,096 (1.56%). For the same total generated idler energy, the Hankel read therefore delivers 15.1 dB more across all useful outputs and 3.0 dB less per output than the single row. With all 64 data teeth active, the row and Hankel reads have up to 127 and 190 idler positions, respectively. Both exclude 126 positions, whose power depends on interference. The [archived dense-unitary comparison](../archive/README.md) includes both conversions and the time sum needed for that arbitrary-matrix reader.

**Absolute gain for one Hankel stage.** Let η be the signal-generated power in the modeled idler family divided by the power of one lit input tooth, at the waveguide facets. In first-order co-polarized FWM, the dual-pump estimate scales from a single-pump measurement at power P₀ by `4 P_reference P_weights,total / P₀²`, assuming the same coupling and phase matching across the comb. An equal split of 63.1 mW therefore gives the measured single-pump peak as the estimate for η. Lei et al. measured −7.3 dB at 2.12 cm and a best peak of −5.47 dB in their length sweep. With 127 equal-power weight tones these imply −28.3 and −26.5 dB per useful tooth and −10.3 and −8.4 dB summed across 64 useful teeth. At 50 + 50 mW, quadratic extrapolation from the 2.12 cm result gives −24.3 and −6.3 dB, respectively. All comb values are model estimates before external losses. With only the central weight tone, each input maps to one reversed output and collects the full η on that path. [Lei et al., Fig. 2e–f](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Dual-pump coupling, Eq. 1](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.19.006648)

At 50 mW reference plus 50 mW across 127 equal-power weight teeth (394 µW each), one 200 µW data tooth gives an estimated 0.74 µW per useful output and 47 µW summed over 64 outputs. If all 64 data teeth carry 200 µW and the programmed field matrix is proportional to a unitary with equal-magnitude entries, the useful total is about 3.0 mW, or 47 µW per output on average. Individual output powers depend on the coherent field sums.

The [archived dense reader](../archive/README.md) also needs the second filtered factor and a coherent sum over 64 slots. A direct, uniformly phase-matched `b conj(a) x` term would have coefficients `Σ_l b[l+j−i] conj(a[l])`, with zero padding. These depend only on `j−i`. In the two-factor product the inner sum `Σ_{r=0}^{63} b[j+r] conj(a[i+r])` is restricted by the intermediate filter, allowing more general matrices. A useful first gain and fidelity test is `h[63]` alone, which reverses all 64 data teeth. Then enable multiple anti-diagonals at the same total weight power.

Hu et al. measured about +9 dB idler conversion for eight wavelength channels in nonlinear fiber using two CW pumps totaling about 0.59 W. Each input generated its own conjugate at the mirror frequency, corresponding to one anti-diagonal. With many programmed weights, strong parametric gain can amplify different superpositions of input teeth by different factors. To establish the maximum gain at a specified matrix error, fit a single field gain to the simulated transfer matrix across a pump sweep and track the residual. [Eight-channel phase conjugation](https://doi.org/10.1109/JLT.2014.2370038) · [Multimode parametric transfer](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.21.001374) · [Broadband on-chip parametric amplifier](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-08666-z)

Oliver et al. demonstrated three-way frequency mixing in **100 m of dispersion-shifted fiber**, sweeping the pumps up to a few watts peak. Their equal-pump field map is `y_i = x_i + q(x_1 + x_2 + x_3)` up to a common phase. It can be dense and full rank with one adjustable mixing strength. Successive independently controlled interactions can theoretically realize any unitary. For an arbitrary real matrix `W = U Σ Vᵀ`, orthogonal mixers implement U and Vᵀ, and independently set mode transmissions implement Σ after normalization. [Oliver et al., Eq. 2 and Discussion](https://journals.aps.org/prresearch/pdf/10.1103/PhysRevResearch.7.023108) · [Miller, optical singular-value decomposition](https://www-ee.stanford.edu/~dabm/430.pdf) · [FWM optical multiplication experiment](https://opg.optica.org/abstract.cfm?uri=ofc-2025-W1D.5)

These MVMs add **fields**. Direct detection measures the squared magnitude. If independent input phases average out, the mean output powers instead obey a nonnegative matrix with entries `|U_ij|²`. For a lossless frequency mixer at nearby wavelengths, one input spread equally over N outputs gives `1/N` power per tooth. That is −6.0 dB for 4 outputs, −9.0 dB for 8, and −18.1 dB for 64, before device losses. Total useful output power and power at one tooth answer different questions. [Derived from Oliver et al., Eq. 1](https://journals.aps.org/prresearch/pdf/10.1103/PhysRevResearch.7.023108)

**Square MVM energy.** For a specified nonzero real field matrix `W`, the largest passive common scale is `1/σmax(W)`. An orthogonal transform preserves the total signal photon flux for every input. For general `W` at that scale, the mean ideal fraction in useful outputs, averaged over equal-power single-tooth tests, is `sum(σ_i²)/(N σmax²)`. Pump energy is an additional cost. [Optical singular-value implementation](https://www-ee.stanford.edu/~dabm/430.pdf)

For a balanced 64 × 64 map, demanding −10 dB at each output from one illuminated tooth requires `64 × 0.1 = 6.4` times the input power across the useful outputs (+8.06 dB net at nearby wavelengths). For a multilayer analog simulation, set the target matrix normalization first, then fit `y = g W x + e`. Report the common power gain `|g|²`, the output error on the full-scale signal range, and energy per completed vector. This separates intentional small weights and cancellations from device attenuation and noise.

One candidate derived from Oliver’s selectively phase-matched model uses N equal-power pump tones with signs `s_i = ±1`. A π phase shift on the collective mode `s/√N` implements the reflection `y = x − (2/N) s (sᵀx)` in one interaction. At N = 4 every coefficient has magnitude 1/2. At N = 64 a single reflection sends only 1/1024 of a basis input to each other tooth. A balanced 64 × 64 transform can instead use three layers of four-mode reflections on different groups of teeth (16 blocks per layer). Both examples have tied weights. [Oliver et al., Supplement Secs. 5.2.1–5.2.3](https://journals.aps.org/prresearch/supplemental/10.1103/PhysRevResearch.7.023108/Supplemental_Material.pdf)

**On-chip pump estimate for simulation.** Combining that model with Lei’s measured `γ ≈ 437 W⁻¹m⁻¹` and `0.8 dB/cm` loss gives `φ = 2γ P_total L_eff`, where `L_eff = (1 − exp(−αL))/α` and `α = 18.4 m⁻¹`. In a 4 cm guide, a balanced 2 × 2 needs `φ = π/2`, about 64 mW total pump and −6.2 dB signal transfer per coefficient. The balanced 4 × 4 needs `φ = π`, about 127 mW total (32 mW per tone) and −9.2 dB per coefficient. With a continuous pump at R vectors/s, launched optical pump energy per vector is `P_total/R`. These estimates assume undepleted pumps, equal linear loss and selective coupling among the intended modes. Coupling, pump preparation, parasitic mixing and electrical power are outside the calculation. Lei measured single-probe conversion and Oliver demonstrated three modes in fiber. [Lei et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Oliver et al., Eqs. S91–S96](https://journals.aps.org/prresearch/supplemental/10.1103/PhysRevResearch.7.023108/Supplemental_Material.pdf)

### Repeated Hankel multiplication in a data loop

One comb-vector packet can visit the same FWM waveguide on successive laps. Select the 64 generated idlers, switch in a new 127-tooth weight comb on each visit, and extract the packet after the last visit. This eliminates the temporal summation lattice. The archived 64-slot reader sent each contribution through two conversions. The circulating packet experiences every conversion, filter and loop traversal in series. Output duration depends on pulse shaping and dispersion. Latency grows by one round trip per additional visit. [Editable recirculating Hankel cell schematic](../figures/recirculating_hankel_cell.svg)

For one fixed Hankel matrix H, two ideal visits give `H conj(H) x = H H† x` up to common gain, since H is symmetric. Repeating the pair takes powers of the same matrix. With independently programmable factors, Ye and Lim prove that a generic complex 64 × 64 matrix is a product of **33 Hankel matrices**. Their upper bounds are **66 for every invertible matrix** and **133 for every matrix including singular ones**. The factors can be complex even when the target is real. The 33-factor family is dense, so any target can also be approximated with unrestricted coefficients. On the phase-conjugating cell, leave the final factor as written and conjugate every other factor working backwards. An odd number of visits leaves the input conjugated, which is immaterial for real input. For an ordinary complex input and output, even depths **34, 66 and 134** suffice in the three cases. To add a visit, factor `J A` and append the Hankel reversal J, for which `J² = I`. The theorem assumes unrestricted factor coefficients and gives no optical power or conditioning guarantee. [Ye and Lim, Theorem 4.3, Corollary 5.3 and Theorem 5.5](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1307.5132#page=9)

**Factor order matters.** On teeth 0 through 63, program J with only weight tooth 63 lit and K with weight teeth 0 and 64 lit equally. Under H[i,j] = h[i+j], both unscaled factors are real orthogonal permutations. J then K shifts every input tooth up by one modulo 64, while K then J shifts it down by one. Normalizing the two pump combs multiplies both orders by the same scalar. For real factors, replaying the stored factors in reverse implements the transpose because each Hankel is symmetric. For two ideal FWM passes with complex programmed combs A followed by B, the forward map is B conj(A). Swapping the same combs yields A conj(B), its Hermitian adjoint. Thus arbitrary forward products cannot be reordered to simplify adjoint replay. A pairwise-commuting restriction on real Hankel factors would make every realizable product symmetric.

**Returning the idlers.** The relation `f_R + f_h[i+j] = f_A[i] + f_B[j]` allows the two data bands to exchange roles on alternate laps. In the one-way example above, the products H+H−H and H+H−R from the pumps alone together hit all 64 selected carrier centers on the return visit, even with data off. A two-way example at 10 GHz spacing uses 64-tooth data bands starting at **194.50 and 192.00 THz**, a reference at **199.00 THz** and 127 weight teeth starting at **187.50 THz**. Enumerating all first-order products of the launched carriers gives 64 intended contributions per output in both directions. The nearest other mixing family is 610 GHz away going from the higher data band to the lower and 110 GHz away on return. The nearest discarded idler from the intended family is 10 GHz away in both directions. The coupling, filter response, and loop gain across both data bands still need physical simulation. [Dual-pump mixing processes, Fig. 1 and Eq. 16](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6115365/)

**Power and precision.** In the flat weak-conversion model above, 50 mW each for the reference and total weights gives total idler efficiency `η ≈ 0.468` for one lit input tooth. At fixed pump power each selected matrix column has squared norm at most η. Hadamard's determinant inequality then gives `|det H|^(2/64) ≤ η` for the physical field matrix. Determinant magnitudes multiply across laps. Thus a 34-pass product implementing a unitary up to common gain has at most **−112 dB common power gain before amplification** in this model. This bound also covers unequal allocations of weight power across the teeth.

A balanced test uses weight phase `πs²/64` on tooth s and equal power on all 127 teeth. The selected matrix is proportional to a unitary and transmits a fraction `q = 64η/127 ≈ 0.236` of the signal power per pass. A +6.28 dB amplifier on each pass restores the signal. Assume a 5 dB noise figure, independent amplifier noise, only the 64 modeled data modes coupled to the selected idlers, quiet pumps and ideal routing. With amplifier power gain `G = 1/q` and noise factor `F = 10^0.5`, each compensated pass adds `1 + (FG−1)/2 ≈ 7.21` noise photons per matched output mode, including the conjugating conversion. After 34 passes this gives 245 photons per mode, or **12.5% added RMS field error** for a vector averaging 15,600 signal photons per mode. Additional phase-matched vacuum modes add spontaneous noise. Arbitrary factorizations transform the noise differently and may require larger intermediate gains. Compare both designs at equal pump energy per vector and error in the accepted output packet. [Lei et al., single-pump waveguide measurement used for the extrapolation](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [McKinstrie and Marhic, parametric noise theory](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.21.019437) · [Amplifier model](#amplifier-noise-between-layers)

**Forward energy per completed product.** One 34-factor program and the shared-interleaver sum both deliver one 64 × 64 matrix–vector product, or 4,096 useful real MACs. The loop reuses one *signal* amplifier 34 times. The summed cell runs its raw-idler amplifier across a 64-slot train and drives its high-gain final amplifier once per result. Compare total electrical energy and matched output field error on one input vector. Against the projected 814.5 pJ summed read, the complete product can spend **23.96 pJ per visit on average**. Even the 2.12 cm guide alone requires 9.71 ns for 34 visits at group index 4.04. Continuous loads over that flight are consequential. A continuous 50 mW guide reference at 40% delivery efficiency would alone require 1.21 nJ before DC distribution. [Summed-cell projection](#improving-one-fully-programmable-64--64-read) · [Guide used for the loop estimate](https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447)

An optimistic sensitivity uses 34 illuminated visits, 50 mW in the reference and weight comb during each 20 ps packet, a synchronized pulsed reference (24 ps equivalent width), and balanced factors with useful transfer q = 0.236. Delivering reference light costs 102 pJ on the electrical rails. Replenishing the 34 pJ of delivered weight light costs 358 pJ using the same 25% tap, 0.5 dB delivery loss, 0.2 dB other round-trip loss and 12.1% electrical-to-added-light efficiency as the sum, with aligned, fully used weight slots. This leaves 314 pJ on the rails under the summed-cell ceiling, or about **32 mW for the signal amplifier and all remaining loads** over the minimum flight. Charging any longer recirculation time reduces this power allowance. A 4 pJ packet losing 76.4% on every visit would require adding about 104 pJ of *signal light* across the visits. At an assumed 12.1% conversion that alone would consume about 859 pJ on the amplifier rail, and the weak-conversion assumption also becomes inconsistent with the pump-photon budget at this circulating power.

Alternatively accept the same 4 pJ input, attenuate on entry to a nominal 0.512 pJ circulating packet, and restore about 5.04 pJ on extraction for a unit-norm target. Ideal balanced factors then require 17.83 pJ of added signal light in total, including extraction. Applying the same 12.1% conversion to this pulsed signal gain gives (102 + 358 + 147)/0.95 = **639 pJ, or 156 fJ/MAC**, before unallocated bias, filters, drives, extra memory losses and controls. The efficiency of a loaded amplifier at the short packet duty cycle has not been established. This electrical sensitivity assumes ideal transfers and is independent of the accuracy check below. [SOA efficiency measurement](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26368213/)

**Accumulated nonlinear error at the low-energy point.** A separate CPU test programs the 34 real, 5%-perturbed Hankel factors from the locally full-rank Jacobian example (seed 216). It propagates the 64 retained idlers from each pass through the existing depleted four-field equations with 50 mW on each pump, 20 ps packets, 0.512 pJ after launch attenuation, and one constant scalar loop gain. The small-signal calculation reproduces the dense target on all 20 signed inputs. The depleted propagation gives **60.24%** deterministic complex-field RMS error on ten held-out inputs at nominal calibration. Fitting a single complex output gain on ten separate pilot inputs reduces held-out error to **31.03%**, before adding amplifier noise. Doubling temporal sampling and integration steps on one vector verifies numerical convergence. This is one unretuned factor program. Per-factor predistortion or a different factorization could change the result. The earlier 6.27% estimate includes modeled added noise for balanced factors and cannot serve as a total error budget. [Reproduce the forward test](hankel_product_forward.py) · [Local matrix-control test](#viability-of-a-34-factor-writable-matrix)

#### Forward MLP energy with 128-tooth cells and Monarch shuffles

Price one resident 128 × 128 block as a product of 66 successive Hankel factors in one FWM guide. Each visit uses a different 255-tooth weight packet. The even depth returns data to the launch band. Generic complex matrices admit 65 unrestricted Hankel factors at this width and an extra reversal allows even depth. The factorization theorem does not bound the required dynamic range, fidelity, or conditioning of the physical factors. An MLP can instead train a prescribed number of factors directly. [Factorization result](https://arxiv.org/abs/1307.5132) · [Cell layout](../figures/recirculating_hankel_cell.svg)

**One 128 × 128 forward read.** Start with the pulsed-reference assumptions from the 64-tooth product. A 50 mW reference and 50 mW total factor comb give an estimated useful power transfer of q = 0.468 × 128/255 = 0.2349 per balanced visit. The 20 ps data packet circulates at 1.024 pJ, matching the 64-wide design's energy per tooth. It enters from an already encoded 8 pJ packet and leaves at 10.07 pJ for a unit-norm target (+1 dB). With 66 visits, the reference costs 198 pJ on its rail and factor replenishment costs 694.65 pJ. The signal amplifier replaces 51.71 pJ of optical loss on the visits and adds 9.05 pJ for extraction. At 12.1% assumed efficiency that costs 502.10 pJ on its rail. At 95% DC distribution efficiency the total is **1.468 nJ per read, or 89.6 fJ per real MAC** for 16,384 local MACs. One MAC counts one multiply and accumulation. The guide reference is pulse-gated (24 ps equivalent width, 40% source-to-guide efficiency). The factor store uses a 25% tap, 0.5 dB delivery loss and 0.2 dB other round-trip loss. The FWM measurement used a single CW pump. The amplifier measurement reported peak efficiency on a separately loaded device. Conversion across 255 weight teeth, finite 20 ps waveforms and short-duty-cycle amplifier efficiency are extrapolations. [Converter measurement](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Amplifier measurement](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26368213/)

| One cell | Circulating packet | External input → output | nJ per read | fJ/local MAC | Added RMS field noise before extraction |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 64 teeth, 34 visits | 0.512 pJ | 4 → 5.04 pJ | 0.639 | 156.0 | 6.26% |
| 128 teeth, 66 visits, same total packet | 0.512 pJ | 4 → 5.04 pJ | 1.204 | 73.5 | 12.37% |
| 128 teeth, same energy per tooth | 1.024 pJ | 8 → 10.07 pJ | 1.468 | 89.6 | 8.74% |
| 128 teeth, matched added field noise | 2.000 pJ | 8 → 10.07 pJ | 1.888 | 115.3 | 6.26% |

The noise column assumes balanced transforms, independent amplifier noise at 5 dB noise figure, and spontaneous FWM in the retained modes. It excludes source noise and final extraction noise. At 2 pJ, the linear model would generate roughly 0.94 pJ across all intended idlers with only 1 pJ available from each pump during the 20 ps overlap. Pump depletion matters well before that limit. The cheaper electrical points also assume fully utilized factor packets and a pulsed reference. For example, one isolated 66-visit read spends at least 18.86 ns in the 2.12 cm guide at group index 4.04. Leaving the 50 mW reference on over that flight replaces its 198 pJ rail charge with 2.36 nJ. The baseline cell then costs approximately **228 fJ/MAC** before any additional continuous loads. Pipelining independent vectors could amortize such loads in proportion to measured utilization.

**Eight lanes and any number of banks.** One bank applies eight local blocks in parallel. A cyclic router distributes teeth across the next eight lanes, conserving the number of coordinates. Let n denote the number of banks, including the first and last. There are n−1 shuffles. Keep each internal lane at 1.024 pJ and restore the router's assumed 3 dB insertion loss immediately downstream. Each lane costs 1.38945 nJ per bank, with 0.00444 nJ per shuffle and a one-time 0.07871 nJ extraction increment at the final bank. These quantities include DC distribution. Each pumped lane pays its full reference and factor energy. An 8 × 8 router has been demonstrated with about 1.2 dB on-chip loss at 100 GHz spacing. The 3 dB allowance does not verify the transfer, crosstalk or group delay on the proposed comb. [Fan et al., 8 × 8 router measurement](https://opg.optica.org/jlt/abstract.cfm?uri=jlt-43-11-5366)

| Banks | Shuffles | nJ per 1,024-vector | fJ per local-block MAC | Balanced added RMS field noise |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | 0 | 11.75 | 89.6 | 8.7% |
| 2 | 1 | 22.90 | 87.3 | 12.4% |
| 3 | 2 | 34.05 | 86.6 | 15.2% |
| 4 | 3 | 45.20 | 86.2 | 17.5% |

The denominator is n × 8 × 128² real MACs to evaluate the same block graph with ordinary dense local matrices. Dividing the three-bank result by 1,024² instead gives 32.5 fJ per dense-equivalent MAC. The energy per vector is identical, and these structured banks describe a restricted family of global matrices. At fixed photon energy, the noise variances add for the balanced model. Holding the end-to-end noise fraction fixed as n grows requires more photons per tooth. Increasing circulating power also increases depletion. The table models one linear MLP transform. For an optoelectronic activation or memory interface, add its incremental receiver, electronics and relaunch energy at each physical crossing. A cost of 1 pJ per real coordinate adds 1.024 nJ per full-vector crossing. Already budgeted optical gain must be counted only once.

**Fidelity and scope of the efficiency claim.** For a separate stress test, generate a signed-real 66-factor program by perturbing real orthogonal Hankel factors by 5%. Normalize the query, compute the exact product as the target, and propagate the same query through the depleted four-field model with one fixed loop gain. Six held-out signed inputs at 1.024 pJ gave **74.51% nominal field RMS error and 42.85% after one output scalar fitted on six different inputs**. At 0.512 pJ the calibrated result was 39.40%. The weak model reproduces the programmed matrix. Doubling temporal sampling and integration resolution changed the final probe by less than 4.1 × 10⁻⁸ relative RMS. This test omits stochastic noise. The balanced-factor noise estimates above belong to a different model. The results support a component-energy projection and leave task accuracy and an advantage over electronics open. A directly trained 16-factor block would cost 0.416 nJ per lane at 1.024 pJ. In the same deterministic stress test one 16-factor program had 33.6% calibrated field error. Comparing validation loss at equal energy per token would test whether shorter products are adequate for the MLP. 

Run `python3 notes/hankel_product_energy.py` for the energy table. Run `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1 python3 notes/hankel_product_forward.py --width 128 --depth 66 --packet-pj 1.024 --probes-per-split 6` for the forward precision test. Use `--visits 16` in the budget and `--depth 16` in the propagation for a shorter product. Both budgets exclude initial encoding, static weight loading, unallocated bias, switching and trimming, cooling, and optoelectronic activation. [Electrical model](hankel_product_energy.py) · [Depleted propagation](hankel_product_forward.py)

##### How many banks can represent an arbitrary 1,024-wide matrix?

Assume a linear optical stack with eight lanes, an arbitrary real 128 × 128 block programmable in every lane, and the fixed cyclic shuffle `(lane, tooth) → ((lane + tooth) mod 8, tooth)`. Each ordered source/destination lane pair carries 16 coordinates. These bounds apply to the ideal block matrices. A separate local synthesis and accuracy check is required for their implementation with 66 FWM visits. The following construction is for this shuffle and these dimensions. [Original Monarch architecture](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v162/dao22a.html)

| Banks | Optical shuffles | Mathematical capability | Electrical projection per vector | fJ per dense 1,024² MAC |
|---:|---:|---|---:|---:|
| 3 | 2 | Restricted family of global matrices | 34.05 nJ | 32.5 |
| 8 | 7 | At most 933,888 independent matrix dimensions | 89.80 nJ | 85.6 |
| 9 | 8 | Full local control near one permutation setting | 100.95 nJ | 96.3 |
| 17 | 16 | Explicit construction for every real matrix in ideal arithmetic | 190.16 nJ | 181.4 |

**Why eight fall short.** Each bank has 8 × 128² = 131,072 scalar controls. At a boundary, each of the 64 groups of 16 coordinates can undergo an invertible 16 × 16 basis change which the next bank undoes. This leaves the global map unchanged. With invertible banks these reparameterizations act freely, as can be seen by fixing the first bank and proceeding along the chain. Every invertible target requires invertible banks. Thus the dimension is at most `131072 n − 16384 (n−1)` for n banks. Nine is the first depth allowing all 1,048,576 independent directions.

**Why nine attain local control.** Label the 64 groups of 16 coordinates by the points of an 8 × 8 affine plane over the finite field with eight elements. Each of its nine parallel classes partitions the groups into eight lanes. Run one bank per class. Each consecutive source and destination lane intersects in exactly one group of 16, so local permutations can assign exactly the teeth required by the fixed shuffle. Any two groups share a lane in one class. At the nominal permutation program a perturbation of one coefficient in that lane therefore supplies its individual input-to-output matrix direction. All 1,024² coordinate directions occur. The real inverse function theorem gives a neighborhood of realizable matrices. The size of this neighborhood and its usable optical dynamic range remain open. [Finite affine planes and parallel classes](https://www.math.colostate.edu/~hulpke/lectures/m501/notes.pdf)

**A 17-bank exact construction.** Factor the target by pivoted elimination into input/output permutations, a unit upper triangular matrix, a diagonal (possibly with zeros), and a unit lower triangular matrix, applied in that order between the endpoint permutations. Nine banks implement each triangular factor. Assign every required off-diagonal entry to one bank where its two indices share a lane. In the product of unit triangular factors, an entry at distance d from the diagonal equals its assigned coefficient plus products of coefficients at distances smaller than d. Choosing coefficients in increasing distance therefore matches every target entry. This works in any order of the nine lane partitions.

To include pivot permutations using the fixed wiring, label the physical input and output lane partitions in the pivoted logical coordinates. Choose a middle partition that intersects every input and output lane in exactly 16 coordinates per middle lane. One construction makes a bipartite edge for each logical coordinate between its two endpoint lanes. The resulting 128-regular bipartite multigraph splits into 128 perfect matchings. Assign 16 matchings to each middle lane. For the first triangular factor, take the input and middle partitions as the first and last parallel classes of its affine plane. For the second, do the same with the middle and output partitions, using its own plane. The two middle banks and the diagonal merge into one block-diagonal bank. This gives 9 + 9 − 1 = 17 banks, including for rank-deficient targets. Nine is necessary and seventeen sufficient for arbitrary real targets under the ideal block assumption. The minimum for all targets is open here. [Perfect matchings in regular bipartite multigraphs](https://www.math.tau.ac.il/~krivelev/teaching/co22/LectureNotes.pdf)

The verification enumerates all 1,048,576 first-order directions in the 1,024-coordinate nine-bank construction. An explicit 64-coordinate reconstruction uses the same eight-port shuffle at each of 16 boundaries. Its relative Frobenius errors were 4.50 × 10⁻¹⁵ on a full-rank target and 2.71 × 10⁻¹⁵ on a rank-32 target. These numerical checks use ideal block arithmetic. [Reproduce the depth and routing checks](monarch_depth_bounds.py)

The energy column charges every bank the earlier 66-visit balanced budget at fixed photons per tooth. Exact algebra does not establish that the triangular weights have that transfer or that the output meets a given precision. For nine and seventeen banks, the balanced calculation alone gives 26% and 36% added RMS field noise before extraction. The earlier three-bank number can describe a learned MLP at task-matched accuracy, or a structured map priced against its dense evaluation. It cannot price the execution of the additional banks. [Electrical model](hankel_product_energy.py)

##### Reducing the energy of the 66-pass cell

For nine banks at 1.024 pJ of circulating data per lane, the nominal 96.28 fJ per dense-equivalent MAC comprises 50.21 fJ for replacing tapped weight light, 31.49 fJ for restoring the data on every pass and extracting the final output, 14.31 fJ for the reference, and 0.27 fJ for restoring loss in all eight shuffles. At current component powers a bank costs about 11.15 nJ per additional 1,024-vector traversal. Improving passive shuffle insertion loss alone has little effect on the electrical total. These figures exclude additional fixed bias and optical/electrical interfaces.

**Rebalance the two pumps.** The reference costs 3.00 pJ on its electrical rail per visit at 50 mW optical. The 50 mW stored weight costs 10.52 pJ. In the weak FWM model, holding the product of their in-guide powers fixed holds the transfer fixed. At reference power ratio r relative to 50 mW the two electrical charges sum to `3.00 r + 10.52/r` pJ. This has a minimum near r = 1.873. The coupled four-field model also tests how lower weight power affects depletion.

| In-guide reference / total weight comb | One cell, fJ/local MAC | Nine banks, fJ/dense MAC | Seventeen banks, fJ/dense MAC | One-cell deterministic field error after calibration |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 50 / 50 mW | 89.6 | 96.3 | 181.4 | 42.85% |
| 75 / 33.33 mW | 81.1 | 86.7 | 163.3 | 42.75% |
| 93.65 / 26.70 mW | 79.9 | 85.4 | 160.8 | 43.72% |

The error column uses the same 66-factor signed-real program, six calibration and six independent test inputs, a constant loop gain, and a 20 ps, 1.024 pJ signal. Nominal errors before scalar calibration were 74.51%, 74.21% and 75.13%, respectively. The first two pump splits are effectively tied in this small test. The energy columns use the balanced weak-transfer budget. At nine banks it predicts 26.3% added RMS field noise before extraction. The deterministic one-bank and balanced nine-bank figures describe different models. [Energy script](hankel_product_energy.py) · [Propagation script](hankel_product_forward.py)

**Component targets at fixed optical fields.** At the original 50/50 mW split, a weight packet delivers 1 pJ for 10.52 pJ at the electrical rail, or 9.50% net efficiency. For a fixed MLP, a source that supplies the same sequence of 66 distinct 255-tooth weight packets at 20% net efficiency would use 5.00 pJ per visit. It would give 69.9 fJ per dense-equivalent MAC at nine banks and 131.6 fJ at seventeen. That 20% must include the source, shaping the complex comb, selecting the next packet, and delivery to the guide. The initial programming of static coefficients may be amortized. Separately, raising the electrical-to-added-light efficiency of both weight and data amplifiers from 12.1% to 18%, with the original 50/50 split, gives 69.4 fJ at nine banks. Combining 18% amplifier efficiency with the 75/33.33 split gives 65.3 fJ. These are hardware targets that require the same optical gain and noise performance at the actual pulse duty cycle. Cheung et al. reported an amplifier design reaching 35% efficiency under a particular simulated load and later measured 12.1% peak efficiency in a fabricated device. [Amplifier design](https://opg.optica.org/jlt/abstract.cfm?uri=jlt-31-24-4057) · [Device measurement](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26368213/)

Use `python3 notes/hankel_product_energy.py --banks 9 17 --reference-mw 75` for the pump split. Set `--amplifier-efficiency .18` for the amplifier sensitivity, or use the default pumps and `--weight-delivery-efficiency .20` for the alternate weight supply. Add `--reference-mw 75` to the 128-wide forward stress test above to check depleted propagation. These budgets do not establish a 10%-error operating point or a measured MLP energy advantage.

##### Replacing FWM with DFG in one 128-wide recirculating cell

A single periodically poled lithium niobate guide can apply one signed-real Hankel factor per visit. Let telecom bands A and B use the same pitch Δ. Place weight tooth s at ν_A,0 + ν_B,0 + sΔ, near 775–800 nm. Incident tooth j in A then drives tooth i in B through weight i+j. The output field is proportional to Σⱼ wᵢ₊ⱼ xⱼ*, and the next visit exchanges A and B. The independent FWM reference drops out. The weight supply must now deliver 255 programmed teeth spanning 12.7 THz at a 50 GHz pitch. Li et al. measured 20.9 mW of idler from 132 mW of seed and 85 mW of pump in a 1 cm guide, or −8.0 dB seed-to-idler power transfer. Their broadband measurement swept the seed with a fixed pump. The joint response across the weight and data combs remains a design assumption. [Li et al., Figs. 3–4](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12696117/)

| 66-visit local read | Weight power | Circulating packet | Nominal loop gain | fJ/local MAC | Added field RMS, balanced | Deterministic field RMS, calibrated |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| FWM with 75 mW reference | 33.3 mW | 1.024 pJ | 6.29 dB | 81.1 | 8.74% | 42.75% |
| DFG, κ = 2 W⁻¹ | 33.3 mW | 1.024 pJ | 14.75 dB | 69.2 | 22.47% | 26.21% |
| DFG, κ = 2 W⁻¹, 10% noise target | 81.3 mW | 2.152 pJ | 10.88 dB | 146.0 | 10.00% | 40.02% |
| DFG, κ = 2 W⁻¹, same noise as FWM | 93.0 mW | 2.469 pJ | 10.30 dB | 165.5 | 8.74% | 43.04% |
| DFG, hypothetical κ = 9.36 W⁻¹ | 50.0 mW | 1.024 pJ | 6.29 dB | 76.9 | 8.74% | 67.08% |

Here κ denotes the assumed weak single-triplet ratio of idler power to the product of weight and seed powers, including guide length. At Li's loaded operating point that ratio was 1.86 W⁻¹. The illustrative 2 W⁻¹ coefficient and the stronger-guide case both assume a flat response. For balanced factors the fraction q of incident data power transferred to retained idlers is approximately (128/255)κP with total weight power P in watts. The electrical budget charges 9.50% net rail-to-shaped-weight efficiency, 12.1% electrical-to-added-signal efficiency, 95% DC delivery, and restoration to the same 10.07 pJ output. The first efficiency is unverified at the new wavelength. Fixed bias, encoding of the external input and extra routing losses are excluded.

The balanced field-noise estimate uses 66 independent visits, a 5 dB amplifier noise figure F (linear value 3.16), and one matched temporal mode per tooth. Writing E for circulating energy and r for RMS noise gives r² = 66 × 128 × hν × (1 + F/q)/(2E). For each noise target in the table, substitute E from this expression and minimize the electrical budget over P. In consistent pJ units the per-read budget is [66 × (20P/0.095 + (1−q)E/0.121) + (10.07−E)/0.121]/0.95. The deterministic column propagates depleted three-wave fields with ideal phase matching, one fixed loop gain and no added noise. A single output scalar fitted on six inputs is frozen before scoring six independent signed inputs for the same factor program as the FWM check. The budget assumes balanced output levels and excludes the cost of correcting the measured scalar. These two error columns have separate meanings. [DFG propagation and budget](hankel_product_dfg.py) · [FWM propagation](hankel_product_forward.py)

The stronger-guide example also tests the pump energy balance. At q = 0.235 a 1.024 pJ signal generates 0.241 pJ of retained idler. Each idler photon consumes one pump photon carrying about twice its energy. Under flat conversion and equal mean tooth powers, the retained 128 teeth capture on average 128/255 of total generated idler energy. The weak model would therefore consume about 0.958 pJ of pump, or 96% of the 50 mW, 20 ps weight packet. Depletion changes the programmed transfer. The depletion fraction depends on the factor program and acceptance of out-of-band idlers. Run `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1 python3 notes/hankel_product_dfg.py` for the 33.3 mW case. Use `--pump-mw 93 --packet-pj 2.46918` for the same-noise case or `--eta 9.36 --pump-mw 50` for the stronger guide.

##### Footprint and throughput density of the 128-wide recirculating MLP

Use one 2.12 cm FWM guide for all 66 visits by a 128-coordinate data packet. The separate weight ring stores 66 different 255-tooth factor packets. Count the ring's timed replacement switch, read tap, gain and local optical interfaces. Exclude the length of the storage fiber. The short data return is part of the cell. These are summed areas for a side-by-side hybrid assembly. [Cell topology, drawn at 64 teeth](../figures/recirculating_hankel_cell.svg)

| Per-cell allocation | mm² | Basis |
|---|---:|---|
| Nonlinear spiral and its site margin | 0.28 | 0.70 × 0.40 mm site around a demonstrated 0.60 × 0.30 mm spiral. [Lei et al.](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) |
| Data load/hold/release and idler selection | 2.00 | Two broadband MZIs. Each receives 4 mm × 0.25 mm. The electrode length is demonstrated and the site width is assumed. [Ekici et al.](https://doi.org/10.1002/qute.202300195) |
| Weight-packet replacement and gated read tap | 2.00 | Two further MZIs, independently controlled. The replacement switch admits the new factor packet and diverts the old one. |
| Signal gain and weight-ring replenishment | 1.50 | Two 1.50 × 0.50 mm bare SOA dies. This is a size proxy. Gain and saturation across the 128 data and 255 weight teeth require validation. [SemiNex CHP-305](https://mrp.seminex.com/PI_Sheet.aspx?seminex_part_pk=2229) |
| Pump multiplexer, band filters, trim, short return, terminations and local routing | 2.00 | Unplaced design allowance. A 1–3 mm² allowance moves the total below by ±1 mm². |
| **Optical circuitry including SOAs** | **7.78** | Includes the optical weight-write interface. |
| Four separate high-swing RF driver dies | 9.24 | Four × 2.31 mm². Analog Devices specifies a 1.71 × 1.35 mm die, with 1.05 W dissipation at its specified operating point. The dimension proxy does not establish compatibility with the earlier fJ/MAC budget. [HMC1051 datasheet](https://www.analog.com/en/products/hmc1051.html) |
| **Cell plus driver dies** | **17.02** | Excludes shared optical sources, clock and bias supplies, packaging, fiber attachment and cooling. |

The optical write interface receives a **prepared optical factor packet**. The writing switch and its allocated driver alone occupy 3.31 mm² per lane. Inference can use resident weights. Generating arbitrary factors from digital coefficients also requires a programmer, with its area counted once if it services all banks. Nine banks contain 4,752 distinct factor packets. A historical 100-channel optical arbitrary-waveform generator had a 30 × 35 mm mask layout. The required 255-channel packet rate, retention during loading and programmer area remain open. The baseline density is for resident-weight inference at optical data and weight ports. [Soares et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1109/JPHOT.2011.2170558)

**Time and operations.** Count 2 × 128² operations per completed real block MVM. Assume a 400 ps data-loop round trip, including 286 ps for the 2.12 cm nonlinear guide at group index 4.04 and a 114 ps design allowance for both switches, filters, gain and return routing. Only a post-layout delay measurement or simulation can validate the latter allowance. Sixty-six laps take 26.4 ns. With one packet circulating, a cell completes 37.88 million vectors/s, or 1.241 TOPS. This gives 0.0729 TOPS/mm² on 17.02 mm², or 0.160 TOPS/mm² on the 7.78 mm² photonic allocation. A 500 ps lap would give 0.0583 TOPS/mm² including driver dies. Additional common propagation between banks adds latency; writes and initialization are outside steady-state inference. [Group-index proxy from Chang et al., ring radius and FSR](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1909.09778#page=10)

**Independent inputs.** One possible schedule loads four independent data packets 100 ps apart in the same 400 ps loop. Supply four consecutive copies of factor 1, then four of factor 2, through factor 66. There are 264 weight packets per 26.4 ns frame. All four data packets require the same idler band on a given lap. Release the four answers and exchange them for the next group. This gives an average of 151.52 million completed vectors/s per bank, with burst arrivals at bank boundaries. It requires the repeated factor frame, synchronized routing, guard time for switching, and sufficient gain recovery across the group. The assumed 20 ps illuminated window still needs a compatible band plan. Averaging one completed MVM every 20 ps would ignore the 66 visits. Even at one *visit* every 20 ps, the arithmetic limit is one completion per 1.32 ns per cell, with 20 packets in flight, no guard time and no validated switching or recovery margin.

**Local MVM comparison with earlier implementations.** Count 32,768 real operations per completed 128 × 128 product. A single vector takes 26.4 ns through the 66 factors. With four independent vectors circulating, the cell completes four products in that same time, averaging 6.6 ns per result. Each intermediate Hankel multiply has only 255 independent coefficients and contributes one step to the programmed product.

| Schedule for one 128 × 128 cell | Completed local TOPS | TOPS/mm² on 7.78 mm² photonic area | TOPS/mm² on 17.02 mm² with drivers |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| One vector, one visit every 400 ps | 1.24 | 0.160 | 0.073 |
| Four vectors, one visit every 100 ps | 4.96 | 0.638 | 0.292 |

The earlier 64 × 64 recirculator at 100 ps visit spacing projected 2.41 TOPS. Dividing by its earlier partial 7.65 mm² component subtotal gives 0.315 TOPS/mm². The new cell doubles throughput at that occupancy; its larger area allowance includes weight replacement, the gated read tap, the storage amplifier and local routing. The separate 128 × 128 summed interleaver had a 5.84 ns frame pitch and 5.61 TOPS. Its full 128-wide area was not costed; the 31.98 mm² footprint belongs to its 64-wide version. For the local calculation run `python3 notes/hankel_product_area.py --local-only --packets 4` (use `--packets 1` for one circulating vector). [Earlier product throughput](#useful-throughput-at-100-ps) · [Earlier product area](#area-including-gain-and-switch-drivers) · [Interleaver size scaling](hankel_size_scaling.py)

**Physical banks.** With eight lanes per bank, allow 2 mm² per bank for local reference and prepared-weight distribution. Each boundary has an 8 × 8 cyclic AWGR in a 2 mm² site and eight 0.75 mm² post-router gain dies as used in the energy budget. Zhang et al. measured a 1.3 × 0.9 mm 200 GHz-spaced router. Operation across the proposed 128-tooth band needs characterization. The array allocation in mm² is `n × (8 × 17.02 + 2) + (n−1) × (2 + 8 × 0.75)` for n physically separate banks. The outer connection to a shared programmer and its area are extra. [Zhang et al., 8 × 8 AWGR](https://iris.polito.it/handle/11583/2972259)

| Dedicated 1,024-wide transform | Optical + SOA mm² | With driver dies, mm² | Local-block TOPS/mm², 1 packet | Dense-equivalent TOPS/mm², 1 packet | Dense-equivalent TOPS/mm², 4 packets |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 3 banks, 2 shuffles | 208.72 | 430.48 | 0.0692 | 0.1845 | 0.7381 |
| 9 banks, 8 shuffles | 642.16 | 1,307.44 | 0.0684 | 0.0608 | 0.2430 |
| 17 banks, 16 shuffles | 1,220.08 | 2,476.72 | 0.0682 | 0.0321 | 0.1283 |

All density columns use the area including the driver dies. Local operations per completed vector are `2 × n × 8 × 128²`. The dense-equivalent convention assigns `2 × 1024²` operations to the complete transform. The three-bank transform is structured, nine is locally fully controllable under ideal block assumptions, and the 17-bank construction can represent arbitrary real matrices in ideal arithmetic. The table assumes distinct banks pipeline different vectors after fill. For one strictly dependent stream with one vector anywhere in the stack, nine banks take at least 237.6 ns plus boundary transit per result. That is 8.83 dense-equivalent TOPS, or 0.00675 dense-equivalent TOPS/mm². For a shared programmer and support footprint S, the nine-bank, single-packet-per-lane steady-state density becomes `79.44/(1307.44 + S)` TOPS/mm². Accuracy and amplifier/RF dissipation at these cadences remain to be established. Run `python3 notes/hankel_product_area.py` and `python3 notes/hankel_product_area.py --packets 4`. [Footprint and throughput calculator](hankel_product_area.py)

##### Local 128 × 128 MVM against measured Intel 18A digital compute-in-memory

Agarwal et al. fabricated a 0.0856 mm² digital compute-in-memory macro with 128 inputs, 32 outputs, two resident weight sets and zero-point INT8 × INT8 arithmetic. At 25 °C, 0.4 V and 25% input switching activity, they measured 147 TOPS/W at 6.1 mW. At two operations per real MAC this is 13.61 fJ/MAC. The same operating point gives about 0.897 TOPS and 10.48 TOPS/mm², inferred from the rounded measured power and efficiency. At 1.1 V and 2.62 GHz they measured peak area efficiency of 250 TOPS/mm². This is a separate operating point. Four copies can cover the output columns of one 128 × 128 MVM with no cross-tile partial sums. Keeping the measured macro efficiency gives 223 pJ per vector and 0.3424 mm² of macro area before input broadcast, output wiring and system overhead. [Agarwal et al., ISSCC 2026, paper 30.9](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/11409207/)

The optical cell uses the 75 mW reference / 33.3 mW total weight setting, 66 visits, and one local result per 16,384 real MACs. It projects 1,329 pJ/vector, or 81.1 fJ/MAC, and 0.292 TOPS/mm² on the 17.02 mm² allocation with four independent vectors. At the silicon energy optimum the gaps are 5.96× in energy and 35.9× in density, with different precision and I/O boundaries. The loaded optical budget excludes encoding, unallocated control and RF bias. The depleted four-field model gave 42.75% calibrated deterministic field error on six held-out inputs from one factor program. The Intel macro executes quantized integer arithmetic. The optical single-vector latency is projected at 26.4 ns. The Intel high-voltage figures imply a new 128 × 32 result can be initiated about every 0.38 ns; inferring the latency of an individual result would require the pipeline depth and interface timing. [Optical propagation](hankel_product_forward.py) · [Electrical budget](hankel_product_energy.py) · [Area and throughput](hankel_product_area.py)

**Break-even for this design.** With weak conversion, the pump-power product is fixed. At reference/weight split r relative to 50/50 mW, the two pumps alone cost `(198r + 694.65/r)/0.95` pJ per completed vector. The minimum is 781 pJ, or 47.7 fJ/MAC, before paying for any data gain. This conditional bound uses the current tap, sources, guide and visit count. A full-system comparison at the same task accuracy would require electronics to incur over 1.1 nJ more per vector in omitted costs than optics to reverse the present energy ranking. A digital weight fetch from external memory can only be charged when the workload actually requires one.

At 75/33.3 mW, the light delivered for one vector already totals 223.55 pJ. This consists of 118.8 pJ for the reference, 44.0 pJ for weights, 51.7 pJ to restore data on 66 visits, and 9.05 pJ at extraction. With 100% electrical efficiency, fresh pump packets and ideal delivery, this is 13.64 fJ/local MAC. Reoptimizing the pump split for ideal delivery at fixed weak transfer gives 45.6/54.8 mW and 12.53 fJ/MAC, leaving less than 8% margin against Intel. The result depends on pump reuse, operating power and the number of visits. A single signed-real Hankel has 255 independent coefficients. Sixty-four such factors expose at most 16,320 real parameters for a general 16,384-parameter matrix, so at least 65 visits are necessary in this representation. When the task itself calls for a single Hankel transform, the same component budget gives 97.65 pJ per transform including extraction. An electronic convolution exploiting the same coefficient sharing is the relevant comparator. [Electrical model](hankel_product_energy.py)

Wave mixing can produce positive idler gain. Zhao et al. measured +34.5 dB on chip in an 11 mm AlGaAs waveguide using 1 ps pump pulses at 22.9 W peak with one seed wavelength at the gain peak. Ko et al. separately measured roughly five-bit optical MVM arithmetic with a PPLN waveguide that serialized the output rows. Neither experiment establishes electrical energy per completed, fully programmable 128 × 128 MVM at matched precision. [Zhao et al., Fig. 3](https://doi.org/10.1002/lpor.202503221) · [Ko et al.](https://doi.org/10.1364/OL.563017)

**Evidence from other optical architectures.** Lightmatter's four programmable 128 × 128 cores operated at 65.5 TOPS of adaptive block floating-point arithmetic while consuming 78 W electrical plus 1.6 W optical input. The electrical draw alone gives 2.38 pJ/MAC at that system boundary. Bernstein et al. projected about 10 fJ/MAC for a 1,000 × 1,000 single-shot optical layer including generation, spatial modulation, data conversion, and activation. Liang et al. demonstrated signed optical multiplication with nine inputs and nine outputs at 100 MS/s. Their FAST-ONN electrical budget ranges from 370 fJ/operation at small scale to a projected 2 fJ/operation (4 fJ/MAC) at 1,024 inputs by 1,024 outputs. Spatial fan-out lets one input drive many weights and each detector collect many products. Achieving that energy and INT8-equivalent accuracy on a fabricated dense MVM remains open in these examples. LightGen separately reported measured advantages on generative-vision tasks against GPU implementations using a trained diffractive system. Its task and equivalent-operation count differ from a programmable resident-weight INT8 MVM. [Lightmatter processor](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-08854-x) · [Lightmatter power figures](https://lightmatter.com/blog/a-new-kind-of-computer/) · [Bernstein et al.](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10284542/) · [Liang et al.](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41377-026-02206-8.pdf) · [LightGen](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.adv7434)

#### Energy bounds for two-beam multiplication with independently programmed rows

The target is one signed, dense 128 × 128 matrix–vector product with resident matrix coefficients. The energy allowance at 13.6 fJ per MAC is 222.8 pJ per vector, or 1.74 pJ per output. Intel measured its efficiency with INT8 operands. The approximate optical comparison uses RMS error relative to the RMS of the ideal signed outputs over isotropic inputs. A finite absolute noise floor precludes a uniform relative error guarantee near a null of the matrix. [Intel ISSCC macro](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/11409207/)

**Photon requirement.** Normalize a coherent field so its squared amplitude is its photon count and a vacuum quadrature has variance 1/4. With a strong local oscillator, ideal signed homodyne requires a mean of 1/(4r²) detected signal photons per row at fractional field RMS error r. At 10%, that is 25 photons per output or 0.0000704 fJ of detected 551 nm light per MAC. At 1/256 RMS the figure is 0.0461 fJ/MAC. This is a conditional readout floor. Pump light, the part of the input that misses the selected mode, practical detection and other system costs must be supplied separately. With L detected reference photons and m mean signal photons, the ideal finite-reference expression is 4r² = 1/m + 1/L. [Coherent optical computing and shot noise](https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevX.9.021032)

**Information entering in a fresh optical weight beam.** Suppose a previously unknown row w is supplied only as coherent amplitudes √P w, with P photons for a unit-norm row. Even a receiver given x exactly can rotate the modes so that one carries the unknown real displacement √(P/N) wᵀx for ‖x‖² = N. Its real-quadrature variance is 1/4, also the coherent-state local unbiased estimation limit with known phase. For a unit RMS target dot product, the weight packet needs P ≥ N/(4r²) to resolve that projection. Thus the weight light alone costs at least hν_w/(4r²) per MAC under this encoding. At 860 nm that is 0.00577 fJ at 10%, 0.577 fJ at 1% and 3.78 fJ at 1/256 RMS. At 25% delivery the last value becomes 15.14 electrical fJ/MAC before mixing. This lower bound applies when the light contains the only copy of the unknown weights available to the computation. A reusable material state or amortized resident optical control changes that boundary. The weight and readout bounds constrain different resources and need not be summed to estimate the error.

**A waveguide that reads one matrix row.** In mode-selective sum-frequency generation, the field of the shaped pump selects the signed superposition of input frequencies converted to an output mode. Other input modes ideally continue at the input wavelength. De et al. used up to 64 input bins in a 35 mm PPLN waveguide and read programmed projections sequentially by photon counting. The supplement reports 125 fJ per shaped 860 nm pump pulse and about 0.1% conversion, with 1540 nm inputs and a filtered 551 nm output. Use those aggregate values as a matched-mode energy anchor. Their experiment used weak input states and did not measure a complete, bright, signed MVM. Folge et al. estimate about 200 inputs by one output or 14 by 14 for their example 7 cm multioutput waveguide with 4 THz of pump bandwidth. The independent row schedules below avoid assuming 16,384 independently addressable pump frequencies in one guide. [De et al., experiment and transfer equations](https://journals.aps.org/prresearch/supplemental/10.1103/PhysRevResearch.6.L022040/Frequency_bin_unitaries_supplement.pdf) · [Folge et al., Appendix B](https://journals.aps.org/prxquantum/pdf/10.1103/PRXQuantum.5.040329#page=10)

**Engineering the 64-bin projector.** Choose the waveguide cross-section and polarizations for matched pump and input group velocities and a different output group velocity. Choose the poling period and operating temperature for the central frequency triplet. The poled length sets the narrow output acceptance, while the complex pump spectrum supplies the row. A uniform guide has approximate intensity FWHM 0.886/τ, where τ = L |n_g,out − n_g,pump|/c is the differential group delay. Babel et al. measured a 583 GHz output acceptance in 3 mm of thin-film lithium niobate. Holding dispersion fixed, 25 GHz FWHM would imply roughly 70 mm and 35 ps of walk-off. This illustrates the length and timing costs on a 50 GHz data grid. Use the finite-width tooth envelopes and 3.15 THz input span to test the actual complex transfer, pulse overlap, and residual data loss. [Babel et al., Figs. 2–4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.09346v1)

Babel et al. report 96.8% average selectivity among five low-order Hermite–Gaussian modes at low pump power after spectral filtering. A separate measurement on one matched mode gave 89.6% internal photon conversion from input depletion at 20 mW average pump power. The existing 64-bin budget uses 0.057% matched-mode conversion for its optimized 71.2 fJ pump. For an 8% stochastic readout allocation within a 10% total vector RMS goal, up to 6% remains for coherent row error if other contributions are negligible. For isotropic data, that requires about 99.64% squared overlap of the measured complex row with the intended row after scalar calibration. Leakage to uncollected modes additionally affects subsequent reads. [Babel et al., Figs. 4–5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.09346v1) · [Energy model](two_beam_mvm.py)

Split the coordinates into two disjoint blocks of 64. Each row uses two programmed pump pulses, each projecting its block directly onto one output field. A fixed coherent 50:50 coupler sums the partial fields, introducing a factor of two in the required input photon count. All rows have independent weights. A data copy can serve G successive rows before disposal. The 128/G copies can run in parallel or be supplied over time. One circulates or forwards the residual input-band data and removes each row's completed output. There is no inter-row amplifier or sum over arrival times. The budget covers all copies and charges the full allocated pump energy for both halves of every row even when smaller weights can use less.

**Compensating for an earlier row.** Within each block let A be the known residual input transfer before a row, w the desired row as a column, and t the desired field scale. Precompute v = t A⁻ᵀw. Program the selected pump direction u = v/‖v‖ and photon conversion q = ‖v‖². Its emitted field is then vᵀAx = t wᵀx. The residual transfer is updated by multiplying A on the left by I − (1 − √(1−q))uuᵀ and by √T for the next transit, where T is the passive power transmission. The zero row needs no conversion. For full row norms at most one, a sufficient common scale is t² = q_max T^(G−1)/[1+(G−1)q_max]. This follows by bounding the power removed by each row in AᵀA; it covers identical and nonorthogonal rows. For arbitrary W normalize once by the largest row norm. The numerical energy examples use unit row norms and isotropic inputs. For highly unequal rows the signal-to-noise calculation at fixed common scale must also account for N maxᵢ‖wᵢ‖²/‖W‖²_F.

For time-varying rows, an approximation also allows each pump to use the row directly. Scale each read for the known passive transmission. Any preceding normalized extraction changes the unconverted field by at most 1 − √(1−q_max) in operator norm. Telescoping the product of the contractions bounds the row-direction error by (G−1)[1 − √(1−q_max)]. This is 0.43% for G = 16 at the 71.2 fJ operating point. This bound concerns the ideal converter and excludes pump programming costs.

**Electrical source budget.** Assume 40% net electricity-to-delivered shaped data light, 25% for shaped weight light, 80% output collection including detection, 25% electricity-to-detected reference light, and 0.2 dB passive loss between row reads. These are targets. At the quoted pump energy the 0.1% conversion applies to a perfectly matched input mode. For another pump energy E assume q_max = sin²[arcsin(√0.001) √(E / 125 fJ)]. For B equal-sized coordinate blocks and G rows per input copy, the source energy per MAC is

\[
e = \frac{BE}{N\eta_w}
  + \frac{B h\nu_s\, m}{G t^2\eta_x\eta_{\rm det}}
  + \frac{h\nu_o\, L}{N\eta_{\rm LO}}.
\]

The data term includes splitting, the rejected projection, and the coherent output combiner. The weight term charges fresh pump light per input vector, including upstream optical delivery losses through the assumed net efficiency. Programming electronics, additional routing and control, and receiver electronics are separate. The calculator minimizes over the coherent reference level and the maximum pump pulse energy. At weak conversion the signal cost varies approximately as 1/E and the weight cost as E. Equal spending on the two is close to optimal. The minimum is for this budget and topology. [Budget and propagation check](two_beam_mvm.py)

| Two 64-bin blocks, 10% readout RMS | At the published pump energy, fJ/MAC | Optimized source subtotal, fJ/MAC |
|---|---:|---:|
| Fresh copy per row | 28.12 | 25.22 |
| 8 rows per copy | 11.38 | 10.53 |
| 16 rows per copy | 10.42 | 8.97 |
| 128 rows per copy | 69.61 | 48.43 |

Sixteen rows per copy give the lowest subtotal among equal groups that divide 128 at 0.2 dB per transit. At 10% RMS, the 71.2 fJ pump allocation per half-row gives 4.45 fJ/MAC for the weights, 4.49 for the data, and 0.036 for the coherent reference. At 8%, 5% and 1% readout RMS, the reoptimized totals are 11.24, 18.08 and 94.81 fJ/MAC. At zero passive loss, 128 rows per copy give an idealized optimized 2.27 fJ/MAC at 10% RMS. At 9.5% shaped-weight supply efficiency, reoptimizing the 16-row case instead gives 14.50 fJ/MAC at 10% RMS. All readout errors refer to the terminal quadrature measurement. A further optical layer requires its own input band, brightness, and cumulative noise budget.

**Checks and remaining margin.** The implementation verifies the programmed rows and integrates the classical trilinear equations with back-action on both beams for an ideal guide with one accepted output mode and no other nonlinear channels. For 12 random inputs and one row-aligned input on a normalized dense matrix and a repeated-row matrix, deterministic RMS error was 0.34–0.41% at the 10% optimized source setting. It checks photon conservation and step-size convergence. This tests the mathematical construction and bright-beam loading in that ideal guide. A measured complex mode-transfer function, loss, and leakage for bright arbitrary 64-bin inputs are still needed. In a receiver sensitivity case, two 10 fF nodes at 300 K add reset charge variance 2kTC. Reoptimizing the reference and pump and allowing two 0.8 V full-swing capacitor resets gives 9.80 fJ/MAC including the sources. Noise and dissipation of a complete bandwidth-matched receiver, matrix programmer, and timing circuit remain unverified. At the base 8.97 fJ/MAC point the remaining allowance is 0.59 pJ per completed output. [Reset noise analysis](https://www-isl.stanford.edu/groups/elgamal/abbas_publications/J036.pdf)

**Scope across nonlinearities.** If every input–output pair has an independently programmable coherent frequency-conversion coupling, an ideal lossless converter with integrated coupling matrix UΣV† implements cross-band transfer −i U sin(Σ) V†. Programming the inverse sine of the desired singular values gives any norm-scaled dense matrix. This ideal addresses all N² controls; assuming them freely available would remove the main physical resource question. Bragg-scattering FWM demonstrated a three-way frequency beam splitter. Conventional small-signal Raman multiplication produces an intensity change proportional to the two intensities on top of a transmitted carrier, with added requirements for signed sums, shot noise on the carrier and independent row addressing. Ordinary SBS gain similarly requires a specified response bandwidth. Device efficiency depends on the interaction, bandwidth, accessible modes and energy used to replenish or dispatch the weights. No common numerical optimum follows from the name of the nonlinear process. [Multioutput frequency-conversion theory](https://doi.org/10.1103/PRXQuantum.5.040329) · [Bragg-scattering FWM experiment](https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevResearch.7.023108) · [Stimulated Raman multiplication of intensities](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3576036/)

##### Building an arbitrary dense MVM from two useful Hankel outputs

Use a 2-output, 64-input tile. Its first Hankel row uses pump indices 0 through 63 and its second uses 1 through 64. For two desired independent rows, form length-65 arrays A = (row 0, 0) and B = (0, row 1). Program the two successive pump pulses to (A−B)/2 and (A+B)/2. Between passes transmit the unconverted data and the two generated output fields, applying π phase only to the second output. The first output adds the two contributions to give row 0. The second subtracts the first contribution from the second to give row 1. The fields add during conversion in the second guide or second visit. Losing the first output field, or its phase relative to the second pump, breaks the construction. Two tiles on disjoint input coordinates feed a coherent 50:50 combiner for the two partials of each 128-term row. Sixty-four pairs supply 128 independently programmable rows. The control rank was checked for all entries of a real 128 × 128 matrix. [Implementation](two_output_mvm.py)

For each shared anti-diagonal, the two pump energies add to (|Aₛ|²+|Bₛ|²)/2 at the same weak coupling and final optical amplitude. Both amplitudes contribute to both outputs. For two single-row reads at successive positions the required pump energy is proportional to (1+T) T^(−r−1), where r is the first position (starting at 0) and T the per-visit power transmission. The two-coded-visit energy is proportional to T^(−r−1) for full rows of unit norm and equal data/output transmission. Thus the ratio is 1/(1+T), or 0.5115 at 0.2 dB. This assumes pulse energy drawn from the source scales with the sum of squared coefficients. A fixed source that discards the difference during shaping may deliver little or none of the electrical saving.

For comparison a separate one-pass design takes the even-index and odd-index data in separate 64-tooth guides, 100 GHz apart within each guide for a 50 GHz external grid. Choose two generated output frequencies separated by 50 GHz. Each half-row specifies a distinct pump tooth at output frequency minus data frequency. The two rows occupy alternating pump teeth and can be programmed independently in one pass. Each guide needs 128 programmable pump tones spanning 6.35 THz. A suitable joint phase-matching window or two engineered peaks should suppress undesired sums. The measured 64-bin single-output conversion slope does not establish either new mixer's slope. Multioutput SFG has demonstrated five simultaneous temporal projections. [Serino et al.](https://doi.org/10.1103/PRXQuantum.4.020306) · [Bandwidth and control count](https://journals.aps.org/prxquantum/pdf/10.1103/PRXQuantum.5.040329#page=10)

| Architecture | fJ/MAC, 16 rows per copy | Lowest source subtotal over power-of-two group sizes | Rows per copy at that minimum |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Independent one-row projections | 5.39 | 4.74 | 32 |
| Coded pair, two visits | 3.86 | 3.40 | 32 |
| Direct independent pair, one visit | 4.87 | 3.36 | 64 |

These are weak-conversion electrical source budgets at 10% terminal readout RMS. Each design pays for data, both programmed weight distributions and the optical local oscillator. They assume 0.2 dB per transit, 40% data-source and 25% shaped-weight efficiencies, and the single-row weak conversion slope of 0.008 per pJ for each participating spectral pair. Each row's pump is scaled for its position. The previous 8.97 fJ/MAC single-row budget charged every tile its full pump allowance based on the weakest position. The lowest subtotals use different group lengths and have no corresponding full propagation or timing validation. At 16 rows per copy the coded version beats its matched single-row comparison only if its useful slope exceeds 51.2% of the single-row slope with the assumed losses.

At 3.86 fJ/MAC, an ideal classical three-wave simulation gave 0.65% deterministic vector RMS on 12 random inputs to one dense 16 × 128 group, and 8.06% with uniform positive inputs and uniform positive rows. It includes pump depletion, generation of unwanted sidebands, back-conversion and continued propagation of the original data through 16 visits. These errors exclude readout noise. The simulation assumes perfectly periodic combs, ideal selection and phase shifting of two idler teeth, flat phase matching and preserved temporal overlap. For a 50 GHz spacing a simple delay producing the π difference is 10 ps, appreciable for 20 ps packets. A two-channel phase section with equal group delays is another implementation. A finite-packet comparison requires its measured or modeled complex transmission, through-loss, and optical-field overlap in the second section. [Propagation check](paired_hankel_stress.py) · [Matched source budgets](two_output_mvm.py)

#### Footprint of a 64-channel optical multiplier

Use one independently pumped 2.12 cm AlGaAs-on-insulator FWM section per Hankel operation. Lei et al. report about 0.60 × 0.30 mm for their roughly 2 cm converter. The table scales this 0.18 mm² device footprint to one active 64-coordinate block. The model assumes those sections can be tiled and their filters and pump ports can be routed separately. [Lei et al., Fig. 1 and device description](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447)

| Forward optical layout | FWM sections fabricated | FWM operations on one signal path | Area of FWM spirals | Illustrative area of listed passive and nonlinear components |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| Existing recirculating Hankel product | 1 | 34 visits | 0.18 mm² | 0.18 mm² plus the return path. An optional 1.163 ns extra delay gives 1.08 mm² for spiral and delay together. |
| 32 parallel branches, one weight comb setting one Hankel per branch | 32 | 1 | 5.76 mm² | 8.00 mm² with a splitter and combiner |
| 32 parallel branches, two independently weighted Hankels per branch | 64 | 2 | 11.52 mm² | 13.76 mm² with a splitter and combiner |

Each tree has 31 binary junctions and depth five. As a routing-area reference, Qian et al. measured a 0.70 × 1.60 mm silicon 1 × 32 splitter at 50 µm output pitch. Using that area for two reciprocal networks adds 2.24 mm². Their measured total splitter loss was 9.15 dB, so the area example gives no assurance of low loss. Poulton et al. measured less than 0.1 dB per larger telecom SiN splitter junction. A layout for the actual lane pitch and paths with matched optical phase and delay is still needed. The loop example uses Hashemi et al.'s 0.30 × 3.0 mm silicon delay spiral. The required return delay depends on scheduling and includes any propagation already supplied by other components on the loop. The 150 m TTT weight fiber is a separate off-chip memory. [Qian et al., 1 × 32 splitter](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.16.004981) · [Poulton et al., splitter measurements](https://pmg.mit.edu/wp-content/uploads/2016/12/ol-42-1-21.pdf) · [Hashemi et al., measured delay footprint](https://doi.org/10.1109/SiPhotonics68911.2026.11520725)

**Operator counted.** Adding 32 single-Hankel outputs on the same tooth grid gives another Hankel, since the sum still has one coefficient per anti-diagonal. The archived arbitrary-matrix construction uses 32 terms of the form H₂ᵦ H₁ᵦ, each with 64 selected intermediate teeth and independently programmable signed or complex factors. For conjugating FWM, program the conjugate of the algebraic first factor. The proof in `hankel_monarch/limitations.md`, §7 of the source bundle, establishes ideal expressivity.
