Optical coefficient range, conditioning and online weight updates remain separate questions. If each branch instead applies an independent per-tooth weight mask in addition to one Hankel, add 32 mask footprints to the single-stage row and evaluate that operator separately. [Archived derivation and numerical checks](../archive/README.md#original-notes-code-and-results)

**Floorplanning allowance.** Reserving 0.70 × 0.40 mm per nonlinear section gives a 5.6 × 3.2 mm array of 64 sites before the trees. With the two data trees this is about 20.2 mm² for the paired case, or 11.2 mm² for the one-stage case. The extra 100 µm in each site dimension is an assumed routing allowance. The loop time-shares 34 weight patterns. The paired array delivers 32 patterns to each of two stages. At the assumed 50 + 50 mW per nonlinear section one fully energized 32-guide stage requires 3.2 W of in-guide reference plus weight power.

For identical ideal balanced input and output trees, each branch receives x/√32 and the selected output is (Σᵦ Mᵦ x)/32, where Mᵦ is the branch transfer including its calibrated optical phase. The fraction collected depends on the branch fields. Each tree's ideal per-arm division and its excess loss are distinct. The counts above cover a forward multiplier. At eight simultaneous blocks, the bare nonlinear areas are 1.44 mm² for the loop, 46.08 mm² for one-stage branches and 92.16 mm² for paired branches.

##### Area including gain and switch drivers

Compare one forward 64 × 64 lane with clocked optical weights. The loop uses one 2 × 2 MZI to load, hold and release the data. A second MZI after a wavelength demultiplexer selects the generated idler and dumps the surviving input as bands A and B exchange roles each lap. Both switches and the amplifier are reused for all 34 visits. In the spatial design, 31 passive 1 × 2 splitters distribute the data and 31 passive 2 × 2 couplers sum the fields into the selected output. Each optical path crosses five of each. The two 1.12 mm² tree allowances already include these 62 junctions and their internal routing. The output tree uses the measured input-tree layout as an area proxy. [Qian et al., Fig. 6](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.16.004981)

| Fast optical MZIs, 1.00 mm² allocated each | 2.00 mm² | — |

Ekici et al. used 4 mm electrodes and about 6 V half-wave drive in a thin-film lithium-niobate storage-loop switch. The 1 mm² allowance assumes a 0.25 mm wide strip for each switch. The 8 V peak-to-peak, 30 GHz HMC1051 driver die is 1.71 × 1.35 mm. This legacy part sets an area scale. A sequencer can serve synchronized lanes, with its area apportioned over those lanes. Each independently driven switch added or removed changes the estimate by 3.31 mm². An independently gated weight-memory tap brings the SOA loop subtotal to 10.96 mm². The table assumes weights arrive in timed slots and that the routed return supplies the necessary delay. With one equally sized amplifier on each side, the spatial subtotal exceeds the loop subtotal by 13.26 mm² per lane. [Ekici et al., Fig. 1 and Methods](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41534-024-00929-3) · [Analog Devices driver dimensions](https://www.analog.com/en/products/hmc1051.html)

The SemiNex CHP-305 is a 1.50 × 0.50 mm bare 1550 nm SOA die with a vendor-listed 350 mW output and 6 dB noise figure. Liu et al. give 1.2 × 3.6 mm for a 0.50 m erbium-doped spiral and measured 145 mW output on their separate 0.21 m device. These are component sizes. Both amplifier options still need loaded gain and field-error measurements across the two data bands at the modeled 20 ps packet energy. At assumed group index 2 the 0.50 m spiral adds 3.3 ns flight per visit, which already exceeds the short-loop timing example. Optical pump sources, SOA bias, heat removal, RF packaging and the shared clock have separate footprints. For example, a discrete LT3081 current source uses a 4 × 4 mm package before passives and thermal copper. Both SOA cases need bias. [SemiNex preliminary data, CHP-305 column](https://mrp.seminex.com/PI_Sheet.aspx?seminex_part_pk=2229) · [Liu et al., Figs. 1 and 3](https://arxiv.org/html/2204.02202) · [Analog Devices current-source package](https://www.analog.com/en/products/lt3081.html)

**Local couplers.** If each pair needs a local 1 × 2 splitter and 2 × 2 coupler in addition to the two data trees, count 32 more of each. A 28.7 × 7 µm splitter body and a 3.5 × 47.25 µm coupler give 0.012 mm² for all 64 bodies. Allocating 0.10 × 0.05 mm per junction for local access would give 0.32 mm² before long routing. These examples apply to broadband power division. Wavelength-selective pump injection needs its own layout. [Measured splitter geometry](https://pmg.mit.edu/wp-content/uploads/2016/12/ol-42-1-21.pdf) · [Measured 2 × 2 coupler geometry](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.optlastec.2023.109493)

**Spatial phase control.** There are 31 independent relative branch phases per wavelength. Static offsets common to all teeth can be compensated in the programmed optical weights. For slowly drifting offsets, 31 thermal shifters at 0.11 × 0.11 mm each would add 0.38 mm² of photonics before monitors, electronics and wiring. Calibrate on a known pilot to preserve signed sums. Differential delay requires calibration across the comb. As a larger example, De Marinis et al. built a 10 × 5 mm 32-input combiner with 31 tunable MZIs, 62 thermal shifters and 31 photodiodes. Using that chip in place of the 1.12 mm² output tree gives 69.79 mm² for the spatial SOA case before external electronics. Their turbulence receiver also adjusts amplitudes. [Measured thermal phase shifter](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.545741) · [De Marinis et al., Secs. II and V](https://doi.org/10.1109/ACCESS.2025.3541870)

With both factors transmitting the balanced power fraction 0.236 and all 32 branch outputs identical and aligned, the spatial pair transmits 0.236², requiring +12.5 dB shared gain before excess losses. Other matrix decompositions give different coherent output power. Omitting the output amplifier gives 20.16 mm² at the raw output level. Restoring the data between factors instead requires 32 interstage gain sites, adding 24 mm² if all use the same 0.75 mm² die. Lower-power branch amplifiers could be smaller. The spatial design still has 32 intermediate band-selection paths and 64 pump/weight insertion sites, compared with one reused interaction site and selectable cleanup in the loop. If each parallel stage uses its own 1 × 32 reference-pump tree, reserve another 2.24 mm² at the same pitch. Weight memory, update hardware, inter-die coupling, routing beyond the allowances and package drawings are outside these forward-lane subtotals. Completed vectors per second and output error are separate comparison metrics.

#### Connecting two loop banks through the Monarch shuffle

Run eight 64-tooth data loops in parallel. Each lane applies its next Hankel weight pattern, selects the newly generated idler band and amplifies it on every visit. After visit 34 the packet is back on band A with its original complex-field convention. Switch that packet to the router. Only data crosses. For zero-based lane j and tooth k, the router sends `(j,k) → ((j+k) mod 8, k)`. Each output lane thus receives eight teeth from each input lane. A second independently programmed 34-pass bank completes `B₂ P B₁` in 68 FWM visits per packet and one shuffle. The 512 × 512 transform has rank at most eight in each 64 × 64 input/output lane-pair block. [Archived shuffle and figure](../archive/README.md#what-carries-forward)

For the two-way 10 GHz grid, an eight-port cyclic AWGR needs 10 GHz channel spacing and an 80 GHz routing period across all 64 band-A carriers (194.50–195.13 THz). Keep the lanes synchronized and include the router’s complex transmission, leakage and group delay across each tooth’s modulation bandwidth. Fan et al. measured an 8 × 8 router on a 100 GHz grid. Fontaine et al. measured a 64-channel waveform shaper at 10 GHz spacing. The 3 dB shuffle loss below is a design assumption at the proposed spacing. [Fan et al., 8 × 8 router](https://opg.optica.org/jlt/abstract.cfm?uri=jlt-43-11-5366) · [Fontaine et al., 10 GHz waveform shaper](https://doi.org/10.1364/OL.33.001714)

For the balanced test above, each bank adds `m = 245` independent noise photons per matched mode at unity net signal gain. An ideal shuffle permutes both signal and noise. At `S = 15,600` input signal photons per mode, the added RMS field error is **12.5% at the shuffle input** and **17.7% after both banks**. For uniform router power transmission T and no gain correction at the boundary, the final signal and added noise are `TS` and `Tm + m`. At 3 dB insertion loss this gives **21.7%** error relative to the attenuated ideal output. Adding 3 dB of gain immediately after that router restores the signal and gives **17.8%** if the extra amplifier also has a 5 dB noise figure. These estimates assume balanced factors and independent noise on each pass. Learned factors require propagating noise through their individual transfer matrices.

##### Writing an association across a Monarch stage

For one real-valued head, call the two block-diagonal banks A and B and the fixed lane permutation P. The raw query read is B P A q. In the archived 512-wide head each bank has eight 64-tooth lanes. At unchanged tooth t, destination lane s gets source lane (s−t) mod 8. Each destination therefore gets eight teeth from each of the eight first-bank outputs. Values from the model's value projection are packed in final output order.

**Forward-only design with one writable bank.** Train A offline and keep it fixed during inference. For a write (k,v), send the key through A and the shuffle, obtaining z=P A k. Output lane s now has a 64-tooth local key zₛ. Give it the 64 coordinates vₛ of the target that belong on its output. For known-slot replacement, supply one slot address a for the association to every output lane. Each lane uses its own pair (zₛ,vₛ) to program its own factor packet a via the existing convolution write with slot-parity tooth ordering. All eight packets can be replaced in parallel. A revisit to the same key with a new value requires the same slot address and recomputed or retained z. This rule can replace the packets without reading their old predictions. Slot allocation and finding a repeated key's address are external assumptions. The AWGR routes the intermediate optical data. Weights stay in local memory, and there is no need to send the residual backwards for this policy.

For a general query q, send x=P A q through the current output bank and concatenate the eight output packets in lane order. At the reversal initialization B=I. The known-slot retrieval experiment extracts (Bx−x)/α with α=0.05, as in the single-lane tests. This needs a coherent, time-aligned reference copy of the *post-shuffle* x and gain calibration. The raw stage output Bx includes the baseline x. With a next-free-slot residual write, first predict using the old B and use the local prediction error plus the saved input to its new factor. The residual target must use the same output convention as the read. Appending a correction does not require the previous slot address, occupies another factor and leaves the old factor in the product. [Local Hankel write rules](#replacing-an-association-in-the-original-product)

**Which associations can interfere?** For an ideal directly writable dense Bₛ, let eₛ=vₛ−Bₛzₛ for a raw-output read. The update ΔBₛ=eₛzₛᵀ/‖zₛ‖² fits the current target exactly when zₛ is nonzero. On another query with shuffled local slice xₛ, the response changes by eₛ(zₛᵀxₛ)/‖zₛ‖². Even if A is orthogonal and kᵀq=0, the overlaps in individual lanes can be nonzero and sum to zero. Their errors occur on different output coordinates. As an example, two lane keys (u,u) and (u,−u) are globally orthogonal; a dense local write at the first key changes the second key by (e₁,−e₂). Hankel-factor replacement adds its own within-lane interference. This ideal dense rule is a control, not a physical factor-write procedure.

**End-to-end check.** The simulation used random orthonormal 512-coordinate keys, independent unit values, a fixed real orthogonal circulant A on each input lane (each is realizable with two Hankels), the specified cyclic tooth routing, and a known factor address shared across lanes. The writer scaled the local key–value correlation by the fixed lane count to account for the mean local key energy. It does not divide by measured key energy. Each of 256 streams per case replaced one random live association. All current values and the superseded value were candidates. The single-lane scorer uses cosine similarity to that lane's slices of the same candidates. Both layouts used identical global keys and values. [Reproduce the two-bank test](monarch_association_write.py)

| Layout and live associations | New value top-1 across 512 coordinates | Other keys top-1 after write | New value top-1 in an average lane | Cosine with new 512-vector | Other/target RMS field change per key |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 8 × 64, 16 keys | 100.0% | 100.0% | 68.6% | 0.291 | 62.9% |
| 4 × 128, 16 keys | 100.0% | 100.0% | 91.9% | 0.293 | 63.4% |
| 8 × 64, 32 keys | 100.0% | 99.4% | 36.6% | 0.209 | 63.2% |
| 4 × 128, 32 keys | 99.6% | 99.2% | 62.1% | 0.213 | 63.4% |

These are ideal field results. All lanes carry evidence about the same candidate. The global nearest-value decoder adds their inner products in software. A subsequent optical layer receives the actual output field. The experiment does not isolate a benefit from P itself, learn addresses, or test repeated overwrites over long sequences.

**If both banks are adaptive.** For the raw operator, an end-to-end squared-error update first computes z=P A k and e=v−Bz using the old weights. Bank B needs z and e. To update A, propagate e through the old B adjoint and then through the inverse shuffle to obtain PᵀBᵀe in first-bank lane order. Its local partners are the original k slices. These are the bank-level operands. Updating the individual factors of a repeated Hankel bank by gradient additionally needs their intermediate activations and local adjoints. Simply writing the same (k,v) into both banks would move the internal keys seen by B. A forward-only two-writable-bank policy would need an explicit target for that internal representation or some other learning rule, followed by an end-to-end retention test. [Factor adjoints](#backpropagation-and-the-ttt-weight-update) · [Monarch factorization](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v162/dao22a.html)

##### Global writes with eight 128-tooth lanes and n shuffles

Here n counts physical shuffle boundaries, so a forward traversal encounters n+1 banks. A token supplies two signed-real 1,024-vectors k and v. A useful test gives different input lanes equal and opposite local similarities. If the full key–query inner product is zero, a global outer-product contribution must vanish on every output. Concatenated nearest-value decisions do not test this property. [Reproducible global routing and write tests](global_monarch_writes.py)

**A forward-only product of global corrections.** Initialize the full map W to the identity. To store (k,v), read p=Wk and form e=v−p. Append one record, with p/‖p‖² as its score weights and e as its output weights. A query enters as z=q and visits the live records oldest first. On a record, compute one scalar across all lanes and return z + e(pᵀz)/‖p‖². Thus W′=(I+e pᵀ/‖p‖²)W and W′k=v whenever p is nonzero. Another key q changes by e(pᵀWq)/‖p‖². If W is initially orthogonal this agrees with a normalized dense delta write on that step. Later interference depends on similarity in output space. All writes use the next chronological packet positions. A global residual comb, optical normalization or calibrated encoding of its scalar divisor, and predictable scaling of the coherent addition are implementation requirements.

In 64 ideal trials per load, unit orthogonal random keys received independent unit random values. The table pools the field errors across all live keys after the final overwrite. Other/target compares the displacement on an average untouched key to the intended change during overwrites. Each individual write fit its own key within 10⁻¹⁴. The intervening updates account for the final bank error.

| Unique keys | Subsequent random overwrites | Records needed | Final RMS value error | Other/target RMS displacement |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 16 | 1 | 17 | 12.9% | 4.3% |
| 32 | 1 | 33 | 17.8% | 4.4% |
| 32 | 32 | 64 | 29.9% | 5.5% |
| 64 | 1 | 65 | 25.2% | 4.6% |

**Unique-key writes compared with ordinary linear attention.** Each key is written once. At each load, query every key written so far and pool the relative RMS error of the retrieved value vectors. The 64 ideal 1,024-dimensional trials use identical signed unit keys and independent unit values for all three methods. The additive baseline is the unnormalized linear-attention numerator S←S+vkᵀ, starting from zero. Dense delta also serves as a control from the same identity initialization as the optical product. [Reproduce with --inserts-only](global_monarch_writes.py) · [Additive and delta fast weights](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html)

| Unique keys | Orthogonal keys: product / additive | Independent random keys: product / additive | Independent random keys: dense delta from I |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 8 | 8.31% / 0% | 11.73% / 8.16% | 8.16% |
| 16 | 12.13% / 0% | 17.07% / 12.15% | 12.13% |
| 32 | 17.41% / 0% | 24.40% / 17.42% | 17.33% |
| 64 | 24.69% / 0% | 34.78% / 24.82% | 24.57% |

For m distinct orthogonal unit keys and isotropic independent unit values in d dimensions, the expected mean squared value error of the global product is (m−1)/d. Each previously unseen key kⱼ still reads as itself before its write, since earlier key directions are orthogonal to it. On a previously stored output z, the new factor replaces its component along kⱼ by vⱼ(kⱼᵀz). This preserves squared norm in expectation over vⱼ, but removes the mean projection of z along kⱼ. Averaging over the isotropic value for association i gives expected squared error 2(m−i)/d after all m writes (i starts at 1). At d=1,024 the pooled RMS benchmark crosses 10% at 12 unique keys, before optical noise. Both additive attention and dense delta retain all orthogonal pairs exactly. For independent isotropic keys the additive baseline itself has expected mean squared interference (m−1)/d. These results concern analog value retrieval and use the signed key vectors as features.

Each record adds at most one to the rank of W−I. A full-rank forward map can consequently have a much smaller writable memory. Small ‖p‖ requires large score weights. If W maps two distinct keys to the same field, subsequent left multiplications cannot distinguish them. For example, starting from I, writing basis vector e₁ to value e₂ merges it with the existing image of e₂. The test retains all records. Evicting an old multiplicative factor changes the inputs to the remaining factors. Token cost grows with the number of live records.

**Shuffle routing for any stage count.** Each input lane i uses a Hankel convolution with its 128 score weights to emit the local scalar sᵢ. Select a fixed idler tooth for the dot. For three shuffles, exchange copies at lane offsets 1, 2 and 4 in order. At a stride b each bank emits its scalar on carrier residues 0 and b. The cyclic AWGR sends these to lanes i and i+b (modulo eight). Each destination coherently combines its two received fields. Consequently, successive stages cover two, four and eight original partial dots. Every output lane receives the same global score. Its final Hankel uses that scalar on one input tooth and the appropriate output-weight comb to emit all 128 local components of the correction.

More generally choose radices r₁,…,rₙ with product equal to the lane count. At stage j copy to offsets 0,s,…,(rⱼ−1)s, with s the product of the preceding radices, then sum the received fields. For eight lanes, examples are (8), (2,4) and (2,2,2). Unit-radix stages can pass an already combined score. Unnormalized arithmetic gives a sum. Ideal power-normalized fanout and collection implement an average, for a total 1/8 field factor relative to the unnormalized sum. Actual two-frequency fanout requires frequency conversion or appropriately encoded mixing: a passive spatial splitter preserves the carrier. Selective idler filtering, phase matching, matched optical delays, gain and transfer for multiple carrier copies need a power and noise budget. [Cyclic shuffle geometry](#connecting-two-loop-banks-through-the-monarch-shuffle)

The global product requires a matched bypass for all eight lanes to retain z, coherent addition of the computed correction, and a return from the final bank to the first for the next record. The guide performing a local dot typically produces unwanted idlers; the full input field used by the bypass also has a power cost. The previous layout completes its local Hankel product inside each bank before crossing the shuffle. Global recirculation changes that topology. Three shuffles give four physical banks per global record. In the explicit signed-real frequency layout the first bank computes its dot on tooth zero, then fans that scalar onto residues 0 and 1. The two intermediate banks use fixed Hankels and fixed output masks, successively mapping teeth {0,1} to {0,2} and {0,2} to {0,4}. On each retained input/output pair the intermediate coefficient is 1/2. The last bank collects its two teeth to tooth zero and uses the stored output comb to synthesize all 128 values. This takes two Hankel visits at each endpoint and one in each middle bank, totaling six per record (n+3 for the corresponding n-shuffle construction). The script verifies the selected rows and columns with literal spectral masks, including their 1/8 total field normalization. The fixed masks and bypass differ from the A/B selector in the existing single-cell schematic. Each write first reads Wk. Reading a separate query adds another traversal of the stored history. No wall-plug efficiency or noisy network performance is claimed here.

**Exact linear-attention alternative.** Use the same global score circuit with k as analyzer weights and v as synthesis weights, and hold the original q across records. Accumulate v(kᵀq) in a separate output register. For normalized dense delta, append (k,v−Wk) for a unit key (scale by 1/‖k‖² for normalized delta with nonunit keys). The model accumulates many rank-one updates without constructing their million individual coefficients. The script matched direct dense delta to within 7 × 10⁻¹⁶ on independent queries with 20 retained records including a repeated key, under one, two, three and four shuffle boundaries. It also verifies a single record using literal Hankels and the two-shuffle collect-at-one-lane then distribute geometry. These checks grant ideal query replay and coherent accumulation. A finite FIFO of raw keys and values realizes windowed additive attention; evicting a stored prediction-error correction generally changes the dense delta recurrence.

**Updating the original local Hankel products in place.** This alternative keeps the current loop wiring. Read k through the old n+1 banks and form the global descent error v−Wk. For each upstream bank, apply the downstream bank’s old adjoint and inverse shuffle to the error. At each selected Hankel factor, convolve its own saved key-side input with its own backward error and add the scaled increment to its 255-tooth weight packet. One clock-selected factor per lane per bank avoids a key-to-slot address table and keeps a fixed read depth. At three shuffles and 66 factors per bank, scheduled single-factor writes left 51.1% of the initial target residual, with orthogonal probes moving by 62.7% of the on-key change. Updating all factors left 49.5% and gave 60.7% collateral. These are single writes from the same untuned orthogonal program, 32 held-out keys, eight orthogonal probes per key, common learning rate chosen on 12 calibration examples. The forward and reverse schedule generalizes directly to n. [Reproduce with --shuffles 1 3 --depth 66](monarch_global_gradient.py) · [Original adjoint path](#backpropagation-and-the-ttt-weight-update)

**Packed global scores at fixed depth.** Another option uses 120 intermediate teeth out of 128 and one fixed Hankel in every middle lane with h indexed by s equal to 1/8 on multiples of 15 and zero elsewhere. Apply contiguous band-edge masks at its input and output. Each residue class modulo 15 on 120 teeth contains exactly one tooth from each AWGR routing residue modulo eight. This Hankel coherently sums one partial from every source lane and sends a copy to every destination. Across eight middle lanes it transports 120 distinct global scores. All 128 original input coordinates per lane can participate through the first bank; all 128 output teeth per lane are available in the last bank.

There are two different programming assumptions for this packed circuit. Ideal independently writable endpoint rows and columns reproduce the sum of 120 arbitrary full-width key–value records (calibrated error below 10⁻¹⁵ on independent queries). Efficient arbitrary row or column replacement inside an endpoint Hankel product remains unresolved. Alternatively keep the first two banks fixed, and give all output lanes the same learned or fixed 120-dimensional global key, in lane-specific tooth order. Then the existing next-factor Hankel writer can use that key and the local value slice. In the fixed-orthogonal-encoder experiment at 32 associations plus one repetition, it selected the new 1,024-vector on 99.2% of trials, but its mean target cosine was only 0.330. The ideal dense projected control had cosine 1 on the just-written key. The fixed projection loses at least 904 global key directions. For isotropic inputs the ideal normalized middle retains 120/1,024 of the entering total energy on average (−9.31 dB), before physical FWM losses. The simulation uses a fixed amplitude calibration to compensate. This variant addresses global features with a key-space bottleneck.

#### Backpropagation and the TTT weight update

Use the two-bank inner loss `L = ½‖ŷ−v‖²` and the descent error `e = v−ŷ` at the output. For one physical visit let `y = c H(h) conj(x)`, where h is the weight comb actually launched and c includes the reference, conversion phase and unsaturated field gain. With the real inner product on complex fields, the following slots implement the ideal derivatives. Each can use the same guide in the same propagation direction.

| Slot | Fields launched | Field to select after calibration |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Forward read | Reference R, old weights h, input x | Output data band, `y = c H(h) conj(x)` |
| Propagate the descent error | R, the same h, output error e | Input data band, `e_in = c H(h)ᵀ conj(e)`. A Hankel matrix is symmetric. |
| Write the weight increment | R, the saved x, the same e | Weight band, `u[s] = conj(c) Σ_{i+j=s} x[i] e[j]`. Coherently inject `ηu` into the old weight slot to obtain `h⁺ = h + ηu`. |

The write term is proportional to `x[i] e[j] conj(R)` at `f_A[i] + f_B[j] − f_R = f_h[i+j]`. One write slot produces all 127 coefficients. With only A, B and R launched on the two-way grid above, all 4,096 intended pairs land on the weight comb, with one pair at either edge and 64 in the middle. The closest competing first-order family is B+B−A, 110 GHz beyond the last weight tooth. Keep the old weight pulse on the memory path during this slot. Match the writer’s complex conversion coefficient and injection gain to the prescribed step size, and compensate the memory coupler’s through loss for unit retention. The carrier check leaves phase matching, write power and memory noise to be tested over 187.50–188.76 THz. [Guo et al., nondegenerate FWM field equation, Eq. 16](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-018-31470-x)

To update both banks, save or replay each visit’s input and process the old factors in reverse order, crossing the ideal shuffle via `P⁻¹`. A physical router with complex transfer S requires its calibrated adjoint `S†`. A time-multiplexed guide uses 34 write slots and up to 34 error-propagation slots per bank after prediction. The last adjoint returning to the original key can be omitted when only weight gradients are needed. Query with the updated weights. These are gradients in the stored Hankel factors. Updating the factors generally induces a different change in the effective matrix than a dense rank-one delta update. Central differences through both 34-visit banks and the shuffle agreed with the input and factor derivatives to within 3 × 10⁻⁸ relative error on eleven noiseless complex directional tests.

**Reading the slots in reverse.** Pair old weights `h₃₄, …, h₁` with saved inputs `x₃₃, …, x₀`, preserving the 127 tooth positions and phases within each weight comb. With one gated tap on a repeating optical frame of period T and adjacent weight slots separated by τ, read slot 34 and open the tap every `T − τ` to select slots 33 through 1. Allow an additional whole frame if processing needs more time. Hold each increment for its matching write slot after the old weight has served the adjoint. For faster replay, tap weight and activation copies during the forward visits and apply a slot-dependent delay. With forward and backward visit spacings F and B, delaying pair k by `D + (34−k)(F+B)` presents the pairs in descending order at spacing B. The common delay D allows the initial error to arrive. This timing construction requires phase-preserving delays and a tap-loss budget. [Optical packet reordering with differential delays, Yilmaz et al.](https://doi.org/10.1364/OL.33.001954)

**Switched fiber delays for the 34-factor backward pass.** For equal forward and backward factor intervals T, give packets 1 through 34 extra delays of 66T, 64T, …, 2T, 0. Six clocked 2 × 2 switches with fiber delay loops of 64T, 32T, 16T, 8T, 4T and 2T suffice for an isolated head. At each stage the switch either passes a packet through or sends it around the delay once. Route the weight sample and its saved input together on their respective wavelength bands. A discrete timing check at T = 400 ps found all 34 pairs in reverse order, at least 400 ps apart at every switch output. The maximum added delay is 26.4 ns (about 5.4 m of fiber at group index 1.468). Each packet crosses switches 6–11 times, counting entry and exit on a delayed path. Ideal routing sends the sampled field along one path. Charge the original sampling tap, switch insertion, leakage, dispersion and path phase to the replay budget. A single FWM guide still needs separate visits to generate the increments and each increment must meet its own stored weight on writeback. For equal intervals the same packet permutation in a second pass restores the original order, with storage or a second set of switches if the passes overlap. Thompson and Giordano demonstrated optical time-slot interchange with switched fiber delay lines. [Thompson and Giordano, OFC](https://doi.org/10.1364/OFC.1986.TuB4)

In one noiseless 64-tooth test, the 34 reversed weight packets and their paired forward inputs gave a directional loss derivative of −0.1240283278, within 1.7 × 10⁻¹⁰ of a central difference (complex perturbed chirp factors, seed 814, perturbation 10⁻⁶). A gradient step of 0.001 reduced the inner loss from 1.114 to 0.984.

The balanced example gives 17.7% forward field error after both banks. If the clean residual is 10% of the prediction in RMS field and the target is noiseless, the resulting error is about 1.77 times the residual before the first gradient operation. Forward activation noise can also enter the write field. A minimal online TTT variant adapts only the final factor of bank 2 in each lane. This needs its input, the output residual and one parallel write slot, and offers 127 adaptive complex coefficients per lane. Compare its written update with the ideal factor gradient by measuring their real inner-product alignment and the change in the clean inner loss after one step. [Archived TTT state and update order](../archive/README.md#what-carries-forward) · [Sun et al., inner and outer TTT parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.04620)

#### A dense TTT memory written one row per slot

The 34-pass MLP can remain fixed during inference. For a separate linear fast state, store each of the 64 rows of W in its own optical packet, with 64 weights on consecutive teeth. This gives 4,096 independently writable coefficients per lane. For squared loss, predict with the old state, form `e = v − Wk`, then write `W⁺ = W + η e k†` and query `W⁺q`. Each row depends only on its own old prediction and the shared key. The state and all reads and writes can follow ordinary row order. This changes the parameterization of the recurrent core. The original joint loss on two adaptive banks `B₂ P B₁` also needs the downstream bank and shuffle adjoints to update bank 1. [Delta-rule fast weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2102.11174) · [TTT inner and outer parameters](https://arxiv.org/html/2407.04620v2)

For a serial output choose a fixed idler tooth `j₀`. In row slot r put `W[r,i]` on weight tooth `i+j₀`. With signed-real data, the selected FWM idler reads the row dot product directly at a calibrated common scale. For complex data use the physical input `x[i] = conj(k[i])` to implement the logical read `Wk`. Subtract the row prediction from the matching target scalar on that idler. The write mixes the saved x comb, the **single** error tooth and the conjugated reference. Each `e[r] conj(k[i])` lands on its own weight tooth `i+j₀`. Gate the old row out of the mixer and add the scaled increments to it in memory. Three ascending row sweeps (64 key reads, 64 writes, 64 query reads on a shared guide) suffice. Preserve or replay the key and query and synchronize the residuals to their row writes. A fixed-idler query output requires time-to-wavelength conversion for the parallel MLP. Guan et al. demonstrated simultaneous conversion of 32 serial tributaries to 32 wavelengths. The row-dependent idler below yields a simpler interface to test. [Optical dot product](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.22.002609) · [FWM write term](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-018-31470-x) · [Guan et al., serial-to-parallel conversion](https://doi.org/10.1364/OFC.2016.W3D.2)

In a noiseless 64 × 64 complex test over 24 token updates, explicit frequency-indexed writes and post-update queries matched the dense delta rule within 1.4 × 10⁻¹⁶ in absolute error. As a read-only power check, split 2 pJ of data over 64 copies of a 20 ps key and use unit-norm rows with the existing 50 + 50 mW pump assumption. At the modeled single-row efficiency `η_FWM = 0.468`, the selected scalar averages 1,782 coherent photons for isotropic keys. Perfectly packing the rows and restoring the original vector energy requires +21.36 dB. With 5 dB noise figure and the modeled spontaneous FWM in the row, the single read gives 3.39% added RMS field error. Packing loss, target/key preparation, residual and write noise, weight drift, and post-update query accuracy remain to be simulated. Use one fixed calibrated weight scale over a sequence.

##### Returning the row results to a simultaneous comb

Store `W[r,i]` on weight tooth `i+r` in row slot r (indices 0–63, weight indices 0–126). Each row dot product is complete on idler r. On the two-way frequency plan the key can enter on A and predict on B. The write then pairs error tooth B[r] with the key comb to address exactly the same weight teeth. For a downstream MLP or shuffle expecting A, launch the query on B so the updated read puts `(W⁺q)[r]` on A[r]. This requires a query projection supplying B and a calibrated conversion in that direction. An interface confined to a fixed input band must budget any required band translation.

Separate the 64 output wavelengths, delay wavelength j by `(63−j)τ`, remultiplex them into one waveguide and open one broadband gate in slot 63. Idler j generated in row slot r arrives in slot `63+r−j`. Only `r=j` reaches the gate in the ideal resolved-mode model, even with consecutive 64-slot frames. The complete output is one pulse with all 64 complex coordinates on their assigned teeth. Correct the fixed carrier phase of each path and keep the sources coherent over the sweep. With 20 ps slot pitch the differential delays span 0–1.26 ns and a sweep takes 1.28 ns before common propagation. Three consecutive complex query frames including every in-band row/idler correlation reproduced the digital fields within 1.3 × 10⁻¹⁶ in an ideal discrete-mode check and retained the energy of the selected row pulses. If the following reader needs 64 copies of this vector, charge its replay separately. For the weight write, a packed prediction can be subtracted from a simultaneous target on B, then complementary delays `jτ` serialize the gated residual into one B tooth per row. [Guan et al., WDM channel alignment with optical delays](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-018-03458-8)

The equal-data-energy read already includes the `η_FWM/64` useful fraction. For uniform transmission t of the demultiplexer, delay, multiplexer and gate before restoration, `G = 64/(η_FWM t)` and `ε² = [64 + (FG−1)/2]/S` for the single unitary read above. Total retimer losses of **0, 3, 6 and 12 dB** give **3.39%, 4.51%, 6.16% and 11.97%** added RMS field error. A 5% budget allows **4.02 dB** before crosstalk or other distortion. One fabricated 64-channel, 50 GHz flat-top AWG had 5.8–7.8 dB insertion loss per device. At 20 ps × 50 GHz, even a rectangular tooth pulse has only 77.4% of its spectral energy inside its nominal ±25 GHz interval (`∫₋₀.₅⁰.⁵ sinc²(u) du`, normalized sinc). The remainder enters other spectral paths or is attenuated according to the filters. Simulate the complex passbands, pulse shapes, path phases, delay errors and finite gate extinction on the redesigned carrier grid. [An et al., 64-channel AWG](https://cpl.iphy.ac.cn/article/id/33126) · [Lowery, optical subcarriers and matched filtering](https://doi.org/10.1109/JLT.2016.2618388)

**Gain before the output delays.** The 4.02 dB allowance assumes all gain follows the gate. For a preamplifier after pump rejection with power gain A and a final amplifier with gain B, set `A t B = 64/η_FWM`. Each amplifier adds `m(g) = (F g−1)/2` ASE photons per matched mode at its own output. With lossless pump rejection the single-read variance becomes `ε² = [64 + t B m(A) + m(B)]/S`, where S is the mean restored signal photons per tooth. The term 64 is the restored spontaneous FWM contribution. Omit the ASE term for an absent amplifier. At 2 pJ delivered across the 64 row reads, uniform 10 dB retiming loss and 5 dB noise figure, all +31.36 dB after the gate gives 9.55% added RMS field error. Moving +10 dB ahead of the delays and keeping +21.36 dB after the gate gives **4.48%**, leaving **2.22% RMS** for independent distortion within a 5% single-read target. The early amplifier also sees the unwanted in-band idlers. Include pump-rejection loss, aggregate signal power, gain compression, and coherent leakage through the finite gate before extending this result to repeated TTT writes. [Amplifier noise convention](#amplifier-noise-between-layers) · [Cost of replaying one MLP packet](#from-one-mlp-output-packet-to-the-next-optical-layer)

A chirped Bragg grating followed by the same gate is an alternative source of wavelength-dependent delay. For 20 ps steps on a 50 GHz comb the group-delay slope is −0.40 ps/GHz, about +50 ps/nm near 1550 nm over approximately 25 nm. A continuous slope also disperses each pulse. Li et al. measured 2.864 ns of group-delay variation over 23 nm in a silicon nitride chirped grating occupying about 2 × 2 mm, with 6 dB insertion loss at its center. Wang et al. measured **49.72 ps/nm over 26 nm** in a 7.4 × 0.7 mm device. At its maximum setting the group-delay ripple was ±14.70 ps and the reported losses at the short- and long-wavelength band edges were 8.55 and 14.01 dB. Both the ripple relative to a 20 ps slot and the transmission across the comb matter for coherent readout. Use the complex response and the gate waveform to test leakage from the other rows. [Li et al., Fig. 5](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.480375) · [Wang et al., Figs. 4–5](https://doi.org/10.1002/nap2.70072)

**Grating efficiency.** The 8.55 and 14.01 dB band-edge losses in Wang et al.'s telecom chip correspond to 14.0% and 4.0% of incident power at its output, before the gate. A fixed grating can return each selected wavelength along the same optical path with high reflectivity. Senyu Wang et al. reported 98.2% average grating reflectivity in fiber over 11.97 nm centered at 1031.7 nm, with group-delay dispersion magnitude 60.7 ps². In frequency units this spans 3.37 THz and gives 19.1 ps delay per 50 GHz, close to the proposed 3.15 THz between outer teeth and 20 ps step. A packaged 1550 nm device with this performance and adequate phase fidelity remains to be demonstrated for the row reader. Full C-band fiber modules have measured 2–3 dB insertion loss at much steeper dispersion settings. [Senyu Wang et al., fiber grating](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.optlastec.2024.111035) · [Tipsuwannakul et al., Table I](https://doi.org/10.1364/JOCN.4.000514)

**Energy in the 20 ps gate.** A smooth grating also disperses the spectrum within each tooth. As an isolated-pulse check, assume a rectangular field pulse of duration T = 20 ps, unit reflectivity, unlimited passband, zero delay ripple and the target constant delay slope. After removing the carrier's intended delay and phase, the field transfer is `H(u) = exp(±iπu²)` for `u = (ν−νⱼ)T`, since the tooth spacing is 1/T. Fresnel integration and a direct Fourier propagation both put **73.62%** of the energy in the centered 20 ps gate and **9.40%** in each immediately adjacent slot. Projection onto the original rectangular pulse mode retains 69.86% of the incident energy. An equal-amplitude pulse displaced by one slot on the same tooth contributes 21.75% of the wanted field amplitude to that projection. Actual error depends on pulse shape, the other FWM products, measured spectral phase and gate response.

**Footprint per simultaneous head.** For 64 separate delay arms, group index 2 gives a 3.00 mm increment per 20 ps slot, an 18.9 cm longest differential path and 6.04 m of extra waveguide summed over the arms. Liu et al. packed a 1 m silicon nitride spiral into 6.6 mm². Scaling its area per meter gives roughly **40 mm² per lane or 320 mm² for eight lanes** for the delays alone. The 64 individual bends and connections, two AWGs per lane and gate need a floorplan. In a grating implementation all wavelengths share one dispersive device per active lane. Wang et al.'s 7.4 × 0.7 mm device gives a reference of **5.18 mm² per lane or 41.4 mm² for eight**, before gates and routing. These estimates cover the query-output interface. [Liu et al., Fig. 4](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-021-21973-z) · [Weihan Wang et al., Fig. 4](https://doi.org/10.1002/nap2.70072)

The archived sizing study schedules **16 logical heads, one active at a time, with eight parallel lanes per head**. The row-stored alternative can also schedule distinct head states through the same eight lanes. If extended to 64 logical heads, one at a time still uses eight query-output retimers. Operating all 64 at once with separate single-mode lanes on the same comb grid uses 512 such interfaces, or 32,768 wavelength paths in the separate-arm implementation. With one shared read/write/query guide per lane and a 20 ps row pitch, three 64-row sweeps occupy 3.84 ns per head and 245.76 ns for 64 sequential heads, before propagation, guards and state access. Each logical head retains its own weights under either schedule. [Archived design point in the source bundle](../archive/README.md#original-notes-code-and-results)

##### From one MLP output packet to the next optical layer

The 4.48% split-gain estimate above assumes that all 2 pJ from one MLP packet reach the FWM guide across the 64 row reads. Call the actual delivered fraction u. Each equal replica carries `uE/64` from a packet of energy E. For a unitary matrix, the selected row energies sum to `η_FWM uE/64`. This counts the division among the reads once. An ideal fanout delivers 31.25 fJ per row from 2 pJ. A six-stage binary lattice that keeps one output of every balanced split–delay–recombine stage has field tap 1/64. It delivers 0.488 fJ per row and 31.25 fJ across the whole train (`u = 1/64`). At the same pump powers this reduces the mean selected row signal from about 1,780 to 28 photons before output handling. Holding the original 100 mW of data in every 20 ps slot would require 128 pJ at the guide per sweep.

Let p be the fraction of the selected row energy delivered to the downstream read window. The useful energy divided by the MLP launch energy is `η_FWM u p/64` before amplification. These calculations assume the other idlers have already been rejected for a wavelength-independent output lattice. Each retained row occupies a distinct tooth. A lattice with L equal field taps of 1/L delivers `p = 1/L²` in one complete output slot or `p = s/L²` over s complete slots. With a fixed idler, summing the row scalars on that carrier would erase the row index.

| Replay and collection for one unitary read | Replay energy fraction u | Collected fraction p | Useful energy gain before amplification |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Ideal replay and lossless wavelength-dependent alignment into one slot | 1 | 1 | −21.36 dB |
| Separate 64-tap single-output lattices for replay and collection, one slot | 1/64 | 1/4,096 | −75.54 dB |
| Same replay, archived 128-tap collector with 7 dB excess, full 64-slot hold | 1/64 | (64/128²) × 0.1995 | −70.50 dB |
| Same replay and archived collector, one 20 ps slot | 1/64 | (1/128²) × 0.1995 | −88.57 dB |

A two-port interleaver retains both paths between stages. Qiu et al. demonstrated 64 time-delayed copies at about 14 GHz and reported 2.2 dB on-chip loss after removing the final 3 dB port split and fiber coupling. One output thus gives a 5.2 dB replay-loss reference. Using it at 50 GHz would require suitable delays, uniform replica amplitudes and calibrated optical phases across the 64-tooth data comb. Pair this with row-dependent output wavelengths and the wavelength-dependent delay described above. Each row already contains the full frequency dot product. The output device aligns the 64 independent results into the same packet. [Qiu et al., Figs. 1–3 and Methods](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z)

At 2 pJ upstream, putting `u = 10^(−5.2/10)` into the split-gain example gives 8.15% added RMS field error for one read, before timing distortion. In the formula above use `A t B = 64/(η_FWM u)` and replace the spontaneous FWM term 64 by `64/u`. The example still assumes +10 dB early gain, 10 dB uniform output loss, 5 dB amplifier noise figures and nonsaturating gain. A hypothetical lossless, distortion-free output interface with one restoring amplifier gives 6.17% under the same input assumptions. Delivering 2 pJ to the row reads with this input reference requires a 6.62 pJ launch, about 331 mW during the 20 ps packet. The two 50 mW pumps already deliver 128 pJ in one 1.28 ns read. A TTT update also needs the key again for the write and a query replay for the updated read.

##### A simulated read through a chirped fiber Bragg grating

One isolated 64 × 64 read uses a 50 GHz grid, 2 pJ of data before replay, 5.2 dB one-port replay loss, and 50 mW each in the reference and total weight comb at the guide. The assumed FWM power efficiency for a unit-norm matched row is 0.468, extrapolated from Lei's −7.3 dB single-pump measurement. The fiber module reflects a flat 3.20 THz band with 3 dB insertion loss. The baseline has zero group-delay ripple and delays adjacent carriers by one row slot. The 3 dB is a design allowance informed by measurements of full C-band modules at different dispersions. External coupling and gate excess loss require their own budget. [Lei et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Qiu et al., replay interleaver](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z) · [Tipsuwannakul et al., Table I](https://publications.lib.chalmers.se/records/fulltext/local_162900.pdf)

In row r, input i and weight W[r,h] generate idler j = r+h−i. Only the terms with h = i give the desired row dot product on tooth r. The calculation propagates all 190 possible idler centers from this mixing process, including spectral tails entering the reflection band. In normalized frequency u = fT and spacing d = ΔfT, the grating has field response exp(iπu²/d) inside the band. A single broadband gate opens where the desired rows coincide. The output modes have the pulse shape and tooth frequencies expected by the next layer. Carrier phases are calibrated per tooth. For physical transfer M in normalized units, fit one common complex scale g = ⟨W,M⟩/‖W‖² for the read. With isotropic inputs, ‖M/g − W‖/‖W‖ (Frobenius norms) gives RMS field error relative to the ideal output. The useful preamplification energy gain relative to the launched data packet is (0.468/64) × 10^(−5.2/10) × 10^(−3/10) × |g|². This excludes light orthogonal to the intended multiplication.

| Input rows → output gate | Test matrix | Useful energy gain | Coherent error in 64 modes | Added noise in 64 modes | Total RMS field error in 64 modes | Whole gated waveform error, before noise |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 20 ps square → 20 ps square | Identity | −29.8 dB | 24.0% | 6.7% | 24.9% | 24.3% |
| 20 ps square → 20 ps square | Dense | −31.25 to −31.07 dB | 62.0–66.1% | 7.8–7.9% | 62.45–66.55% | 62.9–67.0% |
| 60 ps half-cosine → 60 ps matched modes | Dense | −30.0 dB | 4.9–5.0% | 6.8% | 8.4% | 31.2–31.9% |
| 60 ps half-cosine → central 20 ps square | Dense | −32.0 dB | 11.0–11.3% | 8.3% | 13.8–14.0% | 11.1–11.4% |
| 80 ps half-cosine → central 20 ps square | Dense | −32.7 dB | 7.0–7.2% | 8.8% | 11.3–11.4% | 7.0–7.3% |

Dense ranges cover ten seeded real orthogonal matrices programmed directly into the row weights. An independent time-domain propagation measures the full waveform inside the gate against the ideal encoded comb, including light outside the 64 target modes. The 60 ps matched-mode result assumes a following stage selective for that field envelope. A simple open gate passes the additional light shown in the last column. The central 20 ps gate returns a square-pulse interface using a shaped data packet and flat weights and reference during each row. The slower cases assume a native input pulse of the stated duration and a grating slope scaled to the new row pitch. Converting an upstream 20 ps packet into that pulse has an unbudgeted implementation cost. At fixed 100 mW combined pump power, 20, 60 and 80 ps rows consume 128, 384 and 512 pJ of optical pump energy per read, respectively.

At 20 ps the nominal gain budget is −5.2 dB replay, −21.36 dB for FWM and equal sharing across the 64 reads, −3 dB fiber-module loss and −1.56 dB for overlap with the square output mode. This gives about 1.5 fJ of intended output across the comb, or roughly 190 photons per tooth on average at 1550 nm. The fitted identity gain is higher because some leakage adds coherently to the diagonal. For one input tooth addressing coefficient W[r,i], the ideal desired power path also scales by |W[r,i]|². As a ripple sensitivity check, a sinusoidal ±5 ps group-delay error of period 200 GHz with calibrated carrier phases gives dense-matrix total error of 75–78%, 16.6–16.9% and 12.9–13.1% for the 20, 60 and 80 ps rows with a 20 ps square gate. The actual measured complex grating response is needed to forecast device accuracy.

For noise, use +10 dB after pump rejection and before the grating, then enough gain after the gate to restore the intended data energy. Each amplifier has a 5 dB noise figure. The variance sums amplified spontaneous emission over each accepted optical mode and spontaneous FWM from the 64 modeled data vacuum modes per row. The estimate assumes coherent input, flat phase-matched coupling, quiet pumps and unsaturated amplifiers. Real grating phase and amplitude, other mixing bands, neighboring frames, noise from upstream layers and TTT write accuracy need separate validation. Run `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1 python3 notes/fwm_grating_read.py --waveform` from the repo root to reproduce the table. The optional `--ripple-ps` specifies the peak of a sinusoidal delay error of period four teeth (200 GHz). [Simulation](fwm_grating_read.py) · [Amplifier noise model](#amplifier-noise-between-layers)

#### Full factor gradients with a one-directional local ring

The factorized core can keep all 34 factors adaptive. Place them once in an interleaved circular frame and use a gated tap. In a 41-slot local ring store `hₖ` at address `20(k−1) mod 41`. Opening the tap every 20 slot periods visits factors 1 through 34. Once the output error is ready, start at factor 34 and open it every 21 periods. Since `21 ≡ −20 (mod 41)`, the **same packets** return as 34, 33, …, 1. At an illustrative 20 ps pitch the memory round trip is 820 ps and the two visit intervals are 400 and 420 ps. A switchable data-path delay must support both intervals including guide, gain and switching. Whole memory turns may be added for processing. Synchronously gated optical buffers have experimental precedent at other timings. [Mack et al., optical packet buffer](https://ocaqpi.ece.ucsb.edu/sites/default/files/2021-02/2008_Mack_Synchronously-Loaded-Optical-Packet-Buffer.pdf)

Cache each factor input at its weight's address on the key pass and its output error at the same address on the adjoint pass, with fixed path offsets compensated. After the adjoints, run a forward-order write sweep over the two caches. Delay each computed increment to meet its own weight packet at the injector and start the query after writeback. One isolated 34-factor bank uses 34 key reads, 33 adjoint reads, 34 writes and 34 query reads. A preceding adaptive bank also needs the adjoint back to its output through the shuffle. A noiseless test of this address schedule compared one complex gradient direction per factor with central differences (maximum discrepancy 1.3 × 10⁻⁹, normalized by the larger of one and the derivative magnitudes) and reproduced the post-update query. The 820 ps period belongs to an active head's local store. The proposed 150 m shared memory would need synchronized load and writeback of a head frame in the same interleaved layout. Round-trip tap loss, accumulated storage noise, guard time, and the actual adjoint of a pump-depleted guide determine physical feasibility.

For a smaller state on the same loop, adapt only its final **two** factors. Save the key-side input a and the intermediate b. From the residual e, one adjoint read of the old second factor supplies the error at b. The two writes then use `(a, error at b)` and `(b, e)` in forward order. This exposes 254 stored coefficients per lane and needs one adjoint read after prediction. Compare its sequence recall and language-model loss with the row-stored dense state at matched write noise.

**Symmetric read schedule with one copy of each weight.** Place the packets for factors k and 35−k half a round trip apart in the active head's local ring. In a 42-slot frame, choose `a[k] = 20(k−1) mod 42` for k = 1…17 and `a[35−k] = (a[k]+21) mod 42`. Starting at slot 0, wait 20 slots sixteen times, 21 slots once and 22 slots sixteen times. This selects factors 1…34. Starting at slot 21 when the residual is ready and repeating the very same waits selects 34…1. Both passes use one gated tap and visit all 34 distinct addresses. Caching each forward input at its factor's address delivers the correct pair on the backward pass. The subsequent write sweep updates each factor once.

At an assumed 20 ps address pitch, the data return must support 400, 420 and 440 ps intervals. Each sweep spans 13.86 ns, or 16.5 storage laps between its first and last taps. The 41-slot layout above needs two return-delay settings. Both layouts require resolved packet gates, stable phases and a storage loss budget. A noiseless 64-tooth complex test of the 42-slot layout agreed with a central directional loss derivative within 7.1 × 10⁻¹¹ (seed 1213, step 10⁻⁶). Tying the **values** of mirrored factors in the forward operator has a different effect. For signed-real Hankel factors, a 34-visit palindrome built from 17 independent factors computes AᵀA, where A is the first half's product. This restricts the single bank to symmetric positive semidefinite maps. The address symmetry leaves all 34 factors independent.

#### Adding a stored vector on each pass of the original product

Return to the one-register, 34-pass product with 64 data teeth. During a read let H₁ through H_L be the programmed signed-real Hankel matrices, with L=34 and P=H_L⋯H₁. The modeled FWM interaction is conjugating; the algebra here uses the signed-real field model with calibrated phases. A physical vector injector must match the data band of the visit. The two-band frequency-shifting design is retained as a comparison in the [energy notes](#energy-of-the-two-band-displacement-multiplier).

**Ordinary bias packets.** Let Aᵢ=Hᵢ⋯H₁ be the product through lap i and Bᵢ=H_L⋯Hᵢ₊₁ the remaining product, with B_L=I. The recurrence z₀=q, zᵢ=Hᵢzᵢ₋₁+bᵢ gives y(q)=Pq+Σᵢ Bᵢbᵢ. For any two queries, y(q)−y(q′)=P(q−q′). All the bias packets, even when written by past tokens, contribute just one observable 64-vector at the final port of a fixed read schedule. A bias-only correction that changes one query shifts every other query equally. If P also changes, choosing the new offset to compensate (ΔP)q₀ can preserve one anchor q₀; preserving additional answers still constrains ΔP on their differences. A bias can fit a common offset and affect training without adding independently writable directions to the linear map. Changing the output port/time or applying an intermediate nonlinear activation changes this argument.

**Query-selected optical injection.** Give each visit one real scalar gᵢ(q) controlling the entire vector injected at that visit. The read becomes y(q)=Pq+Σᵢ gᵢ(q)Bᵢbᵢ. If gᵢ(q)=rᵢᵀq with fixed directions rᵢ, the extra effective matrix is Σᵢ (Bᵢbᵢ) rᵢᵀ, a sum of at most L rank-one terms. The base product can still be full rank. With general supplied features g(q), write an association (k,v) by launching v through the unchanged factors with injection disabled. On visit i the field is Aᵢv. Tap it and add gᵢ(k)Aᵢv to stored packet bᵢ. Since BᵢAᵢ=P at every visit, the change on read is exactly P v times the inner product of g(k) and g(q). This is an additive linear-attention numerator in the supplied feature coordinates, followed by P. Two orthogonal codes give zero mutual interference. Distinct nonorthogonal codes have the usual inner-product interference. The scheme assumes the factors stay fixed while the packets represent live associations. A later change in a prefix generally invalidates packets stored in its coordinates. [Linear-attention and delta-rule distinction](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html)

The simplest instance is a supplied slot address a. Enable injection only at lap a. To overwrite an address, replace its packet by Aₐv; it is unnecessary to read its old value. The result is P(q+vₐ). For a memory-only read launch a dark data packet and supply the address separately. This stores 34 independently replaceable 64-vectors, or 2,176 signed-real values. The retrieved value is in the common P-transformed output coordinates. Specifying an arbitrary value directly in the output coordinates would require an appropriate preimage if P is invertible. Clock selection, a 64-tooth packet memory, a forward value pass through the addressed lap, stored-field tapping, coherent injection and restoration are hardware costs. A simple comparison is to store the raw vₐ in a fiber slot and inject it alongside q at the input; the resulting P(q+vₐ) is identical. Hard lookup alone gives no computational reason to prefer injection inside the loop.

For distributed writes, 32 mutually orthogonal Walsh codes fit in the first 32 slots, leaving all 34 noncommuting H factors active on the data. The gate uses one whole-comb sign on each active visit, with a common 1/√32 normalization. Address generation and the schedule of signs remain to be implemented. Updating all bias packets involves tapping and scaling each prefix field, with calibrated gain on both the through and injection paths. A coupler cannot supply an arbitrary unity-gain field sum without paying for optical energy. With continuous 34-component features, the read requires delivery of a scalar amplitude per pass. Neither case has a wall-plug energy estimate. True soft-key delta correction to a desired output-space residual e through this same writer would add P e if launched directly. Obtaining e at the output requires a preimage of e or a specially chosen fixed product such as I. Direct hard-address packet replacement avoids this issue.

**Numerical check.** A simulation used the existing seed-216 perturbed bank of 34 Hankels on 64 data teeth. The factors were held fixed. With 34 one-hot addresses and 100 random overwrites, the relative field error against P(q+vₐ) was 2.26×10⁻¹⁵ and the largest change at an unselected address was zero. With 32 Walsh-coded additive writes, relative error was 2.28×10⁻¹⁵. With overlapping continuous 34-component keys and queries, relative error against the independently evaluated dense feature-space numerator with the same P was 2.34×10⁻¹⁵. These tests supply the addresses and ideal coherent field operations; they measure neither learned content recall nor optical accuracy. [Reproducible check](hankel_addressed_injection.py)

**Taking the gate from the circulating field instead.** Tapping tooth j of the current packet to multiply a stored vector b gives a genuinely different factor, H + b eⱼᵀ. A write to b changes one whole column in these local coordinates. After preceding and following products A and B the end-to-end change is exactly (B Δb)(eⱼᵀ A), a rank-one update for every Δb. The effective key direction depends on all preceding factors, including their writable columns. For example, in identity coordinates (I+v e₂ᵀ)(I+u e₁ᵀ) has an extra term v u₂ e₁ᵀ. Changing v also changes the answer at the first key if u₂ is nonzero. Thus a local tooth tap alone gives no independent-slot guarantee. Generating the optical field b times the tapped scalar, coherently and with adequate gain, is an additional multiplication. The numerical test above covers externally supplied gates.

#### Viability of a 34-factor writable matrix

A 64 × 64 bank has 4,318 real comb coefficients across 34 untied Hankel factors. Ye and Lim prove generic complex expressivity at 33 factors and density of that family. Their sufficient bounds for exact factorization of every invertible matrix and every matrix are deeper. The Jacobian test below checks independent local control at an actual signed-real program. Optical power limits and write fidelity require separate evidence. [Ye and Lim, Theorems 4.3 and 5.5](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1307.5132#page=9)

For squared loss and residual e = v − Wk, the desired dense descent direction is G = ekᵀ. Let J be the Jacobian of the 4,096 entries of W with respect to the stored comb fields. A factor-gradient write gives the exact parameter step ηJᵀvec(G), using one convolution of the saved input and local error per factor. In matrix coordinates this induces ηJJᵀvec(G) to first order, with further product terms at finite step size. A simple exact counterexample programs all 34 factors as the central-tooth reversal R, giving W = I. For key eⱼ and residual εeⱼ, every factor gradient lights only its central tooth. After an ordinary simultaneous gradient step the bank computes (1 + ηε)³⁴ I. Dense delta changes just entry (j,j). [Dense delta rule](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html) · [Local FWM adjoint and write](#backpropagation-and-the-ttt-weight-update)

| Starting program (seed 216) | Condition number of W | Numerical rank of J | Median relative error of one dense write with best scalar step, first order | Top-1 recall after 64 writes, ordinary / tooth-corrected |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 34 distinct real orthogonal Hankels | 1.00 | 3,196 / 4,096 | 92.5% | 28.9% / 37.0% |
| Same combs perturbed by 5% in norm, then restored to their initial per-comb energy | 2.21 | 4,096 / 4,096 | 92.8% | 28.0% / 36.6% |
| Direct dense delta from either starting W | as above | 4,096 / 4,096 | 0% | 100% |

Rank counts singular values above 10⁻¹⁰ of the largest. In the full-rank example J has singular values 0.000557 to 38.25, a condition number of about 68,700. The single-write error is the Frobenius distance from a unit-norm rank-one write after optimally rescaling the induced first-order update, over 64 random signed-real key/residual pairs. For recall, each of 64 orthogonal unit keys is written once with an independent unit value. The metric retrieves the closest of the 64 target values after all writes. Six separate streams select a scalar rate by final vector error for the table. Twenty-four held-out streams test it. The tooth correction weights the gradient by inverse anti-diagonal multiplicity. For the perturbed program, seeds 326 and 983 gave ordinary recall of 25.2% and 23.7%.

Selecting the rate on calibration top-1 instead gave 29.4% ordinary and 41.9% corrected recall at the original full-rank program. With the same correction, scaling the initial end-to-end gain to 0.3 or 0.1 gave 51.2% or 50.1% respectively. These are noiseless synthetic tests of untrained initial programs. A jointly trained factorized TTT layer may learn different keys and initial factors. Run `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=4 python3 notes/hankel_product_viability.py --spectrum` and use `--seed` or `--map-scale` for the sensitivity checks. [Experiment](hankel_product_viability.py)

A single-guide predict, update and post-update query uses 34 forward reads, 33 adjoint reads, 34 write convolutions and 34 query reads per isolated bank. The symmetric slot order solves replay. Practical all-optical TTT also needs coherent caches and writeback, and the gradient of the loaded device. At the proposed 100 mW data operating point, the flat weak-conversion model already exceeds its reference photon budget. The simulated factor updates above assume ideal fields. [Power and photon-balance calculation](#20-ps-with-100-mw-of-data-per-lane)

For an exact signed-real comparison, let F be a normalized 64 × 64 Walsh–Hadamard matrix and synthesize Wᵢⱼ = Σₘ hₘ[i+j] Fₘⱼ. A write adds η times the convolution of e with the pointwise product of k and row m of F to comb hₘ. Since FᵀF = I, synthesis gives exactly ηekᵀ. A check on an arbitrary real W and one rank-one write gave 3.4 × 10⁻¹⁶ relative post-write error (seed 221). The central anti-diagonal has 64 independently writable entries and receives one coefficient from each mask, so this fixed diagonal-mask construction requires at least 64 contributions. Coherently summing them on every read brings back the fan-in and energy budget of the summed approach. The single-guide product supports a factorized TTT model with its own learning dynamics. An efficient arbitrary dense write remains unsupported for that product.

**Temporal summation in the output pulse interleaver.** Let zₘ be the partial output comb in slot m. An ideal passive output pulse interleaver adds the fields from all 64 slots with equal weights, giving Σₘ zₘ/8 in the selected packet. The fraction of incoming partial-comb energy in that packet is ‖Σₘ zₘ‖²/(64 Σₘ ‖zₘ‖²). It reaches one for identical in-phase vectors and 1/64 for mutually orthogonal vectors. Rainville et al. measured up to 90% stacking efficiency for a deliberately phase-shaped 81-pulse burst, with 90.5% device throughput reported separately. In a matrix read the partial fields depend on the data and weights. [Rainville et al., §4 and Fig. 7](https://doi.org/10.1364/OPTICA.533803)

For the canonical Walsh encoding of W = I, hₘ[2j] = Fₘⱼ and the odd weight teeth are dark. With one lit input j, each slot produces 32 equal-power idlers inside the 64-tooth output band. The target tooth j has the same phase in every slot. The other 31 cancel in the collected packet. The ideal output pulse interleaver therefore collects all the arriving target-tooth energy and 1/32 of the total arriving in-band energy. If the input pulse extender produces 64 copies without excess loss, each slot has the same total weight and reference pump powers, and the flat small-signal conversion efficiency summed over the intended idler family is η, seed-to-selected-tooth energy gain is η/64. Using the earlier extrapolation η = 0.468 gives −21.4 dB before other component losses, at the cost of 64 pumped visits. The archived 128-tap lattice uses a different temporal transfer function and has its own read-noise budget. [Archived collector calculation](#fwm-with-the-archived-binary-delay-lattice)

##### Choosing the FWM multiplier for a dense temporal sum

The cell must process the frequency channels together in shared optical components and support simple optical backpropagation. A per-tooth AWG phase shaper on either side of the FWM guide defeats the spatial scaling goal. The staggered-timing design processes the whole comb in one guide and requires a second coherent delay grid to propagate input gradients. This rules it out for the cell with full backpropagation. The ideal Walsh and staggered-timing reads are numerical benchmarks. [Delay footprint and design decision](#why-the-staggered-timing-cell-was-ruled-out) · [Whole-comb timing and training](#whole-comb-timing-for-the-adjoint-and-weight-update)

Compare 100 signed-real 64 × 64 orthogonal targets, a 2 pJ input packet at 1550 nm, 20 ps interactions and 128 pJ of available optical pump energy per read. Useful gain is the energy in the correct output vector divided by the original data-packet energy, including passive division by the input pulse extender and ideal output routing. A single guide has a 50 mW reference and up to 50 mW across its weight comb. Each guide of the 33-slot pair gets 48.48 mW for each pump, giving the same integrated cap. The flat weak-conversion coefficient η = 0.468 for a unit weight comb is extrapolated from Lei et al.’s −7.3 dB measurement with one 63.1 mW CW pump. The following entries are model medians. Guide pump energy counts the programmed weights and reference during the interaction windows. Laser inefficiency, upstream discarded pump light, controls, amplifier energy, excess insertion loss and gain saturation are outside that column. [Lei et al., Table 1](https://otip.sjtu.edu.cn/publication/Journal/2025-ACSPhotonics-ZhengshunLei-Efficient%20Broadband%20Wavelength%20Conversion%20in.pdf) · [Reproduce the comparisons](fwm_multiplier_choice.py)

| Exact dense read and output interface | Occupied frame | Guide pump energy | Useful gain before amplification | Added RMS field error after restoring unity gain |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| One dot product per row, ideal wavelength-dependent retiming | 64 slots, 1.28 ns | 128 pJ | −21.36 dB | 3.39% |
| One Hankel per slot, ideal per-tooth Walsh signs and output pulse interleaver (benchmark) | 64 slots, 1.28 ns | 110 pJ | −22.75 dB | 3.74% |
| One Hankel per slot, Fourier phases from staggered timing, retimed output pulse interleaver (benchmark) | 64 slots plus one guard copy, 1.30 ns | 117 pJ | −22.29 dB | 3.57% |
| Two Hankels per slot, first factors fixed in advance, ideal 33-way collection | 33 slots, 0.66 ns with two pipelined guides | 122 pJ | −47.19 dB | 61.82% |

For unit-norm rows the collected row gain is η/64. For an orthonormal Walsh or Fourier code, let p be the maximum norm of the inverse-transformed weight combs and K the number of equal-energy data copies. The single-guide gain is η/(Kp²), including the 1/8 field weight of the 64-slot output pulse interleaver. For M equal-energy copies and an output pulse interleaver summing M slots after two guides, the gain of the program below is η₁η₂/(Mp)², with p the largest second-comb norm before scaling. At 2 pJ input the four rows of the table leave about 1,783, 1,295, 1,439 and 4.7 signal photons per mean output tooth respectively before gain. With only input tooth j lit, the gain into one specified output tooth i is the tabulated linear gain times |Wᵢⱼ|². [Weight synthesis and normalization](fwm_multiplier_choice.py)

**Why two passes cost so much.** Let H(a)ᵢⱼ = a[i+j] on the 64 accepted intermediate and output teeth. The same fixed first factor B in every slot gives (ΣₘH(aₘ))B, with at most 127 independent coefficients. For a Toeplitz target T, a fixed reversal J gives T = (TJ)J. If the preceding layer can label its teeth in reverse order, TJ alone suffices for a signed-real read. A different fixed Bₘ in each slot can represent a dense target. Thirty-two slots give only 4,064 adjustable coefficients. The script gives an exact 33-slot schedule. Thirty-one first combs have equal amplitudes on teeth m and 96+m, m = 0…30. The last two first combs illuminate only tooth 31 and tooth 63 respectively. The first 32 pairs set every interior diagonal difference, then the last pair writes the remaining Toeplitz matrix. Reusing one guide consumes 66 interaction slots. A 33-slot input train would require a matched 33-slot output sum. The existing six-stage output pulse interleaver is sized for 64 slots. One ideal construction splits energy 32:1 between a 32-way tree and a single delayed copy, then uses the reciprocal network for collection.

For any pair, with `D(W) = W[1:,1:] − W[:-1,:-1]`, the intermediate sum telescopes to `D(H(a)H(b))[i,j] = a[i+64]b[j+64] − a[i]b[j]`. The two boundary terms give `‖D(H(a)H(b))‖* ≤ ‖a‖₂‖b‖₂`, where the nuclear norm sums singular values. The passive field coefficients αₘ of the input pulse extender and βₘ of the output pulse interleaver obey `Σₘ|αₘβₘ| ≤ 1`. Thus the useful power scale for exact W satisfies `|g|² ≤ η₁η₂ / ‖D(W)‖*²`, with unit-norm pump caps in both guides. Conjugate the first comb for complex fields. This bound covers adjustable as well as fixed pairs, any slot count at those per-guide peak powers, a flat 64-tooth intermediate filter and an output pulse interleaver that applies the same sum at every wavelength. A uniform intermediate power gain multiplies the ceiling by that gain. Additional wavelength-dependent operators change the model. The median nuclear norm was 79.24 and the median ceiling was **−45.11 dB**. Allowing 5% relative Frobenius error relaxes this ceiling by at most 0.73 dB at the median, since `‖D(E)‖* ≤ 2√63 ‖E‖F` and `‖W‖F = 8`. A Toeplitz target has D = 0 and escapes this bound. The identity and random orthogonal tests probe different regimes.

**Precision and packing.** The noise column uses one final 5 dB-noise-figure amplifier, independent vacuum terms from the 64 modeled modes at each phase-conjugating pass, quiet pumps and one polarization. It measures added field noise, independent of deterministic waveform error. For the pair, +10 or +20 dB between guides lowers the modeled error to 31.8% or 26.2%, with +37.2 or +27.2 dB still needed at the output. A +20 dB middle amplifier supplies about 24 pJ of extra signal energy per read on isotropic data. The reversal slot then delivers about 133 mW of signal to the second guide, making depletion and saturation important. Giving the single-guide designs +10 dB after the input pulse extender and reducing the final gain yields 2.27% for rows and 2.02% for Walsh. The input booster supplies an extra 18 pJ of signal. Unknown pump fluctuations, out-of-band vacuum and device errors can raise these estimates. [Amplifier noise model](#amplifier-noise-between-layers)

Direct rows have a 1.39 dB ideal useful-energy advantage over Walsh. They require an interface that delays output tooth r by (63−r) slots and rejects the unwanted row/idler combinations. If rows are already isolated on their own teeth, putting those pulses through the equal-weight output pulse interleaver costs another 18.06 dB. Unisolated idlers also introduce coherent errors. For the 20 ps square waveform the previously simulated chirped grating gave 62–66% dense-matrix distortion, even with zero delay ripple. The Walsh scheme has its own implementation requirement of tooth-dependent signs across 3.15 THz without distorting the packets. With the same input pulse extender, compare the row receiver’s excess loss against the losses of the Walsh masks and the output pulse interleaver combined. The row route can afford only 1.39 dB more. [Row waveform simulation](#a-simulated-read-through-a-chirped-fiber-bragg-grating) · [Paquot et al., three-channel FWM dot product](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24663554/)

**Generating the phase pattern from timing.** The phase-continuous data comb repeats every T = 20 ps. For the guarded design, the first weight packet starts at 0 ps and the second at P = 25.3125 ps. The second therefore meets the data waveform 5.3125 ps into its next period. Measured relative to tooth 0, tooth 16 at +800 GHz has made 4.25 additional turns in 5.3125 ps, giving a 90° phase advance. Tooth 32 at +1.6 THz has made 8.5 turns, giving a 180° advance.

This phase difference holds throughout the packet. At any local time u after the start of weight packet m, input tooth j has its usual waveform `xⱼ exp(2πiju/T)` multiplied by `exp(2πij τₘ/T)`, where `τₘ = mP mod T`. Express the stored weight comb in the same packet coordinates. The FWM read conjugates the input phase, and the output interleaver overlays the beginnings of all partial packets so the phase factors are retained when they are added. For example, input 0 → output 32 and input 32 → output 0 both use weight tooth 32. With the first two packets carrying that tooth at fields a and b, one coupling is proportional to a + b and the other to a − b. [Spectral phase and optical delay](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.25.016427) · [Dual-pump FWM](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-018-31470-x)

At identical data and weight timing, a common phase on all teeth in each slot would leave the sum Hankel, with just 127 independent coefficients. The timing scheme uses the conjugating FWM read with `F[m,j] = exp(−2πirmj/64)/8`. The advance r is 17 on the 25.3125 ps schedule and 1 on the 20.3125 ps schedule. Each schedule visits all 64 offsets. Program weight tooth s in slot m as `h[m,s] = Σⱼ conj(F[m,j]) W[s−j,j]` over valid columns and rows. The 64 Fourier sequences are orthogonal, so `Σₘ h[m,i+j] F[m,j] = W[i,j]` for every entry. This distinguishes all entries on the same Hankel anti-diagonal and requires complex weight fields even for a real matrix. Set the six output delays to multiples 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 and 32 of P. All 64 output coordinates then emerge together in one 20 ps packet. A sampled instantaneous-FWM check, including the whole generated idler family, recovered the target waveform to 1.9 × 10⁻¹⁴ relative error at the 20.3125 ps pitch. Unwanted teeth in this family cancel in the collected window under flat coupling. Other FWM families still require band isolation. [Field and waveform checks](fwm_multiplier_choice.py)

**Changing a common optical phase every pass.** With aligned packets and the same output sum on every tooth, a phase shifter on the data input gives `W[i,j] = Σₘ exp(−iφₘ) h[m,i+j]`.
