The minus sign comes from conjugation in the FWM read. For example, the couplings from input 0 to output 1 and from input 1 to output 0 always receive the same coefficient. A whole-comb shifter can align partial results or correct the signs of successive input copies. Independent entries along the central anti-diagonal require 64 linearly independent phase or amplitude patterns across the input teeth, with good conditioning for a useful power budget. One actuator could produce the Fourier patterns if it controls the relative phase between adjacent teeth. The first pattern after the uniform one advances by 5.625° per tooth. On a 50 GHz grid this corresponds to 0.3125 ps of group delay. For scale, a nondispersive optical-path change giving π at 1550 nm gives 0.0465° between adjacent teeth. For an actual device, use its measured spectral phase at each setting and its transfer on a finite packet. [Teleanu et al., spectral phase slope and optical delay](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.25.016427)

The timing scheme depends on continuity across data periods. A 5% sinusoidal field-amplitude ripple gave 3.54% coherent waveform error after fitting one gain. A 1 ps raised-cosine rise and fall on every copy gave 19.7% median error across ten dense targets. Both tests use equal input energy and the original weights. With 0.3125 ps between weight windows, switching is demanding. A 25.3125 ps pitch gives 5.3125 ps of guard by advancing 17/64 periods per slot. Since 17 is coprime to 64 this simply permutes the Fourier codes. It requires 81 input periods, a 1.62 ns frame and 0.96 dB more input sharing. The wider guard relaxes the switching requirement. The relative phase and delay stability across the comb still need validation. A device model needs the complex field leaving the input pulse extender, weight-packet edge shapes, residual clock and carrier-phase error, dispersion, and the pump light consumed during guards.

**Experimental timing precedents.** The 20 and 20.3125 ps periods correspond to 50.000 and 49.231 GHz. The 0.3125 ps advance is set by the difference between the periods. The gap between consecutive 20 ps weight windows happens to have the same duration. The two requirements concern different components.

- Dutt et al. demonstrated repeated relative timing sweeps on a chip with two soliton combs near 450 GHz, a 1.12 GHz repetition-rate difference and a measured interferogram period near 0.90 ns. [Dutt et al., Fig. 3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5834308/#F3)
- Li et al. demonstrated optical recurrent classification at a 50 GHz clock and reported real-time optical input generation up to about 48 GHz. Their pulses were at most about 5 ps wide for the tests with electro-optic input signals. [Li et al., Fig. 3 and Methods](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13379394/)
- Qiu et al. demonstrated six-stage, 64-fold pulse replication at 14 GHz. The short-arm delay characterization had 0.4 ps resolution. Wang et al. measured 1.7 fs integrated timing jitter from 10 kHz to 1 MHz for a single 89 GHz microcomb. Those figures describe delay metrology and source jitter, respectively. [Qiu et al., Figs. 2–3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12144144/) · [Wang et al.](https://doi.org/10.1117/1.APN.4.3.036011)

The 64-slot FWM read with individually programmed 20 ps weight packets and coherent whole-comb collection remains a simulation design point. After removing common carrier phase at the comb center, a residual relative delay of 10 fs produces 0.099 rad at either edge of the 3.15 THz data comb. That budget applies to the paths from the data and weight sources through the FWM guide and output interleaver. The 5.3125 ps gap improves switching margin. The relative delay and dispersion still need to be characterized across the comb.

##### Whole-comb timing for the adjoint and weight update

Use the guarded design point with a 20 ps data period T and a 25.3125 ps weight spacing P. One input vector x occupies the whole 81-period frame. The data field repeats phase-continuously from 0 to 1,620 ps. The second weight window, from 25.3125 to 45.3125 ps, overlaps the tail of one data period and the start of the next. Both periods carry x. A single 20 ps input overlaps only the first weight packet. An extender that supplies only 64 contiguous periods gives 50 full interactions, one partial interaction and 13 missed weight packets. The 5.3125 ps guard is on the weight path.

Use one FWM guide for three passes in sequence with a phase-locked reference R and routing by wavelength band. Keep the 64 stored weight packets h in their original order. For the real linear delta rule, x is the key and the signed residual is e = v − Wx. The target v and prediction use the same output band. A phase-aligned coupler can subtract them after matching their amplitudes and arrival times.

| Operation | What the optics do |
| --- | --- |
| Forward, Wx | Mix the 81-period data burst with each stored weight packet and R. Select the output band. The six-stage output interleaver aligns and adds the 64 partials on clock P. |
| Input gradient, Wᵀe | Send 64 copies of e on clock P, in sync with the same old weights and R. Signal and idler bands exchange roles, so select the original input band. Use whole-comb delays to overlap and add the 81 slices of length T. Splitting the frame at these boundaries gives each contributing waveform the inverse shift relative to its forward sampling. |
| Weight update, ΔW = μexᵀ | Hold or regenerate the saved 81-period burst from x. Mix it with the same train of e and R. The FWM term x·e·conj(R) produces 127 weight-tooth increments in each slot. Route that band to a write coupler to add each increment packet to the corresponding circulating packet. The old weight train bypasses the guide during the write. |

The same frequency relation applies in all three passes. Forward generates the output from `f_h + f_R − f_x`. Backpropagation generates the input band from `f_h + f_R − f_y`. The write generates the weight band from `f_x + f_y − f_R`. In the shared co-propagating guide the linear phase mismatch reverses sign for the write. Its conversion gain and parasitic mixing still need their own model. [Dual-pump FWM field equations](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-018-31470-x)

At the input-gradient collector, the waveform from the second weight slot is split by the 40 ps data-clock boundary. Its final 5.3125 ps goes at the beginning of the collected period and the first 14.6875 ps goes after it. With the same folding for all slots, the inverse shifts give Wᵀe. A switched reverse path through a suitable 81-period input extender can serve as this collector. It must equalize the complex tap coefficients. The 64 residual copies can use the output interleaver in reverse on clock P with calibrated slot phases. Both routes must bypass any one-way amplification when used in reverse. A single linear delta-rule memory write needs the residual but can skip the 81-slice collection.

Index the weight slots m and data teeth j from zero. Let `F[m,j] = exp(−2πi·17mj/64)/8` be the forward code. At weight tooth s, the write applies `Δh[m,s] = μ Σᵢ₊ⱼ₌ₛ conj(F[m,j]) eᵢ xⱼ` for real fields. Each slot retains its own write packet. The forward read uses the orthogonality of the 64 phase patterns to turn these increments into the dense update. For complex data the input band encodes conj(x), the returned field encodes conj(W†e), and the write gives μex†. Sampling the waveforms gave relative field errors below 3 × 10⁻¹⁴ for the forward read, adjoint, stored increment and an independent post-write query at both proposed pitches. The tests use ideal tap phases, flat conversion and a calibrated write scale. A physical budget must include injection loss on the old weights, useful write gain and noise, the saved-input delay and coherent memory retention. [Reproduce the three optical passes](fwm_multiplier_choice.py)

Timing supplies the fixed phase pattern. A dense 64 × 64 matrix still carries 4,096 independent values across the 64 stored weight packets. A recurrent memory can start dark and accumulate optical writes. Loading arbitrary pretrained weights through a shared optical interface remains a separate design requirement. Algebraically, 64 writes from zero suffice if the source can supply the matrix columns as value combs and the corresponding one-tooth basis keys. That construction shifts the engineering question to how the source prepares those columns.

##### Why the staggered timing cell was ruled out

The guarded design needs coherent collection on both delay grids to propagate gradients. The forward interleaver can run in reverse to copy the residual onto the 25.3125 ps weight clock. Backpropagation still needs the 20 ps grid to apply the required cyclic shifts to the returned field. A reversible input pulse extender would share the 20 ps grid between forward extension and gradient collection and require reverse routing and phase calibration. The simple-backprop requirement rules out this design for the trainable cell. The ideal local delta-rule write can be evaluated independently of the input gradient.

| Feed-forward delay grid | Differential delay span | Equivalent waveguide length at group index 2 |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| 64 forward slots spaced by 25.3125 ps | 63 × 25.3125 ps = 1,594.7 ps | 23.9 cm |
| 81 backward slices spaced by 20 ps | 80 × 20 ps = 1,600 ps | 24.0 cm |
| Combined | 3,194.7 ps | **47.9 cm** |

These are differential path spans. A six-stage binary forward interleaver installs 23.9 cm of differential delay across its stages. A feed-forward backward collector has a 24.0 cm span, with installed length depending on its topology. As an area proxy, Liu et al. packed 0.50 m of SiN waveguide into a 3.1 mm² spiral. At that packing density, the combined span alone corresponds to about 3 mm² of spirals, before switch, coupler and access routing area. Lei et al.'s roughly 2 cm AlGaAs FWM converter occupies about 0.18 mm². The delay grids dominate this comparison of optical routing with the nonlinear section. Amplifiers, drivers and packaging need their own area budget. [Liu et al., Fig. 4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-021-21973-z.pdf) · [Lei et al., device dimensions](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447)

##### Coding the Hankel sum in one shared interleaver

The input band A and output band B share six long delay arms. In arm b, both bands traverse the delay of 2ᵇP. A coarse band splitter and recombiner give the entire B band an additional 2ᵇδ. Use T = 20 ps, δ = T/64 = 0.3125 ps and an illustrative slot pitch P = 45 ps. The six extra delays total 19.6875 ps. At the external B port, a reciprocal two-path interferometer with differential delay T adds the field to a delayed copy, with a field coefficient of 1/2 on each copy at the selected port. The A port connects directly. At the other end, route the two bands between the interleaver and the co-propagating FWM guide as required by each operation. The weights and reference have their own ports. This candidate chooses the reciprocal interleaver as its input pulse extender. Qiu et al. demonstrated a six-stage interleaver and 64-fold replication to 14 GHz. Their device establishes the base topology. The band-selective delays, coherent two-period operation and three FWM passes here are design assumptions. [Qiu et al.](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z)

**What the two diamonds in a long arm do.** A and B each encode a full 64-component vector on their own optical frequency band. One physical implementation uses a passive wavelength diplexer with a cutoff in the guard band. In each long arm the first diplexer sends the whole A band through a bypass and the whole B band through an extra waveguide length. A second diplexer recombines them. These reciprocal devices exchange roles on a return traversal. The 50:50 stage coupler separately sends a copy of both bands through the short arm. With equal group indices of 2, the stage-0 B detour is nominally 46.8 µm for 0.3125 ps. Physical lengths must deliver the target long-minus-short delays for both bands including waveguide dispersion and the diplexers’ group delays. Single-cutoff silicon diplexers have been demonstrated near 1550 nm. [Measured silicon dichroic splitter](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-018-05287-1)

The 50 GHz cell specifies tooth spacing and leaves the absolute band centers open. The 64 useful centers span 3.15 THz per band. In either read, the first-order raw idler indices span −63 through 126, or 9.45 THz between outer centers. If a single fixed cutoff routes both full raw families as in the ideal model, their centers must be separated by more than 9.45 THz, with room for the transition and occupied pulse sidebands. A narrower return passband requires a finite-packet error check. The reference and weight comb use separate ports at the Kerr guide. [Raw-idler bandwidth model](hankel_idler_bandwidth.py)

The connected layout uses balanced 2 × 2 electro-optic Mach–Zehnder interferometers (MZIs) as the A/B port switches at both ends. Two 3 dB couplers and a driven differential phase shifter select the bar or cross route for a whole band. One common-side port is terminated for 1 × 2 use. The port switches hold their state throughout a 64-slot operation, and transitions between operations need a settling allowance. The B-only two-copy device has a fixed 20 ps arm imbalance and passes both replicas. Wang et al. measured a broadband lithium-niobate MZI switch. Loss, extinction and group delay over the cell’s assigned bands are still design parameters. [Connected layout](../figures/shared_interleaver_multiplier_layout.svg) · [Measured 2 × 2 MZI switch](https://doi.org/10.1364/AO.56.008164)

| Operation | Route and timing |
| --- | --- |
| Forward | Launch x on A. Its 64 copies reach the old weights every P. Return the resulting B packets to the interleaver. The path from slot m into the selected sum traverses the equivalent of q = 63−m base delays. After nominal alignment it is qδ late. Gate one period starting at 63P+T. Two coherent copies give the full waveform shifted cyclically by qδ. |
| Adjoint | Launch the residual e on B, advancing its launch by the same 63δ for every frame. Its m-th copy takes m(P+δ). The 20 ps weight window at mP sees the residual advanced by (63−m)δ within its period. This supplies the conjugate of the forward code. Mix with the old weights in their ordinary order, route the generated A packets back through the same interleaver, and collect the ordinary sum at 63P. |
| Write | Launch the saved x on A and e on B with those same timings. The term at f_A+f_B−f_R convolves the input and coded error onto all 127 weight teeth in each slot. Add that single packet to the corresponding weight packet. The old bank bypasses the guide during the write. |

Common transit times are omitted. Real, calibrated slot signs from the couplers are absorbed in the reference phase for each operation. One optical band carries conjugated logical input coefficients for complex arithmetic. With output index r and the 1/8 ideal sum normalization, the code is C[m,r] = exp[−2πi(63−m)r/64]/8 and W[r,j] = Σₘ C[m,r]h[m,r+j]. Store h[m,s] = Σᵣ C[m,r]* W[r,s−r] over valid r. Orthogonality gives arbitrary dense W and the physical write of the coded residual times the saved physical input gives ΔW = η e x†. The corresponding input gradient is W†e. The FWM guide is visited once per slot per operation. A common scalar on a weight tooth s treats all r+j=s alike. With a shared matched read and write, nonnegative amplitude masks would need disjoint supports to have orthogonal columns. At 64 slots the resulting single-tooth selection passes 1/64 of a uniform comb's power in a given slot.

**Area and energy.** The common differential length is 42.5 cm at group index 2, the B additions total 2.95 mm, and the separate one-period interferometer adds 3.00 mm. The passive differential paths total about 43.1 cm, versus 47.9 cm for the earlier two grids. The 64-slot read occupies 2.88 ns before common flight, memory access and switching between operations. Count twelve coarse band devices for the split-and-recombine implementation, end routing, optical phase trims, access guides and pump rejection in a layout estimate. The one-port two-period interferometer costs 6.02 dB in the selected mode. The modeled fixed port coupler at the guide end costs 3.01 dB on extension and 3.01 dB on collection. Those terms together reduce useful power 12.04 dB relative to ideal extension, ideal coding and ideal temporal collection. With the earlier η_FWM = 0.468 extrapolation, the single-lit-tooth identity read gives −33.4 dB before chip insertion losses or optical gain. Using timed routing to recover the two port-splitting terms requires routing both bands with the right schedules. The 6.02 dB copy term still applies.

**Time for one forward 64 × 64 matrix–vector product.** With resident weights and an input synchronized to slot 0, the modeled output gate opens at 63 × 45 ps + 20 ps = 2.855 ns and the full 64-tooth packet is available at 2.875 ns, relative to launch with common flight removed. The frame period is 2.880 ns. Input copy m takes m base delays on launch and its selected return takes 63−m, so extension and collection overlap. Each contribution traverses the 2.12 cm FWM guide once. Taking group index 4.04 from a comparable AlGaAs device adds 0.286 ns, giving 3.16 ns to the end of the packet plus the remaining transit through the short arms, switches, filters, gain devices and interconnects. Actual input-to-output latency requires those routed group delays. [FWM waveguide length](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [AlGaAs ring radius and free spectral range used to estimate group index](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-020-15005-5)

**Footprint of one complete 64 × 64 shared-interleaver cell.** Budget approximately **32 mm² of photonic component and routing area** for the forward, adjoint and write paths in the [connected schematic](../figures/shared_interleaver_multiplier_layout.svg). A **9 × 4 mm** rectangle is a starting allocation for hybrid integration. The sum combines SiN delays, electro-optic control sections, an AlGaAs FWM spiral and gain dies. Physical placement, chiplet clearances, fiber attachment and electrical escape routing remain open.

| Part of the connected cell | Allocated area | Basis |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| One shared six-stage interleaver with seven couplers | 14.45 mm² | Qiu et al. built an 8.5 × 1.7 mm SiN interleaver. Their longest delay was 2.31 ns, compared with 1.44 ns here. The allocation carries their entire device area. [Qiu et al.](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z) |
| Five MZI selectors | 5.0 mm² | Four A/B end switches and one load/write selector. Allow 4 mm × 0.25 mm each. The electrode length has experimental precedent and the lane width is assumed. [Thin-film lithium-niobate buffer switch](https://doi.org/10.1002/qute.202300195) |
| Four gates | 4.5 mm² | Allow 1.25 mm² each for timed readout and write, and 1.0 mm² each for the A/B read-selection shutters. A measured 5 mm modulator supplies the length reference for a fast gate. [Wang et al.](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-018-0551-y) |
| Reference slot phase, weight tap, write scale and phase, loop phase trim | 2.5 mm² | Combined design allowance for the remaining active optical controls. |
| Additional passive routing | 3.0 mm² | Design allowance for twelve arm diplexers and their detours, B pulse copier, end combiners, pump/weight injection and rejection, and local interconnect. |
| One 2.12 cm FWM waveguide | 0.28 mm² | Allow 0.70 × 0.40 mm around the approximately 0.60 × 0.30 mm spiral reported for the roughly 2 cm device. [Lei et al.](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) |
| Two read amplifiers and one memory-loop amplifier | 2.25 mm² | Three 1.50 × 0.50 mm bare SOA dies as size proxies. [SemiNex die dimensions](https://mrp.seminex.com/PI_Sheet.aspx?seminex_part_pk=2229) |
| **Total** | **31.98 mm²** | **29.73 mm²** with all three gain sites moved off the photonic assembly, before fiber coupling to those sites. |

The 43.1 cm of differential guide calculated above would occupy about 2.7 mm² at Liu et al.’s measured density of 3.1 mm² per 0.50 m of SiN spiral. Six separate routed stages require additional area. Scaling Qiu et al.’s entire interleaver in proportion to the longest optical delay would give 9.0 mm² for the base network and 26.5 mm² overall. Couplers, bend clearances and pads have fixed dimensions, so retain the 32 mm² case for planning. [Liu et al., Fig. 4](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-021-21973-z) · [Qiu et al., Figs. 1–2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z)

The main uncertainty is the gain hardware. The reference SOA lists an 85 nm 3 dB spectral width and 30 dB gain at 10 µW input. The full raw idler family in **each** direction spans 9.45 THz. Covering either family with G₁ and reaching the loaded G₂ output power may require separate band-specific devices or external amplifiers. The budget also excludes electronic drivers, pump and comb sources, thermal packaging and the off-chip fiber. The 2.88 ns of round-trip delay for one 64-slot weight frame corresponds to 0.59 m of fiber at group index 1.468. Chip and component transit times count toward that delay. Guard slots and additional stored frames increase it. The archived 150 m TTT memory is a separate system-level choice. [SemiNex specifications](https://mrp.seminex.com/PI_Sheet.aspx?seminex_part_pk=2229)

**Electrical energy for a forward read with resident weights.** Count 4,096 real-equivalent MACs or 8,192 operations per 64 × 64 vector. Exclude input generation, weight encoding and initial loading. Include continuous reference delivery, replenishment of tapped weights, read gain and the two fast drives. One component-based scenario uses the following electrical powers.

| Function | Per-cell electrical power | Assumption |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Raw-idler G₁, output G₂ and weight-loop gain | 3.00 W | Three continuously biased SOAs at 2 V × 0.5 A each. The loop replaces extracted light and compensates propagation and injection losses. Operating current at the required gain, pulse loading and bandwidth is unverified for each site. [SemiNex specifications](https://mrp.seminex.com/PI_Sheet.aspx?seminex_part_pk=2229) |
| Continuous FWM reference | 1.26 W | A 1550 nm laser at 2 V × 0.63 A delivers 100 mW at the fiber pigtail. Assume 3 dB total delivery loss to supply 50 mW inside one guide. Splitting a shared source requires allocating the delivered power to each active cell. [QPhotonics laser specifications](https://www.qphotonics.com/catalog/Wavelength-stabilized-single-mode-fiber-coupled-laser-diode-100m-p-956.html) |
| Reference phase and final gate drives | 0.82 W | Allow 0.41 W for each of two dedicated channels. Yong et al. measured this power for one 44 Gb/s driver and modulator. The gate waveform and the power of a shared clock network need separate characterization. Forward routing switches hold their settings. [Yong et al.](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.25.006112) |

The 5.08 W subtotal gives 16.05 nJ/vector, 3.92 pJ/MAC and 0.51 TOPS/W at a serial 3.16 ns interval. At the back-to-back 2.88 ns frame pitch it gives 14.63 nJ/vector, 3.57 pJ/MAC and 0.56 TOPS/W. Each calculation assumes full utilization at that interval. Add the electrical cost of cooling, supplies, clock distribution, held biases and stabilization to obtain wall power. This is a component power scenario for nominal analog operations. Useful accuracy and actual gain under load require a separate check. At the serial cadence, 10 TOPS/W would require total attributable cell power of 259 mW.

At the guide the CW reference delivers 144 pJ per 2.88 ns frame, of which 64 pJ overlap the 64 × 20 ps weight windows. At a full 50 mW weight setting another 64 pJ reach the guide from the loop. Counting the reference alone would give 56.9 TOPS/W of delivered reference light. Both optical energy flows are already paid for by the reference and loop gain lines in the electrical budget. Smaller SOA bias currents and shared reference-phase electronics can be inserted in the table once characterized.

##### Optimistic electrical budget and electronic CIM comparison

For a resident, forward-only 64 × 64 read, **5.4 nominal TOPS/W at a 3.16 ns result interval** is an aggressive design target with a continuous 50 mW FWM reference. The corresponding power is 0.480 W and energy is 1.516 nJ per vector (370 fJ per real-equivalent MAC). Assume full utilization, low-leakage held switch states, and uncooled operation. The numerical example uses the phase-coded orthogonal matrix bank with one pump normalization of 1.1576. [Power calculation and sensitivity cases](hankel_power_budget.py) · [Matrix and pulse normalization](hankel_phase_recovery.py)

| Function | Allocation at the 2.88 ns frame rate | Physical basis and outstanding assumption |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Continuous reference, delivered to FWM | 125 mW | 50% laser efficiency × 80% delivery. Xue et al. measured over 50% peak conversion in a 1.55 µm DFB at low current. Maintaining 40% electrical-to-guide efficiency at the chosen current and power is a target. Count power delivered to every active lane even if the source is shared. [Xue et al.](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.optcom.2025.132564) |
| Raw-idler gain G₁, continuous | 60 mW | 40 mA at an assumed 1.5 V. Keyvaninia et al. measured 13 dB peak gain and 70 nm FWHM in different devices. The cell requires about +10 dB over one 9.45 THz forward idler family, about 76 nm near 1550 nm, with controlled gain ripple, phase and noise. Reithmaier et al. obtained at least 10 dB over 120 nm at 300 mA. Using that current and 1.5 V gives the higher-current sensitivity case. An adjoint at another band may need separate gain hardware. [Keyvaninia et al.](https://www.photonics.intec.ugent.be/download/pub_2989.pdf) · [Reithmaier et al., Fig. 24](https://iris.polito.it/retrieve/e384c42d-effd-d4b2-e053-9f05fe0a1d67/1400872-1.pdf) |
| Weight-loop gain, continuous | 175 mW | Orthogonality of the slot code and a matrix Frobenius norm of 8 give 64/1.1576² = 47.76 pJ delivered per read (16.58 mW optical). Assume 0.5 dB from the tap to FWM, 25% power extraction and 0.2 dB other round-trip loss. Before the tap, the amplifier replaces the extracted power times [1 + (1−f)(1−t)/f], with f the tap fraction and t the remaining loop transmission. Cheung et al. measured 12.1% electrical-to-added-light efficiency under steady single-wavelength loading. Extending it to the circulating pulse train, the 127-tooth band and repeated reads requires a saturation, accumulated-noise and phase-error budget. [Cheung et al.](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.23.022431) |
| Final gain G₂, biased around each result | 98 mW | A 150 mA, 1.5 V design point for ~30–31 dB gain, with one ns of charge-equivalent current per read including turn-on and overdrive, and 80% efficient pulsed drive: 281 pJ/read. Fraunhofer reports a C-band small-signal gain curve. Separate SOA gate experiments show sub-ns-class switching. Their combination must deliver the full 2.52 pJ output packet over 20 ps without gain compression, phase distortion, excess noise or timing slip. A continuous 225 mW device bias is the fallback. [Fraunhofer gain curves](https://www.hhi.fraunhofer.de/fileadmin/PDF/PC/LAS/InP-Semiconductor_Optical_Amplifiers_.pdf) · [SOA switching measurements](https://doi.org/10.1590/2179-10742018v17i41346) |
| Output gate and controls | 7.5 mW | A single 2.3 V, 20 ps gate pulse into 50 Ω is 2.12 pJ at the load and 7.05 pJ at an assumed 30% driver efficiency. Allow a further 5 mW per cell for shared clock distribution, bias holding, idle drivers and trims. The measured device reference is a 10 mm, 80 GHz modulator. Flat transmission, dark-state extinction across the band, pulse shape and thermal stabilization need testing. [Wang et al.](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-018-0551-y) |
| **Sum including 95% DC distribution efficiency** | **489 mW** | Each pulsed component is charged once per output. Add any active cooling, higher static driver draw, extra gain needed for end-router loss or pump drift, and light-source locking beyond the trim allowance. |

**Removing read-time reference modulation.** For real coupler signs let sₘ = sign(tₘ t₆₃₋ₘ), where tₘ is the fixed launch tap amplitude. Store sₘhₘ at initialization and add sₘΔhₘ on writes. The forward and adjoint now use a constant reference. The product with the stored packet is exactly the original programmed read field. The finite-packet check gives relative waveform errors below 3 × 10⁻¹⁴ for both reads, the signed increment and the post-write reads. This removes the per-slot reference modulation drive from the read budget. The write operation still needs the slot signs. [Numerical checks](hankel_comb_codes.py)

| Sensitivity case | TOPS/W at 3.16 ns | TOPS/W at 2.88 ns |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| G₁ at 60 mW, G₂ continuously biased, continuous reference | 4.16 | 4.56 |
| G₁ at 60 mW, G₂ biased for each output, continuous reference | **5.40** | **5.82** |
| Brighter 3.5–4 pJ input, allocate 50 pJ/read extra to G₂ drive for loaded output | **5.22** | **5.61** |
| G₁ at 450 mW with the same output timing | 2.91 | 3.16 |
| Full nominal 64 pJ of weight light per read, G₁ at 60 mW | 4.78 | 5.16 |
| Efficiently generated pulsed reference, G₁ at 60 mW and packet-biased G₂ | 6.29 | 6.65 |

The pulsed-reference row is a change to the source. It assumes 50 mW throughout all 64 weight windows, energy equivalent to a 24 ps flat pulse per 20 ps slot, and the same 40% electrical-to-guide efficiency including pulse formation. Leaving a CW laser on and blanking its output during gaps does not save its supply energy. All rows assume that available gain preserves the original optical budgets. The 3.16 ns interval omits remaining common flight times. It also assumes aligned weight access. Lower utilization reduces the return on all continuous loads.

**Measured electronic comparators.** At 2.59 TOPS the power ceilings for a twofold advantage are 523 mW against IBM's 2.48 TOPS/W four-phase PCM mode, 133 mW against its 9.76 TOPS/W one-phase mode, 81 mW against 4-bit NeuRRAM at 16 TOPS/W, and 40 mW against Chen et al.’s 4-bit 32.5 TOPS/W dynamic analog RAM system with sparsity savings disabled. Chen et al.’s corresponding macro reached 102.2 TOPS/W. The 50 mW reference delivered into the present guide alone exceeds the last system's twofold threshold. Published electronic figures are measured at their array sizes and include their reported conversion and peripheral costs. The optical tally assumes operands available optically and passes its result to another optical stage. A device-to-device claim requires matching the scope of the energy measurement, achieved precision, dimensions and utilization. [IBM PCM chip](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41928-023-01010-1) · [NeuRRAM, Extended Data Tables 1–2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-022-04992-8) · [Chen et al., Fig. 15.3.7](https://users.eecs.northwestern.edu/~jgu/nu-vlsi/CIM_ISSCC21.pdf)

One useful acceptance test follows from the IBM four-phase comparison. Twice 2.48 TOPS/W allows 1.652 nJ per vector, leaving 136 pJ beyond the packet-biased estimate at 3.16 ns. Holding the other components at their table values, G₁ must stay below about **101 mW at the die** for that target. Alternatively, with G₁ at 60 mW, the G₂ pulser can consume at most **410 pJ per read** on its supply rail before the common DC loss. These limits draw on the same headroom. A combined increase or active cooling spends that headroom once.

**Accuracy at the comparison workload.** IBM measured 11.9% normalized error on four-quadrant four-phase reads with 30% zero weights and 10% zero inputs. A 64 × 64 optical adaptation uses 16 signed-uniform matrices with Frobenius norm 8, four unit-norm inputs each, 2 pJ per input, one fixed pump scale (1.17475) and analytic gain. On the 64 useful teeth the pooled complex deterministic error was 5.51%. Combining it in quadrature with 13.25% zero-mean FWM and amplifier noise from the separate weak noise model yields an estimated 14.35% complex-field error. The same calculation gives 10.32% on the real quadrature alone. The present optical cascade carries both quadratures. The optical model omits memory noise, loaded SOA compression, finite gain spectra and excess timing jitter. The IBM array was 256 × 256 and its exact Fig. 5 input scaling and aggregation are unspecified. Sweeping the input energy with the same weights and reference gives total complex-field estimates of 12.04% at 3 pJ, 11.28% at 3.5 pJ and 10.66% at 4 pJ. The model scales seeded field amplitude and leaves the absolute weak-model noise covariances fixed. At 4 pJ, the projection combines 5.09% deterministic and 9.37% stochastic error. A unit-norm target output at +1 dB requires 5.04 pJ from G₂, and the largest target in this sample is 7.25 pJ in 20 ps (363 mW averaged over its window). These energies are required from the gain device before accounting for compression.

For the brighter-output case allow a further **50 pJ per result on the G₂ driver supply**, raising its budget from 281 to 331 pJ per read. This is a design allowance, not a measured marginal efficiency. It corresponds to about 5% conversion of the increment to extra useful light for a unit-output vector at 4 pJ. At 3.16 ns the full allocation is 0.496 W, 1.569 nJ/vector and **5.22 nominal TOPS/W**. It leaves approximately 79 pJ on the internal supply rails, or about **26 mW additional at the power input**, before a twofold advantage over IBM four-phase disappears. G₁ could consume at most 85 mW with this larger G₂ allocation, if every other assumption holds. A 450 mW G₁ instead gives 2.86 TOPS/W at the same 4 pJ comparison point. The 1.1576 bank scale used in the power budget slightly overprovisions loop replenishment for this sparse sample. The hardware acceptance test is delivery of the loaded complex packet at the quoted error and energy; the combined device and matched-accuracy superiority remain unverified. [IBM Fig. 5](https://doi.org/10.1038/s44335-024-00010-4) · [Reproduce the 64 × 64 adaptation](hankel_sparse_read.py)

##### Commercial GPU comparison for batch-one inference

IBM's PCM chip tests the advantage of storing weights at the multiplier. A commercial GPU tests the architecture against a complete accelerator that can keep a small matrix on chip, process heads in parallel and fuse recurrent reads and writes. For both comparisons count one real MAC as two operations. The brighter optical forward read projects 2.59 TOPS at full serial utilization and 5.22 nominal TOPS/W, with weights and inputs already optical. The optical precision estimate is 10.66% relative complex-field RMS error on the sampled workload. No equivalence to a digital format has been demonstrated. [IBM chip and both read modes](https://research.ibm.com/publications/a-64-core-mixed-signal-in-memory-compute-chip-based-on-phase-change-memory-for-deep-neural-network-inference)

| Sustained dense GPU workload | Measured throughput per GPU | Measured GPU power | TOPS/W | Projected optical TOPS/W divided by GPU TOPS/W |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| B200 INT8 | 2,931 TOPS | 991 W | 2.96 | 1.76 |
| B200 FP8 | 2,524 TFLOP/s | 994 W | 2.54 | 2.06 |
| B200 NVFP4 | 5,708 TFLOP/s | 993 W | 5.75 | 0.91 |
| B300 NVFP4 | 6,877 TFLOP/s | 1,091 W | 6.30 | 0.83 |

MIT ORCD measured tuned cuBLASLt matrix–matrix multiplies in 60-second runs and reported power for the same workloads. These rows give an arithmetic throughput comparison at high GPU utilization. At the cell's 3.16 ns serial interval, matching the B200 NVFP4 result would require at most 451 mW total cell power, compared with the projected 496 mW. A twofold advantage over B200 INT8 would require 438 mW. [MIT ORCD measurements, §4](https://github.com/mit-orcd/orcd-benchmarks/blob/main/b300-nodes/COMPARISON-b200-vs-b300.md#4-cublaslt-gemm--per-gpu-throughput-incl-fp4-tflops)

For single-stream decode the frequency of off-chip reads matters. Antepara et al. fit a GH200 high-bandwidth memory (HBM) traffic cost of 11.68 pJ/bit to hardware measurements with randomized data. Reading 4,096 packed INT8 coefficients from HBM once would contribute about 383 nJ. A 64 × 64 INT8 matrix occupies only 4 KiB and can remain on chip. Apply the coefficient to bytes that actually cross the HBM interface after caching and batching. On the optical side the 1.569 nJ read budget already charges loop replenishment at the modeled utilization. Expanding to multiple stored frames also requires budgeting circulation between uses, waiting for the appropriate frame, and writes. [Antepara et al., Table 3](https://escholarship.org/content/qt6189368s/qt6189368s_noSplash_8589457fdac04188de748390daac1128.pdf)

For a recurrent layer comparison, NVIDIA measured a **4.90 µs median fused Gated DeltaNet decode kernel** on a GB200 for one request, with 48 value heads of 128 × 128 FP32 state each. It includes the gated delta update, convolution and output normalization after the QKVZ projection. The publication gives kernel duration without energy per call. A twofold latency advantage on that workload would require an optical implementation of the same operations in **2.45 µs** including scheduling, routing, state reads and writes. The 3.16 ns figure applies to one 64 × 64 forward read before remaining common flight time. A complete-model comparison should also report energy per generated token and language-model quality under the modeled optical noise. [NVIDIA benchmark geometry and timing](https://docs.nvidia.com/cudnn/latest/operations/LinearAttention/gdn_decode_flashinfer_backend#performance)

##### Improving one fully programmable 64 × 64 read

A read takes one normalized real input x to one vector Wx. Each matrix has 4,096 independent real coefficients and the budget counts 4,096 MACs per result. All scenarios include electrical supply for both pumps (including weight-loop replenishment), raw-idler and final gain, controls and DC losses. The source boundary assumes the data and weights already exist optically. The calculation uses the single-input serial interval and holds the final gain stage's 331 pJ driver allowance fixed. The baseline allocates about 142 fJ/MAC to the stored weight beam, 102 to the reference, 49 to raw-idler gain, 85 to output gain, and 6 to control (rounded independently).

| One vector, one matrix | Reference / nominal weight at the guide | Serial interval | Electrical energy/read | fJ/MAC | Relative complex-field RMS |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Current cell with a continuous reference | 50 / 50 mW | 3.16 ns | 1.569 nJ | 383 | 10.81% |
| Reference emitted during the slots, same in-guide powers | 50 / 50 mW | 3.16 ns | 1.355 nJ | 331 | 10.81% |
| Emitted pulses, pumps rebalanced | 84.7 / 29.5 mW | 3.16 ns | 1.257 nJ | 307 | 10.87% |
| Alternative with 2× CW reference buildup and rebalanced pumps | 83.6 / 29.9 mW | 3.16 ns | 1.267 nJ | 309 | 10.86% |
| Pulsed reference, 4 cm traveling-wave guide at 0.2 dB/cm, rebalanced pumps | 38.2 / 12.3 mW | 3.41 ns | 0.898 nJ | 219 | 10.57% |

The CPU test uses 16 fully populated, nonsymmetric, full-rank matrices with independent uniform entries before Frobenius normalization, and four independent input vectors each. No output from a second vector enters the efficiency figure. One bank scale (1.18781) and one analytic gain cover all 64 reads. The power calculation keeps the earlier, slightly more conservative weight-energy allowance for continuity. The solver checks the synthesized weak-field output against each dense digital product, integrates pump depletion, and adds modeled weak-field noise. Doubling the sampling and propagation resolution for one matrix checks numerical convergence. The error estimates omit phase mismatch across comb pairs, Kerr phase shifts, walk-off, memory noise and loaded amplifier compression; they do not characterize worst-case W or x. [Reproduce the single-vector sweep](hankel_single_read_energy.py)

The pulsed source supplies flat coherent light over each 20 ps multiplication window, spending the energy of 24 ps of full power per 45 ps slot at a target 40% electrical-to-guide efficiency including pulse formation. Blanking a continuously powered laser does not give that saving. At the same guide power the pulsed source beats the original CW electrical budget at efficiencies above 19.4% for the 3.16 ns serial case. Power conversion scales with the product of the two pump powers in the weak model. Increasing the cheaper reference and reducing the more costly stored weight maintains the desired transfer while reducing electrical energy. Actual pulse-to-pulse phase and residual amplitude ripple matter. The CW alternative enhances only the reference in the nonlinear region; phase-lock and other cavity costs are extra. Ye et al. demonstrated selective pump enhancement in a different, degenerate FWM converter. [Singly resonant converter](https://orbit.dtu.dk/files/404271153/046115_1_5.0239950.pdf)

For the longer guide, use fixed nonlinearity, equal distributed loss and exact phase matching. The idler field coefficient scales as exp(−αL/2)[1−exp(−αL)]/α with power attenuation coefficient α. This gives a 2.308× coefficient relative to 2.12 cm at 0.8 dB/cm. Matching the weak transfer permits reducing both pump powers. Their ratio is chosen to minimize the two pump supply terms. The extra length costs 0.253 ns at assumed group index 4.04. The depletion check treats the stronger coefficient as an effective lumped interaction. A frequency-resolved propagation model of the proposed longer guide remains necessary. Lei et al. measured 133 nm conversion bandwidth and 0.8 dB/cm propagation loss in the spiral used for the baseline. They simulated broadband conversion in lower-loss guides and cite 0.2 dB/cm measured in a microresonator. The combination in the last row is a device target. If electrical-to-guide pulse generation reaches only 20%, re-optimizing the pump power split raises the last budget to 250 fJ/MAC before a new precision check. [Lei et al., Table 1 and Fig. 4](https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447)

At 219 fJ/MAC the projected cell efficiency is about 3.1× sustained B200 INT8 (676 fJ/MAC) and 1.45× sustained B300 NVFP4 (317 fJ/MAC). These GPU figures are measured arithmetic efficiency of dense GEMM. Matched workload quality and the optical interfaces would determine a system comparison. To reach **200 fJ/MAC** with the longer guide and the other entries held fixed, the final amplifier may spend at most **256 pJ per read** on its driver rail, including the loaded output. A twofold advantage over B300 NVFP4 requires at most **159 fJ/MAC**. This would leave about **95 pJ per read** on the final amplifier rail with the same other allocations. At this operating point the reference, weight loop, raw-idler gain and controls alone consume **134 fJ/MAC**. [Commercial measurements](https://github.com/mit-orcd/orcd-benchmarks/blob/main/b300-nodes/COMPARISON-b200-vs-b300.md#4-cublaslt-gemm--per-gpu-throughput-incl-fp4-tflops)

**Recover light at the interleaver port.** In the last delay stage, the first 32 replicas of one vector end on one rail, and the last 32 end on the other. The fixed 50:50 terminal coupler sends half the light to an unused port. Use a timed 2 × 2 selector to connect the occupied rail to the guide and route each returning idler to its complementary delay. Ideal routing recovers 3.01 dB on extension and another 3.01 dB on collection. The A and B bands need independently timed selectors or an equivalent band-routing implementation. The 20 ps copier remains in place. Adding 40 ps to the longest arm of both bands and delaying the second half of the weight frame by 40 ps leaves the Fourier mask intact: exactly one of a launch path and its complementary return path picks up the extra delay. Finite packets in an ideal linear model reproduce the forward product, adjoint, programmed rank-one update and post-update reads to relative error below 3 × 10⁻¹⁴. The added delay allows a 45.31 ps midframe blank interval, and reserving 40 ps for reset gives 25.31 ps at frame wrap. A 60 ps reset would make both gaps at least 45 ps. A separate packaged thin-film lithium-niobate 2 × 8 switch demonstrated 33–34 ps edges at about 3 dB total insertion loss. Full settling, extinction and insertion loss for the proposed two-band cell need a device model. [Waveform and energy checks](hankel_port_recovery.py) · [Wang et al., Fig. 3](https://academic.oup.com/nsr/article/13/19/nwag480/8758332)

With the 4 cm traveling guide, 40% pulsed-source efficiency and 4 pJ input, charge 3.493 ns of serial electrical consumption, allow 0.5 dB added loss per selector traversal and 10 pJ total for all selector drives per read. At 25.84 mW reference and 8.14 mW nominal weight power the single read uses **814.5 pJ, or 198.9 fJ/MAC, with 10.79% modeled RMS** (4.82% deterministic and 9.66% stochastic in quadrature). G₂ needs 29.76 dB. A 200 fJ/MAC ceiling allows 14.45 pJ total on the selector supply rails at that point. At 26.7 mW reference and an 8.4 mW nominal weight-power cap the result is **201 fJ/MAC, 10.46% modeled relative field RMS** on the same 16 fully populated matrices. At 30.3/9.5 mW it is **208 fJ/MAC, 9.30% RMS**. The 10 dB raw-idler amplifier and final amplifier stay in the budget; the required final gains are 29.5 and 28.4 dB respectively. These are device targets. The 10 pJ assumption requires counting both transitions and any driver draw while holding the route. At the 201 fJ point the selector supply could spend about 83 pJ per read before exhausting the saving over the 219 fJ traveling-guide case. High optical gain at useful output power and bandwidth, phase matching across occupied comb lines, realistic switch transitions and full accounting for control and interconnects are necessary before treating these figures as device performance.

**Electronic baselines for the 200 fJ/MAC cell.** Counting two operations per real MAC, 200 fJ/MAC corresponds to 10 TOPS/W. MIT's sustained dense GEMM measurements give 676 fJ/MAC for B200 INT8 and 317 fJ/MAC for B300 NVFP4. At 4,096 MACs per read, an additional 0.48 nJ per optical result would consume the margin over the B300 figure. Specialized digital silicon also reaches lower energies. Tu et al. report up to 36.5 TOPS/W at INT8 on the fabricated ReDCIM processor, or 55 fJ/MAC. A batch-one recurrent comparison needs energy and latency per token at matched model quality, with state retention, reads, writes and interfaces charged on both sides. [MIT GPU measurements](https://github.com/mit-orcd/orcd-benchmarks/blob/main/b300-nodes/COMPARISON-b200-vs-b300.md#4-cublaslt-gemm--per-gpu-throughput-incl-fp4-tflops) · [Tu et al., IEEE JSSC](https://doi.org/10.1109/JSSC.2022.3222059)

**Optical baselines for the same read.** PACE measured 2.38 TOPS/W including lasers for its 64 × 64 MVM, or 840 fJ/MAC at two operations per MAC. Luan et al. estimated 250 fJ/MAC for their 16 × 16 diffractive matrix–matrix processor with 100 time steps per detector readout, and projected 138 fJ/MAC with different modulators. Their measured 20 aJ/MAC denotes optical sensitivity. Bernstein et al. projected roughly 10 fJ/MAC including sources and electronic interfaces for a 1,000 × 1,000 free-space layer, which shares encoding and readout among many more products. Our 200 fJ/MAC is a single-vector projection with operands already optical and a restored optical output. Compare achieved error and the cost of passing results between layers before inferring an energy advantage. [PACE](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-08786-6) · [Luan et al.](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-68452-x) · [Bernstein et al.](https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.adg7904)

**Doubling single-cell throughput with a 10 ps window.** The 200 fJ/MAC baseline already uses a pulsed reference. The Fourier period T and tooth spacing obey TΔf = 1. A 10 ps rectangular gate on the existing 50 GHz comb has normalized adjacent-mode overlap 2/π. The faster design uses 100 GHz teeth, a 10 ps B copier and a B detour increment T/64 = 0.15625 ps. Use a 21.4453125 ps reference clock, weight-slot pitch and base interleaver delay. Launch 32 weight slots, leave two clock ticks dark, launch the other 32 and leave three ticks dark before the next frame. Add two ticks to the longest arm for both bands. This leaves 44.49 and 55.94 ps for the two selector transitions. The resulting 69-tick frame takes 1.47973 ns, compared with 2.960 ns for 64 × 45 ps plus the two 40 ps guards. Sustained throughput for independent vectors doubles from 2.77 to 5.54 nominal TOPS. The first full result takes 1.414 ns plus common transit, or 1.953 ns including the 4 cm guide at group index 4.04, before the remaining routing. The corresponding baseline is 3.454 ns on the same latency boundary. The ideal forward, adjoint, write and post-write checks have relative field errors below 3 × 10⁻¹⁴. The full return waveforms of three consecutive independent vectors also pass both read gates. [Reproduce with the --ten-ps option](hankel_port_recovery.py)

At the existing guide pump powers (25.84 mW reference, 8.14 mW nominal weight), the 16-matrix forward model gives 11.78% pooled relative field RMS at 4 pJ per 10 ps input and 14.48% at 2 pJ, versus 10.79% for 4 pJ at 20 ps. The 4 pJ case requires a nominal 5.04 pJ optical output, or 504 mW averaged over the 10 ps window. These figures assume flat FWM conversion and gain, ideal envelopes, linear amplifiers and unchanged matched-mode noise. The 64 useful teeth now span 6.3 THz, the 127 weight teeth 12.6 THz and the full raw idler family 18.9 THz in each direction before pulse sidebands. Verify conversion and walk-off in the 4 cm guide, amplitude and phase of the raw-idler gain across the accepted band, selector settling, output pulse saturation and amplifier recovery across successive reads. Phase-stable pulses near this clock rate and switch edges of 33–34 ps have separate precedents. Source shaping, blank pulses, drivers and regeneration need a new electrical budget. [40 GHz pulsed source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s12200-011-0142-0) · [Packaged switch](https://doi.org/10.1093/nsr/nwag480) · [AlGaAs FWM converter](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [17 THz amplifier](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-08666-z)

**Native 128 × 128 at 20 ps.** Keep 50 GHz spacing, 45 ps slots and two 40 ps guards. Adding a seventh delay stage raises the work per forward read from 4,096 to 16,384 real MACs and the period between independent reads from 2.96 to 5.84 ns (338 to 171 million vectors/s, 2.77 to 5.61 TOPS). The electrical model charges 6.373 ns for a serialized 128 read. At equal pump powers, matrix normalization and insertion losses, the useful weak-signal power transfer scales as 1/N. Scaling each pump by √2 offsets this loss when doubling N. Doubling the input packet energy preserves the mean photons per input tooth. Under these idealizations pump energy per read grows as N√N, compared with N² MACs. The additional long path, wider optical bands and brighter output limit this trend. [Size-dependent four-wave and electrical model](hankel_size_scaling.py)

At group index 2 the new differential path is 64 × 45 ps, or 43.2 cm. Each selected term traverses this length once, either before mixing or afterwards. Liu et al.'s 1 m SiN spiral measured 2.4 dB/m, giving 1.04 dB incremental propagation loss. Qiu et al.'s six-stage interleaver used 8–10 dB/m waveguides, giving 3.45–4.32 dB. The corresponding measurements do not establish loss or group delay across all the proposed bands. [Liu et al., Fig. 4](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-021-21973-z) · [Qiu et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z)

Forward reads on 16 normalized dense real matrices and four unit-norm inputs per matrix at each size, with one fixed programming scale across both banks. Error is the pooled relative field RMS with the calibrated linear gain. Each row targets +1 dB nominal signal transfer with a +10 dB raw-idler amplifier. Electrical energy covers the pulsed source, weight-loop replenishment, amplifiers, gate, selectors and previously budgeted controls. Input preparation and initial weight loading are excluded. [Run the size sweep](hankel_size_scaling.py)

| Size | Added delay loss | Input packet | Reference / nominal weight in guide | Final gain | Electrical nJ/read | fJ/MAC | Field RMS |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 64 | — | 4 pJ | 25.84 / 8.14 mW | 29.76 dB | 0.815 | 198.86 | 10.79% |
| 128 | 0 dB | 4 pJ | 25.84 / 8.14 mW | 32.77 dB | 1.202 | 73.37 | 19.87% |
| 128 | 0 dB | 8 pJ | 36.54 / 11.51 mW | 29.76 dB | 1.473 | 89.89 | 10.67% |
| 128 | 1.04 dB | 8 pJ | 41.19 / 12.97 mW | 29.76 dB | 1.545 | 94.28 | 10.70% |
| 128 | 3.45 dB | 8 pJ | 54.36 / 17.12 mW | 29.76 dB | 1.748 | 106.71 | 14.64% |
| 128 | 4.32 dB | 8 pJ | 60.09 / 18.92 mW | 29.76 dB | 1.837 | 112.11 | 17.95% |
| 128 | 3.45 dB | 16 pJ | 52.90 / 16.66 mW | 30.00 dB | 1.936 | 118.18 | 10.84% |
| 128 | 4.32 dB | 20 pJ | 52.11 / 16.41 mW | 31.00 dB | 2.029 | 123.86 | 10.81% |

The 8, 16 and 20 pJ inputs require 10.07, 20.14 and 25.18 pJ restored output at unit ideal output norm, averaging 0.50, 1.01 and 1.26 W within the 20 ps packet. Instantaneous output peaks and amplifier saturation depend on the comb waveform. The budget allocates 25 additional electrical pJ to the final amplifier drive per additional input pJ and holds the raw-idler bias at 60 mW. Compared with four smaller reads (3.258 nJ before fanout and partial summation), the 1.04 dB point has 1.71 nJ of headroom for additional electrical costs. An extra 60 mW on the raw-idler amplifier rail adds 24.57 fJ/MAC over the charged interval including DC loss. Dispersion, gain ripple, memory noise and optical updates remain outside the table.

At 128 the useful 128 teeth span 6.35 THz, the 255 weight teeth span 12.70 THz and the raw idler centers run from index −127 through 254, a span of 19.05 THz in each read direction. A separate weak-field finite-packet check used a hypothetical 10 dB raw-idler amplifier with a 17 THz flat passband, a 0.25 THz roll-off at each edge, unity transmission outside the gain band and zero phase ripple. It included the timed selector, two-copy output and full passive return. On six example reads the additional nominal-gain RMS error on the 128 useful teeth ranged from 0.028% for the phase-coded dense example to 3.17% for identity with phase-aligned input. At 2,048 and 4,096 samples per period the maximum changed from 3.14% to 3.17%. This sensitivity is separate from the gain/noise and electrical calculation. Kuznetsov et al. measured >10 dB off-chip net signal gain over 70 nm on one side of the pump and inferred a combined signal/idler span of about 17 THz by symmetry. The model’s flat single-band passband and 60 mW raw-idler bias remain assumptions. [Reproduce with --size 128 --selector --samples 4096](hankel_idler_bandwidth.py) · [Kuznetsov et al.](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-08666-z)

**Experimental precedents for 255 weight teeth at 50 GHz.** Fukuchi et al. transmitted 273 data wavelengths over 117 km of fiber with 50 GHz spacing within three separately amplified bands. Kirchner and Diddams resolved more than 600 coherent teeth at 21 GHz spacing over 13.4 THz near 965 nm and demonstrated an alternating on/off spectral mask. This exceeds the proposed weight band’s 12.70 THz span. These experiments support transport and static line addressing. [Fukuchi et al.](https://doi.org/10.1364/OFC.2001.PD24) · [Kirchner and Diddams, Fig. 7](https://tf.nist.gov/general/pdf/2460.pdf)

Kuznetsov et al., Fig. 3, injected a 100 GHz soliton comb with 76 µW total signal power into one nonlinear GaP spiral, amplified it and generated a mirrored idler comb. They report simultaneous small-signal operation on several hundred lines with a strong common pump. A separate 16 GHz comb test used a 4.12 W pump. Paquot et al. demonstrated a programmable coherent FWM field dot product with up to three input channels at 40 Gb/s. The 128-dimensional cell still assumes that 255 populated weight teeth mixed with 128 input teeth give the intended complex sums on 128 outputs at the budgeted in-guide powers. A full-band check would include phase mismatch across contributing pairs, parasitic mixing, pump depletion and gain under simultaneous loading. [Kuznetsov et al., Fig. 3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11946905/#Fig3) · [Paquot et al.](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.22.002609)

**Trading precision for read energy.** Keep all 64 slots, all 4,096 real coefficients, the 4 cm guide, the 3.493 ns charged interval and the selector budget above. Scale the reference and weight pump powers together from their electrical optimum ratio, hold the raw-idler amplifier at +10 dB, and set final gain for +1 dB nominal useful transfer. Errors are pooled complex-field RMS relative to the digital outputs on the 64 single-vector reads. They include the four-field depletion response and the weak-field amplifier/FWM noise estimate. Each nominal output energy assumes a unit-norm ideal result. [Reproduce with the --accuracy option](hankel_port_recovery.py)

| Input → nominal output packet | G₂ gain required | Electrical fJ/MAC | Relative field RMS | Basis |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| 4 → 5.04 pJ | 29.76 dB | 198.9 | 10.79% | Existing candidate |
| 4 → 5.04 pJ | 30 dB | 197.5 | 11.07% | Same output interface |
| 4 → 5.04 pJ | 31 dB | 192.1 | 12.31% | Upper end of the budgeted final-gain target |
| 4 → 5.04 pJ | 33 dB | 183.0 | 15.13% | Requires higher loaded gain for the same driver energy |
| 4 → 5.04 pJ | 35 dB | 175.8 | 18.59% | Requires higher loaded gain for the same driver energy |
| 2 → 2.52 pJ | 31 dB | 179.2 | 16.33% | Lower-energy cascade, G₂ drive allowance falls from 331 to 281 pJ |
| 1 → 1.26 pJ | 31 dB | 179.2 | 22.58% | Same assumed 281 pJ pulser as at 2 pJ |

At the 31 dB, 4 pJ point the reference and nominal weight powers fall to 22.4 and 7.1 mW. The 12.31% estimate combines 5.32% deterministic and 11.10% stochastic error. The saved electrical energy is 3.4% relative to the existing candidate. With the two amplifier drives held at their brighter-packet budgets, those drives and the controls consume 148 fJ/MAC before either pump is supplied. A 31 dB ceiling places a stronger limit because a minimum pump product is needed to restore the useful output. A full bypass of G₁ while holding G₂ at 31 dB requires more pump light and costs 234 fJ/MAC. The 33–35 dB rows extrapolate past the present loaded amplifier budget. The 2 pJ case saves 9.9% only if the lower load permits removing the existing 50 pJ driver reserve and neighboring stages accept the lower-energy packet. Phase accuracy, extra loading from noise, actual driver consumption, and language-model accuracy require separate validation. The published G₂ data are small-signal and gain-compression curves. [Fraunhofer HHI amplifier data](https://www.hhi.fraunhofer.de/fileadmin/Departments/PC/LAS/InP-Semiconductor_Optical_Amplifiers_.pdf)

**Returning the used weight light is less promising.** On two dense matrices the gain budget fell to 317 fJ/MAC for this modification alone if the returning complex weight retained its phase. A return-arm Kerr phase sensitivity test gave 82% stored-matrix shape error after 32 reads, including the best common amplitude/phase correction. A read-dependent phase error across the comb would corrupt subsequent products. The reference-only and interleaver-selector proposals above leave the stored weight on its existing bypass. [Repeated-read weight-return test](hankel_weight_return.py)

**Finite-packet test.** The script propagates all interleaver taps on a common time array at 512 samples per 20 ps period. With a random complex 64 × 64 matrix, exact rectangular windows and flat conversion, relative waveform errors were below 3 × 10⁻¹⁴ for both reads, the written fields and two post-write queries. Tapering only the first and last 0.5 or 1 ps of every weight packet gave 7.6% and 12.8% forward field error, respectively, on the same matrix and input after fitting a common gain. These are one-case sensitivity tests. Device simulations must propagate real band-filter responses, group-delay dispersion, optical carrier phases, weight edges and amplifier noise. The ideal calculation retains the entire generated idler family until collection. A sharp filter retaining only the central 64 B teeth at the guide output changes the waveform and needs its own test. [Reproduce the shared, switched and two-bank checks](hankel_comb_codes.py)

**Two alternatives.** An electrically selected six-bit delay can apply mδ to a complete packet, followed by the same one-period interferometer and a fixed gate from T to 2T. Place this circuit on the input before FWM for the forward and write operations. Route the generated input-band field through it after FWM for the adjoint, selecting (−m mod 64)δ before the ordinary temporal sum. This single-bank alternative leaves the choice of input extender open, costs 6.02 dB in the passive copy stage and needs slot-rate delay selection. Shi et al. report five switchable delay stages with 12.5 ps delay steps and a 500 ps switching time. The proposed 0.3125 ps steps and 45 ps slots require different device performance. A second alternative stores the Fourier encodings of W and Wᵀ in separate fiber slots. Use the forward reader for both and swap which operand receives the timing code on the second write. At the earlier 25.3125 ps pitch the extra bank occupies 1.62 ns of optical storage. Two reads and two writes occupy 6.48 ns before overhead. The continuous 81-period burst from either operand, extra write energy and divergence of the two stored states under noise are specific costs. This alternative only saves the second long grid if the burst source itself can be implemented compactly. [Shi et al., device and switch measurements](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/lpor.71605)

##### Analog transfer between forward MLP layers

A signed-real forward layer can omit the final optical amplifier G₂ in the model. Keep G₁ at +10 dB ahead of the temporal sum, collect the completed 20 ps packet, separate its teeth, and measure each signed field with a phase-referenced balanced detector. Integrate the difference current, hold the voltage and drive a Mach–Zehnder modulator on the corresponding tooth of a fresh coherent comb. The new packet carries the known tooth phase code and the same nominal 5.04 pJ for a unit-norm output. The architecture requires 64 readout and modulation channels. Lederman et al. demonstrated analog detector-to-modulator cascading with intensity-encoded neurons. Arahata et al. measured directly wired devices with 22 ps optical excitation. These experiments establish the conversion mechanism. The receiver here additionally resolves field sign and weak 50 GHz-spaced modes. [Lederman et al.](https://doi.org/10.1109/IPCon.2023.10360794) · [Arahata et al.](https://doi.org/10.1063/5.0321491)

The test reuses the 16 dense real matrices and four input vectors per matrix from the single-read budget. At the original pump setting the gated signal before G₂ is 5.32 fJ for unit output norm, or 648 photons per tooth averaged over 64 teeth at 1550 nm. The receiver model applies a Gaussian field filter to the full gated waveform, then projects onto a centered 40 ps reference. An et al. measured 27.2 GHz equivalent intensity bandwidth and 3.5–6.4 dB center-frequency loss for a 64-channel, 50 GHz-spaced arrayed waveguide grating. An illustrative linear increase in loss from the central channels to the edges, 80% detector quantum efficiency, flat residual spectral phase and compensated delays give 7.8–15.1% net efficiency in the measured mode. Each detector pair receives 10 fJ of reference light, with 150 electrons RMS assumed equivalent charge noise including reset. The calculation removes G₂ spontaneous emission, retains the calculator's FWM and G₁ noise, assumes white excess noise within the gate, and adds one quadrature of homodyne shot noise. Results are pooled signed amplitude RMS relative to digital Wx on one forward read. [Measured filter](https://cpl.iphy.ac.cn/article/id/33126) · [Modulator-neuron noise analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.07325) · [Run the model](hankel_analog_relay.py)

| Interface and in-guide reference / weight powers | Signed RMS | Whole-cell electrical fJ/MAC |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| G₂, existing 25.84 / 8.14 mW | 8.24% | 198.9 |
| G₂, 22.45 / 7.07 mW | 9.40% | 192.2 |
| Analog, ideally linearized field up to ±2.5 times nominal RMS, 32.07 / 10.10 mW | 9.28% | 185.1 |
| Analog, ordinary sinusoidal modulator with fixed drive gain, ±3 times nominal RMS, 32.97 / 10.38 mW | 9.38% | 203.8 |

Use `--pump .252`, `--pump .36` and `--pump .37` for the last three operating points. Doubling the waveform sampling, propagation steps and noise quadrature order with `--fine` gave the same rounded 9.28% for the linearized case. At the original pump setting the illustrative receiver alone gives 10.48% and the linearized relay with ±2.5 times nominal RMS gives 11.08%. The previously quoted 10.79% for the original optical design includes both field quadratures. The relay's modeled output is a fresh carrier with the expected phase. Phase drift, measured filter impulse responses, leakage through a finite-extinction gate, bias drift, output waveform distortion and settling during a multilayer sequence require validation.

The energy estimate charges 3.493 ns per vector and assumes 0.35 mW per lane for all analog electronics including detection, hold, driver, phase/bias controls and any linearization. The reference costs 6.4 pJ per vector at 10% wall-to-detector delivery. For a flat fresh comb and a signed field range of ±H times nominal RMS, the fresh carrier costs 5.04 H² / η pJ per read. Here η includes source electricity, pulse formation, distribution, peak modulator transmission and routing up to the next layer. Encoding the coefficients by amplitude modulation consumes the H² headroom separately. At H = 2.5 and η = 20%, the carrier, reference and electronics cost 157.4, 6.4 and 78.2 pJ. The higher pump powers bring the rest of the cell to 516.3 pJ. This saves 28.9 pJ (3.7%) relative to the 9.40% G₂ case. At η = 10% it reaches 223.5 fJ/MAC. Doubling the lane electronics to 0.70 mW at 20% gives 204.2 fJ/MAC. Break-even is 16.9% delivered carrier efficiency at 0.35 mW per lane, or 0.479 mW per lane at 20%. Both the receiver noise and the power allowance must be achieved in the same circuit. A fabricated 10 Gb/s binary receiver consumed 0.70 mW for the receiver front end alone. A comb laser in a different configuration measured 5.6% wall-plug efficiency counting both ports before external routing. Pulsing by discarding light between vectors would spend most of the source energy outside the 20 ps useful window. [80-channel optical link](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41566-025-01633-0) · [Measured comb source](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-024-48544-2)

The dense test bank reaches 3.76 times nominal RMS in its largest coefficient. The ±2.5 range trades clipping for light savings. For a unit-norm output concentrated on any one tooth, preserving its full magnitude with fixed uniform carriers requires H = 8. At 20% efficiency that costs 1.61 nJ per vector for the transmitter alone. The present comparison concerns single-vector MLP reads in the simulated distribution. The adjoint, complex data, trained activation distributions and state updates need separate evaluation. A held output can also feed a low-rate ADC. Rabuske et al. measured 0.783 pJ/conversion for a nominal 5-bit ADC at 300 MS/s, or 50.1 pJ per vector. Avoiding the ADC and subsequent DAC can benefit a local interface. The existing G₂ comparison already uses an analog optical output. A digital bridge comparison must match end-to-end precision and include both the analog front end and the DAC. [Measured ADC](https://doi.org/10.1109/TCSII.2016.2547741)

##### Simultaneous adjoint and weight update

After prediction supplies the residual e, the adjoint W_old†e and increment η e x† can be calculated in parallel. Each slot uses the same coded B residual. The adjoint also needs its old weight tap, and the writer needs its saved A input. With all fields in one Kerr guide, the emerging A band contains both the transmitted input and the adjoint contribution. The weight band contains both the transmitted weight tap and the increment. Selecting wavelengths alone cannot isolate either contribution within its band. [Two-pump coupled-field equations](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.19.006648)

In the flat, first-order model the 64 forward/return tap products for the direct A field contain 32 values of +1/128 and 32 of −1/128. Their sum vanishes at the adjoint gate. However, the adjoint uses a reference with slot signs that also appear on the simultaneous weight increments. Ideal subtraction of the old weight tap followed by slot-wise sign correction recovers the increment to numerical precision. This assumes coherent access to the transmitted tap. At the 2 pJ operating point, the four-field model gives slot-dependent gain to the saved input even with e = 0. Against a separate nominal unit-residual adjoint, the spurious field on the 64 A teeth was 2.37% for identity, 38.81% for one dense orthogonal matrix and 61.35% for the signed-diagonal stress case. These are full-scale field fractions on individual tests. [First-order joint-path check](hankel_comb_codes.py) · [Nonlinear zero-residual test](hankel_simultaneous_read.py)

An alternative parallel layout keeps one long interleaver and splits the coded residual between two Kerr guides. One receives tapped old weights and the adjoint reference phase schedule, and returns its A idlers to the interleaver. The other receives the saved A input and write reference, and sends its weight increment directly to the memory coupler. Tap each old slot before injecting its update. This adds a Kerr path, coarse routing and residual fan-out. An equal passive split sends half the available residual power to each guide. A gain and noise budget for this parallel layout is open.

##### Single-sequence throughput in the archived TTT architecture

Use one model pass per generated token, updating every recurrent state once. The archived model has 24 layers, 16 logical heads per layer and width 512. Each structured map has two banks separated by a shuffle, with eight 64 × 64 cells operating in parallel in each bank. The six fixed maps have 96 local readers in total. The adaptive core has 16 prediction/update cells and 16 dedicated readers for querying the updated weights. All layers and heads reuse these engines. Real fixed factors give 159M real parameters and complex fixed factors give 310M real parameters under the archived counting convention. Both use the same schedule if a complex local map fits one frame. The earlier 1.356M passes/s estimate used a different cell and separate nonlinear forward, backward and write paths. [Archived model and source bundle](../archive/README.md#original-notes-code-and-results)

For the 20 ps, 4 cm FWM design, the interval between independent frames is *I* = 2.960 ns. The complete forward packet arrives after *d* = 3.454 ns including guide transit. Budget the same duration for an adjoint or a complete weight write. The ideal cell calculation checks three successive inputs in each read direction, the gradient and a post-update read. Physical write settling, changes between operating modes and propagation outside the modeled guide and delay arms require additional timing. [Cell timing](#improving-one-fully-programmable-64--64-read) · [Waveform checks](hankel_port_recovery.py)

| Single-sequence schedule | Optical arithmetic per layer | Optical arithmetic per token | Core passes/s | Tokens/s including example vocabulary transfer |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Finish one head before starting the next | 773.70 ns | 18.57 µs | 53,900 | 44,100 |
| Pipeline 16 heads in three sweeps | 181.56 ns | 4.36 µs | 229,500 | 118,300 |

A single head takes 14 dependent bank operations. The two input FFN maps take four, parallel K/Q/V projections take two, and prediction through both old banks takes two. Bank 2 then applies its old-weight adjoint to the residual. Once that result reaches bank 1, the two banks can write simultaneously, each in its own guide. The updated query takes two operations and the output projection takes two. Processing heads serially therefore takes 24 × 16 × 14 × 3.454 ns per token.

For the overlapping schedule, in each layer first run all 16 heads through the FFNs, projections and predictions. Next, run all 16 adjoints through bank 2 using its old weights. Finally, write both banks for each head and send its query and output projection through their dedicated readers. The first head takes 8*d*, *d* and 5*d* in those respective sweeps. The last head finishes 15*I* later in each sweep, giving 14*d* + 3 × 15*I* per layer. This requires synchronized holds for the keys, queries, values or residuals, and first-bank predictions. Each query waits 4*d* + 30*I* = 102.6 ns between its projection and the updated-state read. Bank 2 consumes three successive sets of frames in the same guide. Both schedules assume taps supply the old and updated weights within the pass.

The last table column assumes the electronic 16,000 × 512 vocabulary projection streams its 8,192,000 weights once per token at one byte per weight and 2 TB/s sustained, starting after the final hidden vector. This adds 4.096 µs assuming the dot products keep up. The actual precision, memory location and bandwidth are open. Additional time includes activation and shuffle transit, switching and writes beyond the bank allowance, detection, the head sum, residual and normalization, re-encoding between layers, and sampling and next-token feedback. At 100,000 tokens/s the three-sweep schedule has 5.64 µs for all these costs including readout, or 1.55 µs after the example transfer. End-to-end quality under successive optical writes also remains unverified.

Each archived adaptive ring holds 384 layer/head frames. Packing them at the new frame pitch alone requires 1.137 µs, about 232 m of equivalent fiber per ring at assumed group index 1.47. The old 150 m ring therefore needs a different storage or timing arrangement. With the archive's one-lap-per-token convention the arithmetic-only schedules correspond to about 3.79 km per ring for serial heads or 0.89 km for three sweeps. Other components supply part of the delay, and unhidden token feedback increases the required period under that convention. The 232 m figure describes contiguous frame storage. [Archived memory organization](../archive/README.md#what-carries-forward) · [Corning group index at 1550 nm](https://www.corning.com/media/worldwide/coc/documents/Fiber/product-information-sheets/PI-1424-AEN.pdf)

##### What survives 1,000 circulations in optical fiber memory

Yin and Che recorded a captured electrical transient as an optical intensity waveform. A small tap supplied a replica each lap, a low-noise erbium-doped fiber amplifier replaced the lost power, an electronic feedback loop held net gain at unity, and a filter suppressed noise. A lap took 7.8 µs. Figures 4–7 show the captured signal at turns 1, 100, 400 and 1,001. The 2,000-turn trace in Fig. 3 shows the gating signal. Photodetection recovered the transient envelope. The experiment gives direct precedent for analog pulse storage and leaves the optical carrier phase and the error of individual comb coefficients unmeasured. [Yin and Che, EPAC 2006, Figs. 1–7](https://proceedings.jacow.org/e06/PAPERS/TUPCH103.PDF)

Zhu et al. circulated 10 Gb/s binary data 10,000 times through a 125 km loop. Two semiconductor-amplifier interferometers reshaped the data on each lap, and a modulator retimed it using the recovered clock. At a bit-error rate of 10⁻⁹, lap 10,000 required less than 0.7 dB extra received power relative to lap 1. Jang et al. used a different mechanism. A continuous laser sustained stable soliton pulses in a passive fiber ring, and phase modulation of the drive trapped each pulse in its bit slot. They retained a 4,536-bit pattern for one minute, over 100 million laps. Both experiments encoded the information in which slots contained a pulse. [Zhu et al., Fig. 6](https://doi.org/10.1109/LPT.2006.871141) · [Jang et al., Figs. 3–4](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1607.07154)

For a phase comparison, Durán et al. generated more than 1,500 mutually coherent lines in an amplified frequency-shifting fiber loop. Successive laps shifted a common narrow-linewidth laser by a known frequency increment, and heterodyne detection used the seed as a reference. All the lines derived from one input field. Our weight memory requires 127 individually programmed complex coefficients in each of 64 slots and coherent addition of updates to the returning fields. [Durán et al., Fig. 1](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.26.013800) · [Weight encoding](#coding-the-hankel-sum-in-one-shared-interleaver)

For one 25% power tap and 0.2 dB of other loss, the memory amplifier needs 1.45 dB of gain per lap. Additional active taps and propagation loss increase that value. Known pilot fields could track common gain and phase without forcing the changing weights toward a fixed total power. Filtering can reject noise outside the occupied modes. At unity net gain, independent in-mode noise variances add across laps. Allowing 10% total RMS complex-field error after 1,000 idle laps gives a per-lap allowance of 0.316% relative to the stored vector norm if independent noise consumes the entire budget. A persistent gain bias or phase offset accumulates coherently. The relevant retention test compares the recovered comb with the programmed complex vector relative to the write reference, first with idle circulation and then with a known sequence of optical updates. [Weight-loop tap budget](#optimistic-electrical-budget-and-electronic-cim-comparison) · [Caves, noise in linear amplifiers](https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevD.26.1817)

**Minimum hardware for the long weight loop.** Keep the read tap and coherent write coupler on one optical loop. Specify a low-noise amplifier with gain control and a flat passband covering the weight packets, including their pulse sidebands. A bandpass filter limits recirculating out-of-band spontaneous emission and can be integrated with the amplifier. A known pilot packet in a reserved slot can reference the returning field to the write beam for common gain and phase control. Its arrival time provides a separate observable for packet alignment. A scalar trim corrects a common error. For example, a residual gain difference of +0.001 dB per lap on one tooth accumulates to +1 dB relative to the reference after 1,000 idle laps, a 12.2% field-amplitude difference. The measured or modeled complex round-trip response and in-band noise determine the usable retention time. [Yin and Che, amplifier and feedback](https://epaper.kek.jp/e06/PAPERS/TUPCH103.PDF)

The long storage fiber also needs dispersion management. For the illustrative 1550 nm center, 127 teeth at 50 GHz span 6.30 THz or 50.5 nm between the outer carriers. Taking the representative G.652 dispersion of 17 ps/(nm·km) as constant gives about 129 ps of differential group delay across 150 m, or 199 ps across 232 m, in a single lap. The weight window is 20 ps and adjacent occupied slots are 45 ps apart. A common delay trim moves the entire packet.
