A length of fiber with opposite dispersion can compensate the wavelength-dependent delay for all teeth together. Include the dispersion slope and component delays over the full band, and check that teeth overlap at each read and write tap. Li et al. used a shared section of dispersion-compensating fiber in an experimental broadband waveform buffer and studied ten replicas. [ITU-T G.652, Table I.1](https://www.itu.int/epublications/publication/itu-t-g-652-2024-08-characteristics-of-a-single-mode-optical-fibre-and-cable) · [Li et al., Fig. 3 and Methods](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-017-00093-7)

##### Amplifier placement for the shared interleaver

For a two-amplifier design, place **G₁ after the FWM guide and pump rejection, before the generated field re-enters the interleaver**, and **G₂ after the final 20 ps output gate**. Both are on the signal branches and the weight write bypasses them. A forward read amplifies the new B field and an adjoint read amplifies the new A field. Sharing the physical amplifiers requires suitable gain spectra for both routed bands. The numerical choice is +10.0 and +29.80 dB for the identity. It gives +1.0 dB useful signal gain in the linear model. The coupled-wave stress test below finds that the actual useful gain can be negative for allowed inputs even if both gain stages operate linearly. [Editable cell schematic](../figures/shared_interleaver_hankel_cell.svg)

**Budget and comparison.** The physical references are a 2 pJ, 20 ps input and the completed packet after G₂. The ideal identity power transfer is 0.468/(16 × 64), or −33.40 dB. Allow 2.2 dB excess on each traversal of the shared interleaver, 0.5 dB at pump rejection and 0.5 dB at the final gate. This gives −38.80 dB. The 2.2 dB is a proxy from a different single-band interleaver. The two 0.5 dB values are allowances. The coarse band routers, chip interfaces and full gain spectra need measurements or layout-based estimates. At +1 dB, the intended output is 2.52 pJ, equivalent to 126 mW during the occupied window and 0.874 mW averaged over one packet per 2.88 ns frame. A single added dB of loss consumes the nominal margin. [Qiu et al., interleaver and loss convention](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z) · [Lei et al., CW data underlying the FWM extrapolation](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447)

Sweep the first stage over 0–10 dB by 0.25 dB and set the last stage to hit the same +1 dB target, with a 35 dB ceiling. For sites handling the full raw family, use 6 dB high-gain noise figure. For the input and completed-packet stages use 5 dB. The objective minimizes the larger of forward and adjoint added RMS complex-field errors. Input amplification is also tested as a counterfactual. It produces more signal loading in a guide already far outside the weak-conversion regime for some patterns. The result is conditional on the two-stage budget, pump setting and achievable wideband gain. Kuznetsov et al. measured over 10 dB net signal gain across 70 nm with a driven GaP parametric amplifier and estimated a combined 17 THz signal/idler gain span by symmetry. The reported amplifier used watt-scale pumping and exhibited saturation. VIAVI lists ≥35 dB multichannel erbium amplifier gain under its −20 dBm input test. Neither result establishes loaded gain for these packets. [GaP amplifier experiment](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-08666-z) · [VIAVI specifications](https://www.viavisolutions.com/en-us/literature/erbium-doped-fiber-amplifier-medfa-c1-data-sheet-en.pdf)

| First gain location (second gain after the output gate) | Forward RMS | Adjoint RMS | Interpretation |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| Gated launch, before making copies | 7.08% | 7.26% | Optimistic clean gate after first gain. Increases the FWM seed. |
| Replica train, before FWM | 7.50% | 8.80% | Independent noise on the replicas. Increases the FWM seed. |
| **Pump-rejected raw idlers** | **11.52%** | **15.29%** | Best tested location with unchanged FWM input power. |
| After return interleaver, before B copy and gate | 18.79% | 23.83% | More of the first amplifier's continuous noise reaches the selected mode. |
| Both amplifiers after the gate | 22.37% | 22.95% | The weakest useful packet enters the first amplifier. |

The noise calculation covers the full 64-tooth output vector, one matched temporal mode per tooth and one polarization. It includes the conjugating FWM vacuum term for all weight-supported partners, plus phase-insensitive amplifier ASE. The replica-booster case admits ASE in the 64 nominal operand modes. For high-gain noise factor F use m(G) = F(G−1)/2, so a bypass adds zero. Continuous raw-idler ASE reaches a forward output mode with power transfer 0.13426 and an adjoint mode with 0.26852. White-noise Monte Carlo through the actual return tap coefficients gave 0.13358 and 0.26925. Multiple-source pump noise, signal-dependent nonlinear noise, amplifier compression and leakage into following layers during dark gaps are outside the stochastic comparison. If +15 dB flat first-stage gain can be delivered at the required load, the model gives 10.23% forward and 14.54% adjoint. A reserve for 100 seeded real orthogonal programs plus identity gave a common pump normalization of 1.16908 and G₂ = +31.16 dB for the same +1 dB target. That scale stays fixed across that bank. Training can exceed the reserved weight range. Gain is relative to the intended matrix output and a legitimate matrix null can be dark. [Reproduce the gain sweep](hankel_amplifier_sweep.py) · [Noise conventions and references](#amplifier-noise-between-layers)

**Bandwidth.** Before coherent collection, 127 weight teeth and 64 data teeth generate up to 190 centers over 9.45 THz. A 4 THz flat gain window with 0.25 THz smooth edges and unity transmission outside it produced 0.44% fitted waveform error for a dense matrix with random-phase input and 10.60% for the equal-phase identity input. A 17 THz window gave 0.18% and 6.58% on those cases. These errors isolate the finite bandwidth acting on rectangular weight packets. Phase ripple, pump-removal notches and gain saturation require device transfer functions. [Finite-band waveform simulation](hankel_idler_bandwidth.py)

**Pump depletion and useful gain.** A separate four-field calculation evolves the two pumps, input and generated field in retarded time. The effective coupling reproduces the monochromatic undepleted efficiency of 0.468 at 50 mW per pump. Each time slice conserves photon flux, with equal photon energies assumed across bands. The test omits dispersion, group-velocity mismatch, self- and cross-phase modulation and parasitic families. It tests the same common-velocity assumption as the instantaneous linear multiplier. The canonical identity weight comb has a peak-to-mean power ratio of 64. At the passive input loading, aligning 64 input phases makes the weak model predict a 902 mW instantaneous idler against a 50 mW reference. For a single input tooth, adding +10 dB before FWM pushes the prediction from 14.1 to 141 mW. Moving gain downstream avoids increasing this loading. [Four-wave mixing and pump depletion](https://orbit.dtu.dk/files/5516307/3BC14264d01.pdf) · [Reproduce coupled-wave and convergence checks](hankel_fwm_stress.py)

| 2 pJ input; pumps at nominal power and ideal flat G₁/G₂ | Gain projected onto the intended output after +39.80 dB | Whole-waveform error after the best scalar fit |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| Identity, one lit tooth | +4.16 dB | 32.0% |
| Identity, equal-phase comb | **−15.58 dB** | **687%** |
| Identity, seeded signed input | +3.13 dB | 71.8% |
| One dense orthogonal matrix, same signed input | +0.36 dB | 7.36% |

The equal-phase case retains approximately 358% error when projected onto just the 64 intended teeth. Waveform error can exceed 100% when most light is orthogonal to the intended answer. Total optical output power can therefore rise while the useful matrix gain falls. These deterministic errors and the small-signal noise table come from different models. The ideal forward, adjoint and post-write field checks verify the diagram's routing. A centered directional-derivative check also tests the reused guide as a backward operator. For the one-tooth identity input, the weak forward derivative and reverse response both gave 1.000 in units of the weak-path gain. In the depleted model they gave −0.250 and +3.116 respectively. The reverse read therefore cannot supply the true local gradient at that operating point. The operating point still fails a full-scale arbitrary-input gain and accuracy claim. [Three-pass waveform verification](hankel_comb_codes.py)

##### Can fixed tooth phases recover the multiplication?

Give tooth j a permanent phase q[j] = exp(iπj²/128). With Q = diag(q), the input band carries Q conj(x), the output band carries Qy, and the optical program encodes QWQ. The conjugation in FWM then gives QWQ conj(Q conj(x)) = QWx. The same convention on the residual and saved input encodes the dense write Q(e x†)Q. This proposal assumes native preparation and retention of the phases through successive layers. A new phase shaper on each layer would need its own loss and area budget. Fixed multitone phases are an established way of lowering peaks. [Schroeder, IEEE Transactions on Information Theory](https://doi.org/10.1109/TIT.1970.1054411)

The deterministic tests use the same coupled four-field model, 2 pJ packet, 50 mW reference, 50 mW weight cap and +39.8 dB read amplification. For the equal logical input and identity, the weight peak/mean falls from 64 to 1.82 and the weak-model raw idler peak from 902 mW to about 1.19 mW. The depleted model recovers **+1.02 dB useful gain and 1.67% whole-field RMS error** against the fixed weak calibration. Across 32 independently sampled real orthogonal matrices and 32 signed real inputs, a single pump normalization of 1.1576 and one calibration gave **6.74–8.18%** deterministic error (median 7.34%). At the previous gains useful gain ranged from −0.11 to +0.05 dB. Adding 1.27 dB to G₂ (31.07 dB total) gives +1.16 to +1.32 dB. The separate weak-model noise estimate on that scale is 13.2% forward and 17.4% adjoint. These noise figures are single-read estimates; the coupled-wave waveform tests omit ASE. [Phase-coded tests](hankel_phase_recovery.py) · [Amplifier covariance model](hankel_amplifier_sweep.py)

A real diagonal matrix with entries ±1 and a real unit input chosen to reinforce the encoded phases gave **83.1%** deterministic error at fixed calibration, still 64.3% after fitting a scalar. The true and optically returned radial derivatives differed by 0.66 in weak-gain units for that case, versus 0.009 for the equal-input identity case. Any fixed choice of phases has a related limitation. For N unit phasors, some choice of real signs has field sum at least 2N/π in magnitude: average the sum of the absolute projections over directions in the complex plane, then choose signs along the best direction. Choosing the diagonal signs and the input signs independently achieves this bound for both combs at the same time in one weight slot. At this operating point the perturbative model then demands at least 902 × (2/π)⁴ ≈ 148 mW from a 50 mW reference. That bound diagnoses failure of the uniform weak approximation. The explicit signed-matrix test measures the error after summation. Separately, a signed reversal required a 5.095 pump normalization and delivered −13.58 dB useful gain with the same amplifiers. Positive gain also depends on how a matrix loads the weight slots.

For three individual phase-coded writes with 2 pJ each for the saved key and error, comparing the reconstructed matrix increment to the desired outer product gave 0.10–0.72% deterministic error. The test projects onto 127 harmonics before synthesis and precedes memory injection. It covers neither retention and repeated noisy writes nor the derivative of a distorted read. Reducing the interaction alone in the original encoding also trades noise for accuracy. At 16% of nominal effective length the four original test cases showed 3.7–6.5% fixed-calibration errors, but +55.7 dB of read amplification was needed to restore +1 dB on the calibration input. The two modeled amplifiers alone would then add 69% RMS error. [Effective-length and power sweep](hankel_drive_tradeoff.py)

##### Can a fixed gain per output frequency correct the read?

For each fixed 64 × 64 matrix, fit 64 complex output gains by least squares on 128 independent signed real inputs and freeze them for 64 new inputs. All packets have 2 pJ. Pooled RMS field errors on the 64 intended teeth, with one fitted gain versus 64 fitted gains, were **1.10% → 0.19%** for identity, **4.53% → 4.52%** for one random dense orthogonal matrix, and **34.25% → 14.55%** for the signed diagonal chosen above. Each matrix received its own calibration. These results cover deterministic forward reads and exclude amplifier noise. [Simulation and calibration](hankel_tooth_calibration.py)

For the signed diagonal, send two inputs with equal tooth powers and the same positive field on tooth 0. Change only the signs of other inputs. The intended output on tooth 0 stays at +1/8. Its simulated output divided by that target changes from 1.700 at −11.06° to 1.257 at +7.34°. Even fitting the correction on both answers leaves 21.53% RMS field error on that tooth. With the second sign pattern, halving the whole input field and doubling the measured output also changes the output vector by 39.1% of the ideal full-scale vector norm. This second test directly establishes input-amplitude dependence in the depleted model. The tests establish a limit of output equalization for that weight program and operating power. [Equal-energy and scaled-input probes](hankel_tooth_calibration.py)

##### How accurate is a forward read on trained TTT matrices?

The phase-coded cell was tested on frozen 64 × 64 states from the pretrained TTT-Linear-125M model, using one head per layer. For each of 12 heads, three WikiText-2 articles supplied calibration states and three different articles supplied test states, at prefixes of 64, 256 and 1,024 tokens in each split. Eight later queries were read against each saved matrix, giving 288 calibration reads and 288 held-out reads. Each query retained its direction and was scaled to a 2 pJ packet. One optical weight scale and one complex output gain per head were fixed after calibration. The scale reserved 15% field headroom over the largest calibration slot. The largest held-out slot used 40.57 mW of the 50 mW weight budget. [Trained-read probe](hankel_trained_reads.py) · [State and query extraction](ttt_state_probe.py)

On the 64 intended output teeth the pooled error was **2.83% deterministic RMS field error**, relative to the pooled energy in the exact matrix outputs. The separate weak-conversion noise model, evaluated on those same programs with +10 and +31.07 dB amplifiers, gave **10.41% added forward RMS noise**. Treating this noise as independent and zero-mean gives **10.79% estimated RMS field error per read**. Across heads the estimated combined error ranged from 6.89% to 17.39%. This is a single-read estimate on frozen states with normalized inputs. End-to-end inference would additionally exercise the native amplitude distribution, chained layers, optical writes and subsequent reads of the modified memory. [Forward error and noise calculation](hankel_trained_reads.py)

##### How much does 10% read noise affect next-token prediction?

Evaluate the pretrained TTT-Linear-125M checkpoint on 12 held-out WikiText-2 passages at 1,088 tokens each (13,044 next-token predictions per run). For every token, head and layer, add independent, zero-mean real Gaussian noise before bias and normalization. The noise standard deviation per component is 10% of the RMS of the current clean 64-component read. In the first condition the perturbed product is the query read used for retrieval. In the second, an independent noise draw also perturbs the key read used to calculate each update. Updated memories and downstream layers receive the effects of earlier perturbations. State storage and update arithmetic remain digital. This test measures model sensitivity to a relative error distribution. The optical cell also has an amplifier noise floor and signal-dependent distortion. [Reproduce both conditions](ttt_read_noise.py)

Without retraining, clean perplexity was **26.1379**. Averaging next-token losses across noise seeds 1729 and 2718 before exponentiation gave **26.3063** with query-read noise (+0.64%) and **26.3623** with key- and query-read noise (+0.86%). The paired increase in nats/token was 0.00642 for query only and 0.00855 for both. A paired bootstrap over the 12 articles gave respective 95% intervals of [0.00484, 0.00841] and [0.00653, 0.01111], conditional on the two noise seeds. Both zero-noise runs reproduced the archived per-article losses.

Two precedents put the device error budget in context. A photonic tensor processor achieved 10.9% relative MVM error with four-read averaging. Accuracy in that mode was 98.1% on MNIST, compared with a 98.9% digital baseline, and 72.0% on a 400-image CIFAR-10 subset, compared with a reported 89.5% digital baseline. A 27B hybrid Gated DeltaNet quantization preprint reported 10.6% median relative error for the query/key/value projection in a four-bit replay. The model with quantized linear layers scored 85.10% on a five-task average, compared with 85.62% at BF16. Each paper uses its own error metric and operating regime. [Photonic tensor processor, Fig. 4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-71599-2/figures/4) · [Gated DeltaNet quantization, Tables 1 and 3](https://arxiv.org/html/2609.04098v1#S5.SS2)

##### Extra hardware for the 20.3125 ps weight spacing

Take the input pulse extender, one FWM guide with its reference and band filters, the weight memory, amplification and the six-stage output pulse interleaver as the baseline. One master timing reference can define 65 data periods and 64 weight starts in 1.30 ns. This requires frame alignment with the memory and optical phase control on the shared paths. The 0.3125 ps offset per slot follows from the programmed spacing of the stored packets. The six forward delays grow by 1.5625%, adding 19.6875 ps in total differential delay, or 2.95 mm of guide at assumed group index 2. The number of stages and couplers stays six.

**Input extension.** The required output is 65 phase-continuous periods of the data comb. For an extender that already makes 64 equal copies, one ideal implementation uses a 64:1 power splitter, a phase trim and a timed 2 × 1 switch. The small branch supplies the first period. Delay the start of the 64-copy branch by 20 ps relative to it and select that branch for periods 2–65. Each period then gets 1/65 of the incident energy before insertion loss. The added differential path is 3.00 mm at group index 2 after matching the common transit times. In the existing two-path model, the 64-copy output has 36 positive and 28 negative field taps. Such an extender also needs a common phase modulator or equivalent control in the switch to align the copies. A fixed phase per weight packet cannot correct a data-phase jump inside that packet. Other input extenders can have a different parts count. [Two-path field model](hankel_temporal_combiner.py) · [Measured six-stage pulse interleaver](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z)

**Local weight write.** Tap and hold the input until the residual is available. A phase-trimmed two-input combiner can form the residual from the target and prediction at matched times and amplitudes. The write also needs 64 residual copies on the weight clock, a route that selects the generated weight band, and a phase-controlled injection coupler into memory with the write strength and circulating gain calibrated. A scheduled reverse pass through the passive output interleaver can generate the residual copies if its switches and slot phases support that transfer. The same nonlinear guide serves the forward read and the write in successive passes.

**Gradient for the preceding layer.** Route the generated input band into a collector for 65 data periods. If the input extender is reciprocal and supports the reverse switch and phase schedule, the two existing temporal networks serve all three passes. For the 64:1 example, the first 64 returning slices traverse the 64-slot collector and its extra 20 ps delay. Their sum meets the last slice from the direct branch. The 64:1 coupler gives every slice a final field coefficient of 1/√65. Otherwise budget a dedicated 20 ps collector and the extra branch. Bidirectional reuse requires optical routing around any one-way amplification and separate time for each pass. Finite switching transients, insertion loss, write noise and the initial loading of pretrained weight packets still need physical budgets.

<a id="a-two-path-interleaver-for-the-64-slot-sum"></a>

##### Output pulse interleaver for the 64-slot sum

Use one clocked 1 × 2 switch, six differential delays of 32, 16, 8, 4, 2 and 1 times the spacing P between partial results, and a phase-trimmed 50:50 coupler after each delay. Two waveguides carry the full comb between stages. The switch sends slots 0–31 through the first long arm and slots 32–63 through the short arm. The first coupler overlaps slots 0 and 32, 1 and 33, and so on. Keep both outputs through the remaining stages. Gate one output in slot 63, starting at 63P plus common propagation. Each incoming slot m has one path to that output with delay (63−m)P and field coefficient of magnitude 1/8. Encode the conjugate of its fixed common path phase in that slot’s FWM reference. This returns Σₘ zₘ/8 on every tooth in the local packet coordinates. The Walsh benchmark uses P = 20 ps. The staggered forward read uses P = 20.3125 ps, giving differential delays from 20.3125 to 650 ps. A static entrance splitter fed at one port costs a further 3 dB in the selected slot. A recirculating accumulator could realize the same ideal sum, but its earliest partial must survive 63 round trips.

At equal incident partial fields and zero excess loss, this delivers **24.1 dB more energy into one 20 ps slot** than the archived 128-tap collector (field coefficients 1/8 and 1/128). Comparing with the energy over its entire 64-slot hold gives a 6.0 dB advantage. Independent equal-variance field noise in each slot has one slot’s variance at the combined output because the squared coefficients sum to one. Common pump fluctuations, downstream amplifier noise and the useful photon count need their own budget. With a passive input pulse extender and a fixed weight-comb cap, the ideal power gain of the Walsh construction for a real orthogonal W is η/(64 p²), where p is the largest norm of the unscaled weight combs. For 100 seeded matrices, the 10th, 50th and 90th percentiles were −23.09, −22.75 and −22.49 dB at η = 0.468. For the identity, allowing 5.2 dB for the single-output input pulse extender and 2.2 dB for the output pulse interleaver gives −28.8 dB before switch, gate and external coupling losses. At 2 pJ across 64 equally populated input teeth, that leaves about 324 photons per output tooth at 1550 nm. A single restoring amplifier with 5 dB noise figure would add about 7.0% RMS field error under the matched-mode noise model. [Input pulse extender loss budget](#a-simulated-read-through-a-chirped-fiber-bragg-grating) · [Amplifier noise model](#amplifier-noise-between-layers)

Tünnermann and Shirakawa demonstrated coherent pulse stacking with delay lines and phase preshaping. Qiu et al. fabricated a six-stage silicon nitride interleaver and demonstrated 64-fold pulse-rate multiplication to 14 GHz. Their 2.2 dB on-chip loss excludes external coupling and their 3 dB final port split. The 20 ps output pulse interleaver adapts this layout for coherent summation. The switch must settle between adjacent packets at the half-frame and frame boundaries. For the 50 GHz grid, Δf × 20 ps = 1 makes the ideal relative path phase common to every tooth. A 10 fs residual delay in one arm after trimming the center frequency gives a 0.10 rad phase error at the edges of the 3.15 THz comb. [Tünnermann and Shirakawa](https://doi.org/10.1364/OL.42.004829) · [Qiu et al., Figs. 1–3 and Methods](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z)

Run `python3 notes/hankel_temporal_combiner.py` for the two-rail field simulation. Three consecutive frames tested a complex sum, exact cancellation and full coherent collection. With all six long arms 10 fs late, one output scale per tooth leaves 7.1% RMS error in the **slot coefficients**. Fixed spectral phase ramps on the input masks and weight combs also correct a known linear phase slope per slot, because the FWM indices obey (i+j)−j = i. This restored a dense read to numerical precision in the carrier-phase model. The model assumes orthogonal 20 ps modes, ideal switching and flat couplers. It omits envelope distortion, higher-order dispersion, phase drift and gate leakage. [Simulation](hankel_temporal_combiner.py)

<a id="replaying-the-short-packet-at-the-next-layer"></a>

##### Input pulse extender for the next layer

The gate at the output of the pulse interleaver passes one 20 ps packet containing all 64 result teeth. If the layer uses an intensity-dependent activation, apply it at its calibrated packet power before feeding the next layer’s input pulse extender. One candidate is a two-path network with differential delays of 20, 40, 80, 160, 320 and 640 ps. Its delays generate copies at m × 20 ps for m = 0…63. Before its final switch, the first 32 copies occupy the short arm and the last 32 occupy the long arm. A clocked 2 × 1 switch routes the occupied arm to one FWM guide and resets between frames. The 1.28 ns dense read with these 64 copies assumes the ideal Walsh signs. The timing scheme requires a longer burst and continuity across all the copy boundaries. It takes 65 periods at 20.3125 ps weight spacing or 81 periods at 25.3125 ps weight spacing. [Qiu et al., six-stage optical replication at 14 GHz](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z)

For input packet energy E and input pulse extender power transmission u, each copy carries uE/64. An ideal input pulse extender fed with 2 pJ delivers 31.25 fJ per slot, or 1.56 mW across the comb during the slot. Maintaining 100 mW across the comb in every slot requires 128 pJ delivered per burst. Allowing 2.2 dB of loss in the input pulse extender gives 18.8 fJ per slot before switch loss. Using the same 2.2 dB allowance for the output pulse interleaver gives −25.8 dB for the identity read at η = 0.468, before switches, gate, masks and external coupling. The −21.4 dB ideal identity budget already includes sharing the input across 64 visits. The switch at the end of the input pulse extender can recover up to the 3 dB that a fixed final coupler would send to its other output. Its insertion loss, extinction and transition time determine the recovery. [Qiu et al., measured loss convention](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12144144/)

In the ideal simulation of this binary-delay input candidate, three consecutive vectors pass through the input pulse extender, phase correction and output pulse interleaver. The recovered vectors have a relative field error of 1.1 × 10⁻¹⁵. The Walsh benchmark adds six ideal wavelength-dependent phase masks in the long arms. In the arm of delay 2ᵇ slots, flip the sign of tooth j if bit b of j is one. The path to slot m traverses exactly the arms for the set bits of m, giving the sign (−1) raised to the number of bits set in both m and j. The FWM reference can absorb the fixed common path phase of each slot. A dense read with complex input matched its ideal target within 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ in the discrete-mode model. The staggered-timing benchmark obtains its phases from the weight-packet start times. [Input pulse extender and dense-read simulation](hankel_temporal_combiner.py)

##### Implementing the fixed phases and FWM waveguide

**Per-tooth shaper ruled out for this cell.** The proposed Walsh implementation used an AWG, one phase section per tooth, and a second AWG. Each shaper fans a 64-tooth comb into 64 physical waveguides. Placing the shapers in the output interleaver has the same cost. A fast phase modulator on every tooth also requires a channel-sized bank of devices. Fontaine et al. demonstrated a 64-channel AWG pair at 20 GHz spacing and Tahvili et al. characterized a 20-channel shaper at 50 GHz spacing. These devices are precedents for the line-by-line benchmark. [Fontaine et al.](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17339963/) · [Tahvili et al.](https://pure.tue.nl/ws/portalfiles/portal/3830952/590796516057448.pdf)

For the input-side encoding, let Sₘⱼ be the resulting ±1 on data tooth j in slot m. For target W, the stored field on weight tooth s in that slot is proportional to `Σⱼ Sₘⱼ W[s−j,j]`, including only indices 0 ≤ s−j < 64 and using one scale across all slots. Play the preloaded 64-packet weight frame in sync with the data copies. Wavelength-multiplex the coded 64-tooth data, the 127-tooth weight packet and a phase-locked reference tone into the same polarization and spatial mode of one dispersion-engineered Kerr guide. With band origins chosen so `f_R + f_h[0] = f_x[0] + f_y[0]`, the idler on tooth i carries `R Σⱼ hₘ[i+j] Sₘⱼ conj(xⱼ)` up to a common gain in the flat, weak-conversion model. A band filter rejects the strong pumps before signal gain. The output pulse interleaver adds the partial fields on each tooth. Orthogonality of the sign rows gives W on signed-real data. The data-field conjugation also specifies the logical input encoding for complex reads. [Dual-pumped waveguide FWM](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-018-31470-x) · [Dense multiplication and input pulse extender simulation](hankel_temporal_combiner.py)

The mask must act on a finite pulse. With square 20 ps packets and 50 GHz pitch, one gated carrier’s spectral first zeros lie at ±50 GHz. For the alternating-sign arm, consider a lossless filter that sets exactly 0 or π across each 50 GHz bin with transitions halfway between teeth. After a common delay is removed, its overlap with the intended signed square-pulse mode is `∫ sinc²(u) sign(cos πu) du = 2/π`, with normalized sinc and u measured in units of the tooth pitch relative to an even tooth. Only the fundamental of the square-wave Fourier series overlaps the triangular transform of sinc². The correct mode therefore carries 40.5% of the input energy (−3.92 dB) on this isolated pass, with the rest in temporal tails. For masks in either delay network, propagate the six complex filter responses and neighboring time slots together. The previously quoted 3.7% added noise used ideal toothwise masks. [Comparison and timing-coded alternative](#choosing-the-fwm-multiplier-for-a-dense-temporal-sum)

##### Walsh masks in the output pulse interleaver

**Walsh benchmark with the shaper on the output.** This calculation leaves all input copies unmasked and puts the signs in the long arms of the output pulse interleaver. The per-tooth shaper layout was ruled out on spatial scaling. The weights are re-encoded for signs on the **output** tooth i. With row i, column j and slot m, let Qₘᵢ be the output sign divided by 8, including the ideal temporal summation coefficient. For any dense signed-real W, store `hₘ[s] = Σᵢ Qₘᵢ W[i,s−i]` over valid column indices. Then `W[i,j] = Σₘ Qₘᵢ hₘ[i+j]` because QᵀQ = I. All equations here use one calibrated overall field scale. The physical model includes 1/8 field amplitude per copy from a passive equal-energy input pulse extender. [Training with the shared-waveguide timing scheme](#whole-comb-timing-for-the-adjoint-and-weight-update)

| Operation | Optical fields and routing in slot m |
| --- | --- |
| Forward read | Mix input x, old weight comb hₘ and reference R in the FWM guide. The output pulse interleaver applies the slot’s signs to the generated output teeth and adds the 64 partial results. |
| Input gradient | Form signed residual e = v − Wx. Apply slot m’s output signs to a copy of e, then mix it with the same old hₘ and R. The Hankel matrix is symmetric. A plain temporal sum of the returning input-band fields gives the input descent signal Wᵀe. |
| Weight update | Mix a retained copy of x, the same signed residual copy and R. The term at `f_B[i] + f_A[j] − f_R` lands on weight tooth s = i+j. One visit generates all 127 increments for slot m. A phase-aligned write coupler adds the scaled increment to that slot’s stored weight comb. |

The write is `Δhₘ[s] = η Σᵢ₊ⱼ₌ₛ Qₘᵢ eᵢ xⱼ`. Substitution into the read gives exactly `ΔW = ηexᵀ`. The Hankel symmetry also lets the originally proposed **input** masks support training. For that encoding the backward pass applies each input mask after its Hankel multiplication, and the writer mixes the masked saved input with the unmasked residual. Each memory slot uses its own old weights until its backward read is finished. A single FWM guide can be scheduled in separate 64-slot passes. A single linear delta-memory write can omit the input-gradient pass when upstream gradients are unnecessary. [Local FWM read, adjoint and write](#backpropagation-and-the-ttt-weight-update) · [Dual-pump field equations](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-018-31470-x)

If the output pulse interleaver is switched into reverse to produce the signed residual copies, slot order matters. Write the usual Walsh sign as S[k,i]. The forward contribution from slot m takes the long arms for 63−m, giving Qₘᵢ = S[63−m,i]/8. The reverse train emits S[m,i]. Applying the single fixed sign pattern S[63,i] to the incoming error gives S[63,i]S[m,i] = S[63−m,i], preserving ascending weight-slot order after common path-phase calibration. Another implementation can address the stored slots in reverse. Full input backprop also needs an unmasked sum, obtained through a separate path or a mask bypass with buffering and scheduled reuse. The write uses a saved input and an error train synchronized to the weight memory. Noiseless 64-tooth checks recovered the forward vector, input descent signal, dense weight increment and post-write query for real and complex fields, all within 1.3 × 10⁻¹⁵ relative error. Finite differences independently checked the stored-weight gradients. In the complex convention the input band carries conj(x), the output band carries e, the returned input field encodes W†e, and the write gives ηex†. For a measured real slot-by-wavelength code C that still acts diagonally on the teeth, using the same code for the read and write gives CᵀC acting on each zero-padded target anti-diagonal before restriction to its valid entries. A common multiple of the identity Gram matrix preserves the dense update up to scale. More general dispersion and slot leakage require the adjoint of the measured wavelength-and-time transfer. Write signal-to-noise ratio, amplifier directionality and coherent memory retention remain device-level questions. [Interleaver and training calculation](hankel_temporal_combiner.py)

<a id="footprint-alternatives-for-input-replay"></a>

##### Input pulse extender footprint alternatives

**Compact spirals for a binary-delay input pulse extender.** At 20 ps pitch its six feed-forward delays sum to 1.26 ns, or 18.9 cm at group index 2. Liu et al. fit 1 m of SiN waveguide into 6.6 mm². Linear scaling gives **1.25 mm² of delay routing** per input pulse extender. Hashemi et al. measured 1.163 ns in a 0.90 mm² silicon spiral. Six separate bends/spirals, couplers, masks, switch, electronics and routing require a floorplan. Qiu et al.’s complete 8.5 × 1.7 mm interleaver operated at 14 GHz with a 33 cm longest arm. The 50 GHz design has a 9.6 cm longest differential arm at group index 2. [Liu et al., Fig. 4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-021-21973-z) · [Hashemi et al., measured spiral](https://doi.org/10.1109/SiPhotonics68911.2026.11520725) · [Qiu et al.](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z)

**A 160 ps tapped loop feeding three delay stages.** Extract eight equal-energy group packets, the first on entry and the last after seven laps. Each group traverses 20, 40 and 80 ps feed-forward delays to make eight copies. The 64 output slots are contiguous. The loop plus the three delay arms total 300 ps, or 4.50 cm at group index 2, **4.2× less installed delay length**. The loop tap changes every 160 ps and the final short-mesh selector changes arms every 80 ps. The Walsh benchmark also needs three within-group masks and an eight-band, group-dependent mask. The staggered-timing benchmark requires a longer phase-continuous burst. The area estimate covers the 64-copy delay routing.

For M equal releases with power survival r per lap, the maximum collected energy fraction is M/Σⱼ r⁻ʲ for j = 0…M−1, assuming lossless loading, ideal variable taps, full discharge and flat complex response across the comb. Each release consumes r⁻ʲ times its delivered energy from the initial packet. At 0.10 dB/lap the penalty is 0.36 dB for eight releases and 3.54 dB for 64. The loop alone uses the provisional 2.2 dB input pulse extender loss allowance at 0.572 dB/lap for eight releases or 0.0647 dB/lap for 64. Ekici et al. measured 0.89 and 1.29 dB/lap in 103 and 198 ps switched buffers. Using these as scenarios gives 3.58–5.48 dB for eight equal releases before the short mesh, mask and external losses. A fixed-tap ring emits exponentially declining copies and needs a means to clear its tail between vectors. [Ekici et al., measured devices](https://doi.org/10.1002/qute.202300195) · [Measured optical replicas from a passive SiN ring](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-019-50691-2)

**Phase-coded fiber Bragg grating.** Lee et al. generated 64 time slots with a fixed phase code from a 2.5 ps pulse at 160 Gchip/s. For 64 echoes at 20 ps pitch, the single-reflection depth span `c × 63T/(2 n_g)` is 12.9 cm in fiber at group index 1.468. This moves the delay off chip and requires packaging and reflection routing. A possible ideal reflection response has 64 equal field taps ±1/√128 from a Rudin–Shapiro sequence. Its complementary polynomial obeys |P|² + |Q|² = 128, so |P/√128| ≤ 1 at all frequencies. This target puts 50% of an isolated packet’s energy into the 64 replicas. At 2 pJ input that is 15.6 fJ per copy before routing or fabrication loss. The FWM reference can undo the common sign of each echo. The tooth-dependent Walsh mask is separate. Device validation requires a measured or synthesized complex reflection response across the full 3.15 THz comb and occupied pulse sidebands, correct pulse shapes, and low leakage into adjacent frames. [Lee et al., experiment and Fig. 2](https://eprints.soton.ac.uk/13704/1/2177.pdf) · [Complementary sequences](https://doi.org/10.1109/TIT.1961.1057620)

Another layout saves area by using the output pulse interleaver in reverse as the next layer’s input pulse extender. Gate the calculated packet, apply the activation, and route it back through the same six delays in reverse. Budget return routing and allocate separate time windows for summing partial results and producing input copies. Any tooth-dependent masks in shared arms need a bypass during collection, or the input pulse extender needs an external mask. Double-pass division/recombination has experimental precedent in lasers. The switched multilayer connection and its isolation need their own design. The simulation checks the eight-group Walsh fields, equal-tap energy budgets and ideal grating response. [Double-pass pulse division](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4085786/) · [Numerical checks](hankel_temporal_combiner.py)

#### Amplifier placement across two or three multiplications

Use the balanced 64-mode test above, with 200 µW mean signal power per tooth at group boundaries, 15,600 signal photons per matched mode, a 5 dB noise figure, and ideal routing. For noise occupancy `m` above the coherent signal, each filtered phase-conjugating multiplication gives `m ← q(m+1)`. A phase-insensitive amplifier gives `m ← Gm+(FG−1)/2`, with `F = 10^0.5`. After k multiplications, gain `G = q^(−k)` restores the group input power. Placing that gain last adds `Σ[r=0…k−1] q^(−r) + (FG−1)/2` noise photons per output mode per group. The group noise powers add because the net signal power gain is one. [Amplifier noise, Eq. 13](https://perso.telecom-paristech.fr/gallion/documents/free_downloads_pdf/PG_revues/PG_R66.pdf) · [Parametric conversion noise](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.21.019437)

| Multiplications before gain | Gain per full group | Signal per tooth entering amplifier | Amplifier visits in 34 multiplications | Added RMS field error after one full group | Added RMS field error after 34 multiplications |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 1 | +6.28 dB | 47.1 µW | 34 | 2.15% | 12.5% |
| 2 | +12.55 dB | 11.1 µW | 17 | 4.61% | 19.0% |
| 3 | +18.83 dB | 2.62 µW | 12 | 9.59% | 31.9% |

The last row uses eleven triples and one final +6.28 dB restoration. With the same twelve amplifier visits, ten triples and two pairs give 31.0%. Even quantum-limited amplifiers placed after each pair or triple give 15.6% and 26.4%, respectively, for the regular schedules. The component gain is plausible. Nuphoton specifies 23 dB gain, 100 mW total output and a noise figure of at most 5 dB at its stated multichannel operating point. The model uses 12.8 mW total restored signal power. [Multichannel EDFA datasheet](https://nuphoton.com/datasheets/EDFA-CW-C4-MR-20-23-FCA.pdf)

**Gain between multiplications.** Amplifying after the first multiplication of each pair gives the mean tooth-power sequence 200 → 47.1 → 849 → 200 µW. With the same 17 amplifier visits the linear model gives **9.2%** added RMS field error after 34 multiplications. This spends more signal energy inside the guide. The amplifier delivers 54.3 mW across 64 teeth and the next pass must generate 12.8 mW of selected idlers. Each such idler consumes one reference photon. At the two-way carrier frequencies this takes at least 26% of the 50 mW reference photon flux before counting discarded idlers. Pump depletion and matrix distortion need evaluation at this operating point. A wavelength-selective branch through the amplifier on band B implements this placement on alternate laps. [Dual-pump photon conversion](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.21.019437)

In a three-pass group, gain after the second multiplication gives 15.6% field error at the same 54.3 mW peak total signal in the model. Gain after the first would raise the signal to 230 mW. The next pass would need 54.3 mW of selected idlers, exceeding the 49.1 mW upper bound from the 50 mW reference at the stated frequencies and equal interaction time. These pump budgets assume that the selected idlers start empty and are generated by the intended reference-plus-weight process.

## Recurrent memories and optical writes

In a product memory `S = L H(h) R`, one local change gives `ΔS = L H(Δh) R`. For invertible L and R an arbitrary key–value write would require `L⁻¹ v kᵀ R⁻¹` to be Hankel, which generally fails. An alternative is to store the entries of S as optical fields. The Hankel multiplier can still implement the projections learned offline. [Schlag et al., additive and delta fast weights](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html)

### Quadratic recurrent state in fiber

**Model target.** All sequence layers use recurrent state during decoding. Let d be hidden width, H the number of heads, p the value width per head and N the key/state width. A matrix-state layer holds H p N scalars per sequence. With H p proportional to d, fixed N gives linear state growth in d, and N proportional to d gives quadratic growth. Both have constant state in context length. A dense update and read visit the whole matrix per token. Mamba-2 usually holds N and p fixed and adds heads as d grows. Its inner recurrence is decayed linear attention. TTT-Linear uses H square p × p fast-weight matrices, so its storage is also linear in d when p stays fixed. These counts exclude the offline-trained projections. [Mamba-2, §7.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2405.21060#S7.SS2) · [TTT-Linear implementation](https://github.com/test-time-training/ttt-lm-pytorch/blob/main/ttt.py#L845-L854)

**Closest precedent.** Liu, Gao and Chen expanded the key width of purely recurrent gated linear attention (GLA) and Gated DeltaNet. At d = 1,024 their eightfold expansion uses 8 heads × 128 values × 1,024 key coordinates = **1,048,576 writable scalars per layer**, exactly d². Their kernel splits each expanded head into subheads, shares values across them, and sums their outputs. On FDA retrieval the 454M expanded Gated DeltaNet scored 38.93 versus 20.69 for a 455M model with smaller state. The 1.47B base scored 37.66 on FDA and scored higher on SWDE and SQuAD than the 454M expanded model. They also report a near-linear increase in the longest context reaching a specified UUID retrieval accuracy across the tested state sizes. Separately, Yang et al.'s standalone models at 8K scored 30.4% (Mamba-2), 98.8% (DeltaNet) and 91.8% (Gated DeltaNet) on passkey retrieval. On the harder 4K number-in-text task the scores were 56.2%, 18.6% and 92.2%. The number of competing writes, the write rule and target distance all matter. [Liu et al., §§4–5, Fig. 2 and Table 4](https://aclanthology.org/2025.acl-long.564.pdf) · [Yang et al., Table 2](https://arxiv.org/html/2412.06464v3#S3.SS2)

**Fiber candidate.** Partition the key coordinates into B banks of width r. Each stores a matrix S_b with d value rows and r columns. Set B = d/r for d² stored fields. At d = 768 and r = 64, 12 banks hold 589,824 fields. In the dense reference all banks participate. For selective access, train separate optical read and write addresses with support in one bank each. A write to bank b can add v kᵀ or correct its old prediction with `U = a S_b`, `S_b ← U + β(v − U k) kᵀ`. Bank c supplies the post-write output `y = S_c q`. Other banks retain their fields between accesses. With unit retention this is the ordinary delta rule for keys and queries supported on the selected blocks. Each active bank accesses d r entries. At fixed r the state arithmetic per token grows as d, excluding projections and routing. The bank restriction changes which associations the model can retrieve per token. Mixture-of-Memories is a purely recurrent precedent for learned routing, tested with two of four routed banks plus a shared bank. Optical address generation, contention and waiting for the selected packet require a design. [Mixture-of-Memories, §§3–4](https://arxiv.org/html/2502.13685)

**What to measure.** At the planned 130–150M scale, hold width, data, trainable parameter budget and training tokens as close as possible. Evaluate TTT with both factors adaptive in the eight parallel branches. Comparisons include the scheduled packet write, fixed-decay retention, compact Mamba-2 and state-expanded Gated DeltaNet. Sweep resident state at fixed active write count in the sparse variant. Measure held-out perplexity and recall while varying target distance and number of competing associations independently, including overwrites. Use the same access schedule, batch size, achieved precision and model for the silicon comparison. At 1,024² entries, an 8-bit digital representation needs 1 MiB per layer and sequence. On one 64-tooth optical lane with 20 ps slots the same field count occupies 16,384 slots, a 327.68 ns frame and about 67 m of fiber at group index 1.468. Sixteen parallel lanes shorten the frame to 20.48 ns at about 4.2 m each. These are ideal packed capacities. A single tap on a full loop waits about half a lap on average for a uniformly located slot. Include read/write access time, storage gain, accumulated amplitude/phase error, pump energy, and the cost of moving active entries from SRAM or HBM. [Corning fiber data](https://www.corning.com/media/worldwide/coc/documents/Fiber/product-information-sheets/PI-1424-AEN.pdf) · [NVIDIA H100 memory hierarchy](https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-hopper-architecture-in-depth/)

#### Writing a memory packet by gated replacement

**Published write rule.** Store column j of W as a comb packet cⱼ. The arriving token supplies an optical vector u from a learned projection and a scalar write fraction gⱼ per column. The update is `cⱼ⁺ = (1−gⱼ)cⱼ + gⱼ u`, with output `y = W⁺q`. This is HGRN2 Eq. 3 with g = 1−f. The authors trained purely recurrent language models at 150M, 1.3B and 2.7B parameters. The write gate depends on the incoming token. In a dense delta write, the new packet first depends on an entire prediction Wk and its residual. Here the same u can be prepared before any old column arrives. A full HGRN2 head still needs the ordinary query read and a copy or replay of u at each column being written. On a 64-column head, passive equal fan-out from one packet divides its power by 64 (18.1 dB per branch). [Qin et al., Eq. 3 and Tables 6–7](https://arxiv.org/html/2404.07904) · [Delta rule, Table 1](https://arxiv.org/html/2412.06464v3)

**HGRN to HGRN2.** HGRN keeps one running value per feature. Its learned lower bound on retention rises with layer depth, biasing upper layers toward slower forgetting. The original model also allowed complex phases. HGRN2 uses a real-valued state and expands each head to a matrix. Its effective linear-attention key is the write fraction g, tied to retention f = 1−g. The query is learned separately. With zero initial state, the contribution of a value written at token s to column j at token t is its write fraction gₛⱼ times the product of that column’s retentions from s+1 through t. Reading multiplies this contribution by qₜⱼ and sums over columns. [Original HGRN](https://papers.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2023/hash/694be3548697e9cc8999d45e8d16fe1e-Abstract-Conference.html) · [HGRN2, §§2–3 and Table 4](https://arxiv.org/html/2404.07904#S3)

**Published state size and recall.** With total recurrent width D and square heads of width p, each layer holds Dp scalars. At D = 768, twelve 64-wide heads hold 49,152 fields, six 128-wide heads hold 98,304, and one full-width head holds 589,824. The authors used p = 128 in the main experiments after observing diminishing perplexity gains above that width in their ablation. The paper also reports a 7B model trained on 300B tokens. In a separate easy needle-retrieval test up to 16K tokens, their 3B HGRN2 outperformed their Mamba comparison but both struggled. A wider fiber memory needs a direct recall test at its chosen precision. [HGRN2, Figs. 2 and 4 and Table 8](https://arxiv.org/html/2404.07904)

Raven's Eq. 18 uses the same scalar interpolation on selected key and value packets, with exact hold on unrouted slots. It supplies a language-model precedent for selective writes. Its learned router, two stored vectors per slot and softmax read over compressed slots are additional operations. The hard clocked factor write below trains a different readout. [Afzal et al., §4.1, preprint](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.25357v1#S4.SS1)

**Optical writer.** For a binary gate, connect the returning packet and the incoming packet at the same wavelengths to a broadband 2 × 2 bar/cross switch. In bar it returns the old packet to the loop. In cross it returns u to the loop and sends the displaced packet to the other port. An optically pumped fiber crossbar switched slots in 40 Gb/s data, using 69.7 pJ per π control pulse in that device. Five percent residual old-field amplitude at equal old and new powers requires at least 26 dB wrong-port power isolation after calibrating the desired path. [Zouraraki et al., §§3–4](https://dl.ifip.org/db/conf/ondm/ondm2007/ZourarakiBVA07.pdf)

For a continuous gate, a balanced coupler forms `(c+u)/√2` and `(c−u)/√2` after phase calibration. Give the difference arm real **field** transmission `t = 1−2g` and recombine. The loop port carries `[(c+u)+t(c−u)]/2 = (1−g)c+g u` on every tooth. An ideal interferometer with arm phases +θ and −θ gives t = cos θ. Hold and replace correspond to t = +1 and −1 relative to the sum arm. With 64 columns × 64 teeth over 256 random updates, this ideal transfer matched direct HGRN2 to 4.2 × 10⁻¹⁶ relative state error and 1.7 × 10⁻¹⁵ relative query error (maximum, NumPy seed 860). The calculation assumes the control is supplied.

**Generating the gate optically.** For one column with signed token projection z and retention floor β, HGRN2 requires `f = β + (1−β)σ(z)`. The difference arm therefore needs `t = 2f−1 = β + (1−β)tanh(z/2)`. A directly measured optical power loses the sign of z. A coherent bias before a power-sensitive control element can preserve the sign over a chosen range. Liu et al. measured a scalar sigmoid-like response near 1540 nm at a 30 fJ threshold, with about 4 ps response time for their resonant activator. A serial writer with 20 ps between column starts needs 50 billion independent gate settings per second and a calibrated field response across the 64 data teeth. [HGRN2, Eqs. 2–3](https://arxiv.org/html/2404.07904) · [Liu et al., Fig. 2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41377-025-02175-4)

**Absorber variant considered for HGRN2.** A separate optical control pulse could vary absorption in the difference arm. If the comb is a weak probe and its phase relative to the sum arm stays fixed, a normalized field transmission of 0 to 1 gives a write fraction `g = (1−t)/2` of 0 to ½. Zhou et al. measured optically controlled absorption across telecom wavelengths. Modeling this variant would require the probe phase, contrast and recovery at the chosen total comb power. For an idealized sequence of holds with residual write fraction ε, the surviving old-field coefficient is `(1−ε)ᴺ`. Retaining half after 1,000 holds requires ε ≤ 0.000693 before loop noise or loss. [Zhou et al., Figs. 2–4](https://doi.org/10.1063/5.0006596)

#### Writing a Hankel factor through a coupler

A fixed trained optical projection generates one 127-tooth packet uₜ on the weight band. Time it to meet factor `j = t mod 34` at a 2 × 2 coupler. After phase calibration the write port returns `hⱼ ← r hⱼ + s uₜ`, with `r² + s² = 1` for an ideal passive coupler. Only the packet contents depend on the token. The read sends the query through the 34 factors in a fixed order. The head stores 4,318 field amplitudes. A clocked full exchange overwrites a factor every 34 tokens. A partial write retains a decaying contribution from earlier visits. Test associative recall across successive writes to the same slot. [Forward reader](#repeated-hankel-multiplication-in-a-data-loop) · [Factor conditioning check](#other-forward-only-optical-memories)

**Cost of a passive tap.** Suppose all 34 packets pass the write tap once per token. An always-connected coupler multiplies every old packet by r each pass, including slots with a dark input. For an illustrative old-field half-life of 1,000 tokens, this gives `r = 2^(−1/1000)` and a write-port power fraction `s² = 0.139%` (−28.6 dB). A clocked coupler that bypasses unselected slots can apply the split only once per 34 tokens to each factor. The same half-life then gives `r = 2^(−34/1000)` and `s² = 4.60%` (−13.4 dB). This option requires a timed switch with a low-loss bypass. Both numbers describe one coupler and exclude fiber loss, read taps and amplifier noise.

**Comparison with a learned key.** RetNet's recurrent retention head writes `Sₜ = γ Sₜ₋₁ + vₜ kₜᵀ` and reads `yₜ = Sₜ qₜ` in column-vector notation with positional phases absorbed in k and q. A coupler can add an already generated update to directly stored entries. In the 34-factor representation, changing one factor changes the effective matrix by `L H(δh) R`, where L and R are the fixed products on either side. For signed-real fields the possible changes at that factor span at most 127 dimensions. The rank-one targets include all 4,096 single-entry basis matrices, which are linearly independent. Updating that one factor therefore cannot implement an arbitrary dense outer-product write. [RetNet, Eq. 6](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2307.08621) · [Dense write layout](#addressing-an-outer-product-in-fiber)

#### Forward-only writes in the repeating Hankel loop

**Decision.** The existing loop can learn a new association by changing its last factor. The recall test below limits the claim we can make for that write. A different encoding implements the full additive linear-attention numerator and the dense linear delta rule using only forward operations, at the price of two spectral registers and a scan of the entire weight bank. Keep the shared interleaver as a comparison. Offline model training can still use ordinary backpropagation. [Schlag et al., linear attention and the delta rule](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html)

For the original product, write the read matrix as `W = B H(h) A`, where A acts before the factor and B after it. Changing h by u changes W by exactly `B H(u) A`. A fixed choice of slot can change at most 127 independent matrix directions. At the last slot, B is the identity. FWM can form the exact local squared-error gradient from the forward factor input Ak and residual v−Wk. That statement concerns learning the factor. Equating its matrix change to the desired dense write would require `H(u) = B⁻¹ (v−Wk) kᵀ A⁻¹` for invertible A and B, which generally fails. The published DeltaProduct network also distinguishes token-dependent transition factors from the matrix state that receives value writes. [DeltaProduct, NeurIPS 2025](https://papers.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2025/hash/e1ea2520fbf9cd1600b287dde67e0a3c-Abstract-Conference.html)

**Recall on the original 34 slots.** Start at 34 central-tooth reversals. Write one new factor per association. Test 128 streams at each load with orthonormal real keys and independent unit values. Read after all writes and choose the closest stored value. The direct write encodes the value and key by convolution. The local delta write also runs the old product on the key and uses the prediction error and its factor input to program the newest factor. Both grant phase-aligned subtraction of the identity response from the read. The local test grants normalization by input intensity. Both use step 0.05, ideal fields, parity-specific tooth ordering and untrained token projections. [Simulation and step-size sweep](hankel_forward_write_recall.py)

| Associations | Direct convolution top-1 | Local delta top-1 | Local delta mean cosine with target | Dense delta top-1 |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 8 | 96.7% | 96.2% | 0.407 | 100% |
| 16 | 73.9% | 73.9% | 0.300 | 100% |
| 32 | 38.1% | 39.9% | 0.209 | 100% |
| 34 | 35.4% | 36.1% | 0.202 | 100% |

Around this initialization, a small change in any one slot contributes a Hankel times a reversal, or a reversal times a Hankel. Both are Toeplitz. All 34 slots therefore share the same 127 first-order directions. Higher-order products break that restriction. Learned initial factors or learned keys could give different recall. In a separate 64-write test of the direct, token-only rule, its 34 surviving factors retrieved 26.7% of their values from 64 candidates. The evicted entries scored 1.54% against 1.56% chance. A writer conditioned on the older state could carry information past eviction. Run the script with and without `--local-delta`.

##### Replacing an association in the original product

After filling 8, 16 or 32 of the 34 factors, revisit one randomly chosen key with a fresh independent value. The known-slot rule replaces the corresponding factor packet with the convolution of that key and its new value, assuming the address is supplied. The address-free local delta rule uses the prediction error to write the next unused factor. Both score the residual output of the product against the current values *and* the superseded value. The table averages 512 noiseless streams per load, with random orthonormal keys, random unit values, reversal initialization and step 0.05. “Retained” is the fraction of the other previously correct keys that still select their correct value. [Reproduce with --rewrite](hankel_forward_write_recall.py)

| Live associations | New value top-1, known slot | Previously correct others retained | New value top-1, next slot | Previously correct others retained |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 8 | 95.5% | 98.0% | 68.4% | 96.4% |
| 16 | 67.4% | 92.2% | 66.4% | 90.8% |
| 32 | 37.7% | 87.7% | 60.7% | 84.4% |

At eight keys the next-slot rule returned the superseded value in 31.1% of trials. At 32 keys its overall top-1 on the other keys dropped from 37.9% to 35.7%. For both rules at all three loads, the RMS field displacement *per untouched key* was 62–64% of the displacement on the updated key, pooled across trials. This measures write interference on analog outputs. A correct nearest-value decision can survive that displacement. A dense delta write on orthogonal keys has zero displacement on the untouched keys. These checks cover one replacement per stream before the 34 slots fill. They do not model learned key or value projections, long-running eviction, optical noise, or language-model performance.

**Increasing the matrix from 64 × 64 to 128 × 128.** Repeat the same 512-stream overwrite experiment per load with a 128-component key and value, 255 teeth per factor, and the original step size. The enlarged bank uses 66 factor slots, sufficient for 64 writes plus one extra factor for the next-slot rule. At 8, 16 and 32 keys the ideal results would be identical with only 34 slots at width 128: the extra 32 untouched reversal matrices multiply to the identity. This equality concerns the ideal transfer. Additional illuminated visits would incur loss, latency and noise. The percentage columns below rank the new value against all current values and the superseded value. “Other keys” counts all untouched responses after the rewrite, including those already incorrect before it. [Reproduce with --rewrite --width 128](hankel_forward_write_recall.py)

| Live associations | Known slot, new value top-1 at 64 / 128 | Next slot, new value top-1 at 64 / 128 | Other keys top-1 at 128, known / next slot | Known-slot mean cosine with new value at 64 / 128 |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 8 | 95.5% / 99.8% | 68.4% / 79.7% | 99.7% / 99.4% | 0.401 / 0.398 |
| 16 | 67.4% / 94.5% | 66.4% / 81.2% | 92.6% / 89.5% | 0.287 / 0.298 |
| 32 | 37.7% / 61.1% | 60.7% / 83.6% | 62.2% / 57.4% | 0.207 / 0.207 |
| 64 | — / 25.2% | — / 85.4% | 27.7% / 26.9% | — / 0.141 |

At 16 associations, the 128-wide known-slot result falls from 94.5% to 71.9% when the scorer uses every other output coordinate and renormalizes the candidate values. At 32 associations it falls from 61.1% to 35.5%. The stored matrix and the 128-coordinate output are exactly the same in this control. Much of the top-1 improvement comes from distinguishing candidate values across more coordinates. With the number of associations doubled as well, the known-slot rule goes from 95.5% at 8/64 to 94.5% at 16/128, from 67.4% at 16/64 to 61.1% at 32/128, and from 37.7% at 32/64 to 25.2% at 64/128. The high-load next-slot rule preferentially recalls the freshly updated key.

The pooled ratio of the RMS field change on one other key to the change on the updated key remained 62.5–63.6% at width 64 and 62.9–63.8% at width 128. There is also an analytic check on the direct writer with all surrounding factors set to reversals. Let D be the Toeplitz change in reversal-corrected coordinates, whose lag coefficients are the correlation of the new-minus-old value with k, and take q uniformly orthogonal to k. For independent unit isotropic values and a unit isotropic key, the pooled ratio √(E‖Dq‖²/E‖Dk‖²) equals √[(d+1)(2d−1)/(5d²+3d+1)]. It is 63.19% at d=64 and 63.22% at d=128. For a single unit value, summing squared lag coefficients with their diagonal multiplicities gives E‖D‖²_F=(2d²+1)/(3d). The key autocorrelation gives E‖Dk‖²=1+(d−1)(2d−1)/[3d(d+2)]. The mean over orthogonal probes is the difference of those two expectations divided by d−1. Replacing one independent value with another multiplies both energies by two. Populated neighboring factors introduce extra product terms, which are included in the table's numerical experiment.

**A dense matrix stored as differences between neighboring entries.** For head width d store d packets, one per matrix column c, in descending column order. Tooth i in that packet holds `h_c[i] = W[i,c] − W[i+1,c+1]`, with zeros beyond the matrix edges. These d² fields independently specify W. One traveling packet contains an answer band initialized to zero and a query band initialized to q. Each Hankel visit's central pump tooth preserves both logical fields after allowing for the physical reversal and conjugation. Another d pump teeth add an upper-triangular Toeplitz transform of the query into the answer. After the addition, shift only the query up by one tooth and discard the overflow. The same answer stays in the loop. There is no external temporal summer.

For example, for `W = [[a,b],[c,d]]` the first packet stores `[b,d]` and adds `[d q₀ + b q₁, d q₁]`. The next sees the shifted query `[0,q₀]` and adds `[(a−d)q₀,c q₀]`. The final answer is Wq. In general the upper-triangular matrix in visit l has element `T_l[i,j] = h_c[d−1+i−j]` for j≥i, c=d−1−l. With S the zero-fill upshift, the matrix is the sum of `T_l S^l`. Each entry telescopes along one diagonal of W. In physical coordinates the antisymmetric choice of sideband positions between successive visits compensates the index reversal, and alternate slots use conjugated coefficients. [Literal phase-conjugating Hankel simulation](hankel_displacement_memory.py)

**Online write.** For additive attention use e=v. For unit-step delta use e=v−Wk after one ordinary forward read on k. A scalar learning rate can multiply e. The column packet increment at tooth i is

```
Δh_c[i] = e[i] conj(k[c]) − e[i+1] conj(k[c+1]).
```

The two terms can land on the same FWM idler. Present the full residual comb and two adjoining key tones with a fixed relative minus sign. Pair the upper key with the next residual tooth. The opposite conjugate sideband serves the other slot parity, assuming the appropriate carrier placements and phase matching. A coherent injector adds the increment to the returning weight packet. Repeat for every c; a query after the write is an ordinary forward read on q. The hardware must keep the key and residual available across the scan, present successive pairs of key teeth to the same two-tone selector, isolate the intended idlers, and replenish the optical state. There is no intermediate factor activation or backward error to save. Additive attention here denotes the unnormalized numerator. A model with a denominator needs a separate running key sum and normalization.

**Verification and cost.** For a width-64 head, 64 random orthonormal complex keys with independent values followed by eight replacement writes gave relative RMS field error under 3×10⁻¹⁵ on recalled values and independent queries against dense delta. This tests the ideal matrix algebra including FWM field conjugation. The two-band version uses 2d live data modes, d stored side coefficients per packet, d programmable FWM visits per read, and d write visits per dense update. A 32×32 head fits on the old 64-mode data grid. A 64×64 head uses 128 modes spanning 6.35 THz at 50 GHz pitch. A signed-real model can use real coefficients. With distinct key and query, a complete width-64 delta step uses 64 visits to predict, 64 to write and 64 for the post-write read, plus field preparation. That count assumes a separate one-tooth shifter on the query band. The physical shift direction alternates with slot parity. The compact loop's gain, loaded conversion, amplifier noise and electrical energy must be recalculated for this operating mode. [Existing forward-energy assumptions](#repeated-hankel-multiplication-in-a-data-loop)

**The physical choice is how to shift the query while preserving the answer.** A band-selective frequency shifter saves a guide visit per slot. Hu et al. demonstrated resonant frequency shifting with near-unity on-chip efficiency up to 28 GHz. Broadband translation of all active query teeth by 50 GHz in this loop remains a design assumption. In one width-64 unitary read, a fixed quadrature residual carrier at −50 or −40 dBc gave 12.6% or 35.1% held-out RMS error after fitting a single complex gain. For this known, time-invariant shifter model, re-encoding the full bank recovered the ideal read to roundoff and raised the squared norm of the programmed side coefficients to 1.24 or 2.35 times its original value. That correction mixes weight slots, so an online writer would need to generate the compensated increments as well. These results exclude fluctuating errors and amplifier noise. [Hu et al., frequency shifter](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-021-03999-x) · [Shifter model and calibration](hankel_displacement_memory.py)

The same guide can alternatively make the shift with a second, fixed two-tone Hankel conversion per slot. The checked layout uses two d-tooth registers and d−1 dark guard teeth between them, with an internal stop band after each visit. For d=64 that is 191 bin positions over 9.50 THz and 128 conversions per read. A separate guarded design applies a fixed relative phase ramp to the query after each visit and uses d coded weight packets. It also produced exact ideal dense writes and reads, including overwrites on nonorthogonal keys. It takes 64 conversions per read but requires a coded copy of the key and the full residual for all 64 writes. Its 0.3125 ps relative delay per visit accumulates to 19.69 ps between first and last contributions at 50 GHz pitch, comparable to a 20 ps data packet. The fixed-bin simulation does not establish whole-packet fidelity. [Two-tone shift](hankel_displacement_memory.py) · [Guarded phase-coded test](hankel_guarded_product.py)

**What fits the original spectral grid.** The 32 × 32 frequency-shift design used 64 data teeth, up to 127 pump-frequency positions across the two parities, and 1,024 independent writable fields. On a separate width-32 test, 32 associations and eight overwrites gave 1.54×10⁻¹⁵ relative error on independent queries. In the same stationary shifter-error model at this width, −50 and −40 dBc residual carriers gave 6.56% and 19.52% calibrated field error on one random unitary. Chen et al. demonstrated optical frequency shifting at 50 GHz. Their reported suppressions of the carrier and unwanted sideband are about 17–23 dB. The loop needs the full complex shifter transfer on its loaded comb, including each occupied tooth's unwanted sidebands. Signals generated one tooth apart can overlap coherently on later laps. [Chen et al., Fig. 4](https://scholars.cityu.edu.hk/ws/portalfiles/portal/282798799/optica-12-5-666.pdf)

With an addressed one-hot key at j, only packet j receives e and packet j−1 receives minus e shifted up by one tooth, omitting the second packet at j=0. The read still accepts a dense q. This could reduce a write to two packet changes if the address and timing are supplied. A learned one-hot key changes the model and still needs a way to select an optical slot. The exact full-key result above assumes neither sparse keys nor an address decoder.

##### Energy of the two-band displacement multiplier

**Workload.** One isolated batch-one read, one resident dense real matrix with orthonormal columns, one output at the same mean signal energy as the input. A head of width d holds 2d data modes, makes d FWM visits and delivers d² useful real MACs. A 64-tooth implementation gives 1,024 MACs in 32 visits. A 128-tooth implementation gives 4,096 MACs in 64 visits. At 50 GHz pitch their data spans are 3.15 and 6.35 THz; the union of weight-frequency positions across the two parities spans 6.30 and 12.70 THz. The conventional raw product used 34 visits at 64 data teeth for one 64 × 64 read. Its earlier 156 fJ/MAC electrical sensitivity and 31% calibrated deterministic error concern a different encoding. [Read schematic](../figures/displacement_fwm_multiplier_layout.svg) · [Earlier 34-pass calculation](#repeated-hankel-multiplication-in-a-data-loop)

**Optimistic electrical estimates per completed read.** These target 10% RMS added field noise from the conjugating FWM and optical amplifier. Readout distortion is tested separately below. Each row optimizes constant reference and central-pilot powers for the stated modeled loss. Sixteen random signed-real orthogonal matrices per width and isotropic inputs set the noise and optical load. In the 4.7 dB row the single shared gain stage precedes the band split; the answer arm incurs the same loss as the translated query so both fields remain correctly scaled. Upper and lower parasitic sidebands are suppressed ideally in this calculation. [Energy and noise calculation](hankel_displacement_energy.py)

| Read assumptions | 32 × 32 energy | 32 × 32 fJ/MAC | 64 × 64 energy | 64 × 64 fJ/MAC |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Ideal lossless shift, free RF | 0.418 nJ | 408 | 1.280 nJ | 312 |
| Ideal lossless shift, continuous 50 GHz RF | 0.739 nJ | 722 | 1.922 nJ | 469 |
| 4.7 dB matched loss, continuous RF | 0.946 nJ | 924 | 2.781 nJ | 679 |

The last row spends respectively 401 / 308 fJ/MAC for both pumps and replenishing the tapped weight fields, 209 / 214 for signal amplification, and 314 / 157 for the RF supply. The optical input packet is 0.211 / 0.480 pJ. The 32/64 reference powers are both 100 mW and their central pilot powers are 13.40 / 26.84 mW. Each weight side tooth consumes pilot power times the squared magnitude of the stored coefficient. The optimizer caps the sum of pilot and all side teeth at 100 mW for every tested slot. For the lossless rows the optimum references are 71.08 / 100 mW and pilots 9.22 / 15.64 mW, with 0.396 / 0.711 pJ input. At 15% added field noise the matched-loss, continuous-RF row becomes 777 / 537 fJ/MAC before deterministic distortion. In the alternative model with spontaneous FWM from all phase-matched seed frequencies reaching the selected output, the 10% matched-loss row becomes 970 / 768 fJ/MAC. The actual bandwidth selects which vacuum modes couple.

**Component assumptions.** The reference and fixed pilot are produced directly in synchronized pulses of 24 ps equivalent energy, at 40% assumed electrical-to-guide efficiency. The adaptive weight teeth illuminate each 20 ps packet. Their store uses a 25% tap, 0.5 dB delivery loss, 0.2 dB other weight-loop loss, and 12.1% electrical-to-added-light efficiency. The same efficiency is applied optimistically to signal gain, with 5 dB noise figure and 95% DC distribution. Lei et al. measured −7.3 dB peak conversion with one CW pump and a 2.12 cm guide. Here the extrapolated two-pump coefficient at 50 mW each is 0.468 for one pump tooth and a weak signal. Cheung et al. measured 12.1% in a loaded amplifier. Pulse efficiency, gain flatness across the two alternately used carrier bands, multiple inactive storage round trips, extra amplifier bias, switching, cooling, source locking and output interface overheads have no additional allowance. Input and weight preparation are amortized. [Lei et al., Fig. 2](https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Cheung et al., optical amplifier](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26368213/)

Chen et al. applied about 10 mW of RF at 50 GHz. At an assumed 30% DC-to-RF efficiency, charge this over a minimum 286 ps per visit, based only on transit through the 2.12 cm waveguide at group index 4.04. That is 9.53 pJ per visit before DC distribution. The final shift can be skipped for answer-only extraction; stopping the RF that quickly would save at most one visit's RF allowance. A longer physical loop adds continuous power cost. Interleaving independent packets would require a compatible weight schedule and gain recovery. A conventional lossless sinusoidal IQ modulator puts at most about 34% of the query light in the desired first sideband, giving the 4.7 dB case above. Higher-order spurs remain even at that optimum. Achieving the peak conversion and the assumed drive power together across the proposed comb is a device target. [Chen et al., §2 and Fig. 4](https://scholars.cityu.edu.hk/ws/portalfiles/portal/282798799/optica-12-5-666.pdf) · [Kodigala et al., IQ transfer and measured conversion](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2204.12537)

**Accuracy of the budgeted read.** A four-field FWM propagation stress test used three independent dense real orthogonal matrices per width. For each matrix six calibration vectors fit a single output gain, then six held-out signed vectors tested it. At the lossless-shift budget points, calibrated deterministic field errors were 37.2–38.4% at width 32 and 81.8–85.4% at width 64 before adding noise. At the pump and packet powers from the 4.7 dB row, the same gain-compensated nonlinear check yielded 56.9–59.9% and 92.7–93.8%, even granting perfect rejection of unwanted sidebands. Reproduce those points with `--fine --case 32 100 13.398 .2112` and `--fine --case 64 100 26.839 .47993`. The small-signal path recovered the ideal dense results to roundoff. The nonlinear model propagates the actual full temporal field in each 20 ps comb period and permits pump depletion, with ideal filters, gain and shifter, uniform phase matching, and no SPM or XPM. No per-packet nonlinear predistortion was applied. The linear budget calls for generated idler energy comparable to the available weight pump on the busiest width-64 visits even for isotropic data. The reported supply figures therefore do not establish a 10%-total-error operating point. A true efficiency comparison needs a calibrated operating point meeting the same dense-read error at both widths. [Pump-depletion test](hankel_displacement_depletion.py)

At fixed per-visit energy E in pJ, the read costs 1,000 E/d fJ per useful MAC. Independent noise is added to each answer tooth on successive visits, and query noise also feeds later partial sums. Holding pump transfer fixed over increasing d therefore requires increasing photons per tooth to retain accuracy. This is why scaling the number of teeth does not guarantee the same saving in signal amplification. The 200 fJ/MAC target allows just 6.4 / 12.8 pJ per visit in the two sizes. Even with ideal lossless shifting and a free RF source, the optimized remaining terms spend 13.1 / 20.0 pJ per visit at the modeled noise target. For a delta token with an independent post-write query, energy is two reads plus one separate d-slot write. Write energy also depends on the selected key-pair power, residual replay and coherent injection. These read prices exclude it. Run `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1 python3 notes/hankel_displacement_energy.py` (options `--rf-mw 0`, `--error .15`, `--all-vacuum`) and `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1 python3 notes/hankel_displacement_depletion.py --fine`.

#### Eight parallel Hankel pairs as recurrent memory

Consider one signed-real 64 × 64 block with `W = Σᵦ H(bᵦ)H(aᵦ)`, full intermediate bands and a coherent spatial sum. The displacement bound applies to this local block before a Monarch shuffle. Eight branches use 16 Hankel stages at depth two and 2,032 coefficients if both factors vary. Thirty-two branches suffice for every local matrix in the ideal model. At fixed branch count B the storage grows as O(Bn) with block width n. Choosing B = n/8 retains quadratic scaling per growing block. Repeating fixed-width heads scales memory linearly with their number. [Local construction in the source bundle, limitations.md §7](../archive/README.md#original-notes-code-and-results) · [Monarch block structure](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v162/dao22a.html)
