**TTT write with both factors adaptive.** On branch r let Aᵣ = H(aᵣ), Bᵣ = H(bᵣ) and zᵣ = Aᵣk. The coherent sum gives the prediction ŷ = Σᵣ Bᵣzᵣ. Form the shared residual e = v−ŷ. For the loss ½‖e‖² and calibrated real fields, the exact gradient-descent writes are

| Writable comb | Increment divided by the step size | Fields mixed in the FWM writer |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Second factor bᵣ | conv(e, zᵣ) | Shared residual and saved branch intermediate |
| First factor aᵣ | conv(Bᵣᵀe, k) | Residual propagated through the old second factor and saved key |

Each full convolution returns 127 teeth. Writing coefficient s requires the sum of the products with i+j=s, which arrive on the same idler. The real Hankel Bᵣ is symmetric, so the local adjoint uses its existing coefficient order. Retain k and zᵣ and compute both increments against the pre-write coefficients. All eight branches share the residual and can perform their local adjoints in parallel. For two adaptive Monarch banks around a physical shuffle, propagate the downstream bank's error through the calibrated shuffle adjoint to obtain the upstream error. The field equations assume calibrated path gains and phases. Residual fan-out, delays and write signal-to-noise ratio enter the hardware budget. Central differences at all 2,032 coefficients gave relative errors of 6.95 × 10⁻¹¹ and 6.24 × 10⁻¹¹ for the first and second factor gradients respectively (seed 85). [Gradient check](reduced_hankel_memory.py) · [FWM field convention](#backpropagation-and-the-ttt-weight-update) · [TTT objective](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v267/sun25h.html)

**Representable maps.** Let J reverse the teeth. One pair implements any Toeplitz map T as (TJ)J, including the identity. For zero-based indices, define `D(W)[i,j] = W[i+1,j+1] − W[i,j]` on the 63 × 63 interior. For H(b)H(a), the sum over the intermediate tooth telescopes to `b[i+n]a[j+n] − b[i]a[j]`. Therefore `rank D(W) ≤ 2B = 16` while W can have rank 64. For a target W*, half the Frobenius norm of the singular-value tail of D(W*) beyond 16 lower-bounds the best eight-branch matrix error, since `‖D(E)‖F ≤ 2‖E‖F`. A small tail alone gives no error guarantee. Low-displacement weight matrices have precedent in trained feed-forward models. [Sindhwani et al.](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2015/hash/851300ee84c2b80ed40f51ed26d866fc-Abstract.html)

**Exact linear attention over a short window.** Set P(u) = H([n−1 zeros, u]) and Zu = (0,u₀,…,uₙ₋₂). The identity `v kᵀ = P(v)P(k) − P(Zv)P(Zk)` follows by canceling the shifted terms. Two branches can encode one arbitrary key–value pair directly. Eight can hold four such pairs with a scheduled overwrite. This requires a one-tooth shift in the programmed spectra, phase-controlled subtraction and a power budget for cancellation. There are longer write sequences outside the family. Nine unit-weight updates with v = k at basis positions 1,3,…,17 produce a diagonal state with 18 nonzero diagonal differences. The resulting operator is impossible with eight pairs on all queries. This gives no ceiling on recall for a restricted query distribution. [Fast weights and linear attention](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html) · [Archived identity, recurrent_update_checks.py](../archive/README.md#original-notes-code-and-results)

**Fixed-first-factor recall comparison.** An earlier experiment fixed the first factors at random and updated 1,016 second-factor fields. With fixed first factors the loss is a convex quadratic in those writable coefficients. Three seeds used independent Gaussian fixed references, orthonormal keys and random unit values. The online writer saw each association once, with one scalar step size selected on separate calibration sequences. At 8, 12 and 32 associations, median relative retrieval errors were 0.889, 0.922 and 0.978. Joint digital least squares on the same associations gave errors below 10⁻¹³ at 8 and 12, and 0.714 at 32. A dense delta write fits these orthonormal keys exactly in one pass. Recall with both factors adaptive is a separate measurement. [Reproducible check](reduced_hankel_memory.py)

#### How much control does a writable Hankel block need?

A dense 64 × 64 delta memory can write arbitrary values against 64 orthonormal keys with one unit-step update per key. Exact retrieval over every such history requires all 4,096 entries to be independently specifiable in aggregate. A TTT learner can instead optimize its loss directly over 2,032 writable fields. With the eight full-band pairs above it can still produce 64 independent output directions. The restriction becomes visible in how matrix entries change from one diagonal to the next. At a fixed state, if all future queries live in a subspace with orthonormal basis Q, storing WQ reproduces every required read. Successive exact dense delta writes also require control over the write keys on which the prediction residual depends. [Schlag et al., ICML 2021](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html) · [Sun et al., ICML 2025](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v267/sun25h.html)

In particular, if k = Qκ and q = Qξ, the smaller state C = WQ obeys the exact projected delta rule C ← C + β(v−Cκ)κᵀ and reads Cξ. For additive linear attention replace the residual by v. Tokenwise scalar retention also commutes with this projection. This gives a sufficient condition for exact compression, even with arbitrary 64-dimensional values. It also supplies a 64 × 32 digital control for an eight-pair block. The 2,032 comb fields count physical coordinates. The effective matrix family has dimension at most 1,887, since 127 boundary entries and a 63 × 63 displacement of rank at most 16 determine the whole block.

The pair has a direct attention interpretation. For intermediate tooth ℓ, let aᵣ[ℓ:ℓ+n] and bᵣ[ℓ:ℓ+n] denote length-n windows of the two combs. Its contribution to the output is the vector bᵣ[ℓ:ℓ+n] times the scalar aᵣ[ℓ:ℓ+n]ᵀq. Summing over ℓ and r gives the physical two-stage read exactly. The 512 overlapping key and value windows share coefficients. During a delta write, both factors change from the same old residual. Only the second factor carries an adjoint signal. Updating the factors changes their product through both first-order terms and the product of their increments. Thus the implemented learner follows its own TTT trajectory.

**Evidence from recurrent language models.** Nazari and Rusch reduced the key dimension of a pretrained purely recurrent Gated DeltaNet-1.3B. After recovery fine-tuning, 4K single-needle accuracy was 81.3% at full size, 77.1% after 20% gradient-selected pruning, and 65.7% after 50%. Wan et al. compressed Gated DeltaNet in a fixed subspace and found that the choice of subspace affected retrieval markedly at the same budget. In the other direction, Liu et al. reported FDA recall scores of 20.69 and 38.93 for comparably sized 455M and 454M Gated DeltaNets with different state sizes. Thomas et al. trained low-displacement *fixed weights* for language modeling, a precedent for learning the feature basis. These experiments motivate testing structured fast weights on language and recall separately. [Nazari and Rusch, Table 11, v3 preprint](https://arxiv.org/html/2602.04852v3) · [Wan et al., Table 1, preprint](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07552v1) · [Liu et al., ACL 2025, Table 4](https://aclanthology.org/2025.acl-long.564.pdf) · [Thomas et al., NeurIPS 2018](https://papers.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2018/hash/8e621619d71d0ae5ef4e631ad586334f-Abstract.html)

**Next-token prediction with causal writes.** Replace the fast matrix in all 144 heads of the published 125M-class TTT-Linear Books model. Each model sees the same 12 disjoint WikiText-2 test articles, reset at article boundaries with 1,088 tokens each, or 13,044 scored next tokens. Four separate validation articles set the step multiplier. Projections, gates, normalizations and the outer LM stay at their pretrained settings. The fast bias still updates. All variants use the reference 16-token minibatch and causally apply each prefix of its gradients. Both Hankel factors use the old local weights for their gradients and the updated pair for the query. The digital full-width update matched the reference implementation across 12 layers within 3.9 × 10⁻⁶ in field value on the validation check. [Online implementation](ttt_online_eval.py) · [Results and paired article bootstrap intervals](hankel_results/lm_summary.csv) · [Comparison figure](hankel_results/memory_comparison.svg)

For a complete Hankel replacement, the initial eight pairs approximate each pretrained W₀. One fit minimizes full-matrix error, a second weights key and query directions observed on the validation articles, and a third also rotates the input and output coordinates using validation covariances. In the third case, two fixed 64 × 64 orthogonal transforms per head sit outside the writable bank. The projected 64 × 32 comparator uses a fixed key basis learned from the validation queries. In a separate control each fast memory represents only a correction to a fixed dense W₀ used on every read. All reported state counts exclude the shared 64-scalar fast bias per head. Only the factors, projected matrix or dense matrix counted as writable receive matrix updates.

| Matrix used by each head | Writable matrix scalars | Test NLL, nats/token | Test perplexity |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Original dense TTT | 4,096 | 3.263 | 26.14 |
| 64 × 32 projected memory | 2,048 | 3.462 | 31.88 |
| Eight Hankel pairs | 2,032 | 4.052 | 57.52 |
| Eight Hankel pairs, calibrated initial fit | 2,032 | 4.032 | 56.39 |
| Eight Hankel pairs, calibrated initial fit in rotated coordinates | 2,032 | 4.120 | 61.56 |
| Fixed dense W₀ + 64 × 32 projected correction | 2,048 | 3.332 | 27.99 |
| Fixed dense W₀ + rank-16 two-factor correction | 2,048 | 3.738 | 42.02 |
| Fixed dense W₀ + eight-pair correction | 2,032 | 4.002 | 54.69 |
| Fixed dense W₀ with bias adaptation alone | 0 | 4.598 | 99.32 |

Allowing the calibrated factors to update reduced perplexity from 99.96 to 56.39 relative to freezing those same factors, with fast bias adaptation in both runs. Dense TTT incurred less loss on every test article. The mean Hankel penalty was 0.769 nats/token, with a document-resampled 95% bootstrap interval of 0.673–0.863. Rotating the coordinates by input and target covariance worsened the eight-pair result to 61.56. Retaining an exact copy of the dense W₀ also left a large penalty for the eight-pair writer. A 64 × 32 state worked substantially better at essentially the same mutable storage. These scores measure post-training replacement on 12 passages in ideal digital arithmetic. Training the projections and fast-weight initialization together with the optical constraint could change the ordering.

**States reached by a pretrained TTT-Linear model.** Twelve disjoint WikiText-2 test articles, 12 layers and 12 heads gave 1,728 native 64 × 64 matrices at each prefix. The median ordinary rank needed to retain 95% of squared singular-value energy in the change Wₜ−W₀ was 8, 13 and 20 after 64, 256 and 1,024 tokens. The median was 37 at all three prefixes for Wₜ including its learned initial matrix. At 1,024 tokens, the rigorous eight-pair Frobenius error floor was 23.6% of the change for a zero-initialized structured addition to W₀, and 30.0% of Wₜ when fitting the whole matrix. A moving pair bank initialized at nonzero factors has rank D(Wₜ) ≤ 16 but rank D(Wₜ−W₀) can reach 32. [Spectrum results](hankel_results/ttt_spectra.csv) · [Checkpoint conversion](https://huggingface.co/RetentionLabs/TTT-Linear-125M-Base-Books-2k/tree/8925d60c24c836b38952e9ef5ecdc445156a7884) · [WikiText-2 test data](https://huggingface.co/datasets/Salesforce/wikitext/tree/b08601e04326c79dfdd32d625aee71d232d685c3/wikitext-2-raw-v1)

At 1,024 tokens the median full-matrix error floor falls to 21.1% for 12 pairs (3,048 fields) and 14.0% for 16 pairs (4,064 fields). Sixteen pairs already cost nearly as much mutable storage as 4,096 dense entries. Holding eight branches fixed makes storage linear in width. Taking one branch for every eight channels gives n²/2 − n/4 stored fields for width n divisible by eight. [Branch-budget sweep](hankel_results/ttt_spectra.csv)

The next table measures the contribution of the saved change to 64 subsequent queries. The denominator is ‖(Wₜ−W₀)Q‖F. Freeze the snapshot, fit its change with two L-BFGS starts, then query it. The future queries were unavailable to the fitter. Each cell is a median across the same 72 snapshots (all 12 articles, layers 0, 5 and 11, heads 0 and 6). For the query-weighted fits, other articles supplied the metric and an optional shared rotation of key and value coordinates. Rank-16 SVD uses 2,048 writable scalars. A fixed 32-dimensional key basis also uses 2,048 writable scalars and a shared static projection. These are offline estimates of achievable read fidelity. [Unweighted fits](hankel_results/ttt_fit_summary.csv) · [Article-disjoint calibration and fit results](hankel_results/ttt_calibrated_summary.csv)

| Relative field error on future queries | 64 tokens | 256 tokens | 1,024 tokens |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Eight pairs, uniform full-matrix fit | 6.2% | 8.8% | 18.9% |
| Eight pairs, query-weighted fit in calibrated coordinates | 4.1% | 6.4% | 11.2% |
| Rank-16 factors, query-weighted SVD | 1.0% | 2.5% | 5.0% |
| Dense values in a fixed 32-dimensional key subspace | 4.3% | 5.2% | 8.2% |

**Learning with both combs writable.** Each episode starts at zero effective W. The two factors in every Hankel branch start nonzero with branch pairs canceling. Each example gives one squared-error gradient step, with the scalar step selected on 12 independent calibration episodes. The following are medians on 32 fresh episodes after 64 writes. Fresh isotropic query error is exactly the relative Frobenius matrix error for these teacher tasks when computed as a ratio of expected squared norms followed by a square root. Row and column rotations preserve every singular value and change the basis independently per episode. [Online experiment and controls](hankel_online_recall.py) · [Results and error quantiles](hankel_results/online_recall.csv)

| Unknown map generating key–value examples | Dense delta | Eight Hankel pairs | Single Toeplitz |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Toeplitz | 60.4% | 12.1% | 1.8% |
| Eight-pair map with random factors | 60.5% | 31.3% | 49.5% |
| Same eight-pair maps after rotations | 60.5% | 98.9% | 99.4% |

The eight-pair learner generalized better than dense delta when multiple observations informed shared coefficients. For arbitrary one-shot associations, 32 independent unit targets at orthogonal keys gave 47.5% nearest-value recall for the eight pairs and 100% for dense delta. At 64 associations the scores were 26.3% and 100%. A rank-16 two-factor writer reached 80.1% at 64 associations. Nearest-value recall tests identification, while the CSV also reports relative vector error. Step sizes and initial references were fixed before evaluation. For the optional reversal-biased Hankel initialization, two of 32 Toeplitz episodes diverged. The tabulated eight-pair initialization completed every episode.

**An exact linear-attention family for this bank.** For each of seven independent pairs of scalar streams, store lag vectors vₜ[i] = γ^(i/2) uₜ₋ᵢ and kₜ[j] = γ^(j/2) sₜ₋ⱼ from zero prehistory. The dense numerator follows Sₜ = γSₜ₋₁ + Σᵣ vᵣₜ kᵣₜᵀ. With F(x) = H([x, n−1 zeros]), the matrix Sₜ − Σᵣ F(vᵣₜ)F(kᵣₜ) is Toeplitz. Indeed Sₜ[i+1,j+1] = γSₜ₋₁[i,j], and the interior diagonal difference of each F(v)F(k) is −v[i]k[j]. The eighth pair implements the Toeplitz remainder as (TJ)J. Its 127 diagonal coefficients accumulate correlations involving the samples leaving the lag vectors. The 64-dimensional query can be arbitrary. A 512-token check with γ = 0.98 achieved relative error below 2.4 × 10⁻¹⁵ and ordinary rank 64. This is an exact additive linear-attention example with seven fresh key scalars and seven fresh value scalars per token. It requires lag shifts and a separate update to the shared correlations. Its recall and optical cost would need their own tests. [Construction and independent dense recurrence](reduced_hankel_memory.py)

The lag construction needs only 7 × 2 × 64 past samples plus 127 correlations, or 1,023 numbers. Its full-rank read alone therefore provides little justification for a large quadratic optical memory.

**Implication for the optical architecture.** Eight branches store 16 full-band combs and compute 16 convolution writes with one local adjoint per branch. This saves 50.4% of the writable matrix coordinates at equal precision. The universal construction in the same optical layout takes 32 parallel branches for worst-case 64 × 64 maps. Beam splitting, coherent summation, tap loss, gain and write noise determine the energy per useful association. The comparison for a paper is a jointly trained eight-pair memory, full dense delta and a dense 64 × 32 memory at approximately equal state. Report held-out next-token loss and multi-query associative recall across the number of independent associations and overwrites. A fixed trained optical projection or an additional static dense path has its own area and read cost. [Local gradient and footprint](#eight-parallel-hankel-pairs-as-recurrent-memory) · [Arora et al., MQAR, ICLR 2024](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/448fc91f669c15d10364ee01d512cc10-Abstract-Conference.html)

**Reproducing the experiments.** Put the pinned checkpoint's `config.json`, `modeling_ttt.py`, `model.safetensors` and `tokenizer.json` in a scratch directory. Add the WikiText-2 raw v1 test and validation Parquet shards as `wiki-test.parquet` and `wiki-validation.parquet`. The extractor verifies their SHA-256 digests. Set `DATA` to that directory and `OUT` to an output directory. The reference forward used PyTorch 2.7.0, Transformers 4.56.2 and CPU float32 with the downloaded bfloat16 weights. The preparation step also needs PyArrow and tokenizers. The numerical fits use NumPy and SciPy. The final command uses Matplotlib. The checkpoint, text and individual TTT states stay in the scratch directory. [Pinned model files](https://huggingface.co/RetentionLabs/TTT-Linear-125M-Base-Books-2k/tree/8925d60c24c836b38952e9ef5ecdc445156a7884) · [Pinned dataset files](https://huggingface.co/datasets/Salesforce/wikitext/tree/b08601e04326c79dfdd32d625aee71d232d685c3/wikitext-2-raw-v1)

```sh
export OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1 OMP_NUM_THREADS=1
mkdir -p "$OUT"
python notes/ttt_state_probe.py prepare "$DATA"
python notes/ttt_state_probe.py prepare-validation "$DATA"
python notes/ttt_state_probe.py extract "$DATA"
python notes/hankel_state_fit.py "$DATA/states.npz" "$OUT"
python notes/hankel_state_fit.py --calibrated "$DATA/states.npz" "$OUT"
python notes/hankel_online_recall.py "$OUT/online_recall.csv"
python notes/ttt_online_eval.py verify "$DATA" "$OUT"
python notes/hankel_state_fit.py --priors "$DATA/states.npz" "$DATA"
python notes/hankel_state_fit.py --prior-metrics "$DATA/prior_metrics.npy" "$DATA/states.npz" "$DATA"
python notes/hankel_state_fit.py --prior-metrics "$DATA/prior_metrics.npy" --frames "$DATA/frames.npy" "$DATA/states.npz" "$DATA"
for mode in dense bias_only projected32_only projected32 rank16_residual hankel_residual hankel_only hankel_calibrated hankel_aligned
do
    python notes/ttt_online_eval.py "$mode" "$DATA" "$OUT"
done
python notes/hankel_memory_results.py "$OUT"
```

### Matrix memory for Mamba-2 and linear attention

**Compact baseline.** Mamba-2 Table 5 includes a 126.5M-parameter language model trained on 2.5B tokens. Each layer has 24 heads with 64 key coordinates and 64 value coordinates, or 98,304 recurrent matrix entries per layer. All heads share the projected key k and query q. Each head has its own value and token-dependent scalar decay. In column-vector notation the inner state is `Sₜ = aₜ Sₜ₋₁ + uₜ kₜᵀ`, with read `yₜ = Sₜ qₜ`. The released step implementation uses `aₜ = exp(Δₜ A)` and `uₜ = Δₜ xₜ` for the head value x. With zero initial state, each past value uₛ contributes to yₜ in proportion to its key–query dot product kₛᵀqₜ times the intervening decays. Independent writes can build a full-rank state. For example, 64 successive basis keys write its 64 columns, with older columns scaled by subsequent retention. An optical implementation of the complete block must also account for token projections, the short convolution, direct skip, gates and normalization. [Dao and Gu, §9.4.2 and Table 5](https://arxiv.org/html/2405.21060#S9.SS4.SSS2) · [Authors’ single-token implementation](https://github.com/state-spaces/mamba/blob/main/mamba_ssm/modules/mamba2.py#L289-L325)

#### Addressing an outer product in fiber

With simultaneous, equally spaced 64-tooth value and key combs, the FWM family at `fᵥ[i]+fₖ[j]−fᴿ` puts vᵢkⱼ on idler i+j. There are 127 observable sums. For example, v₀k₁ and v₁k₀ both reach idler 1. To store separate signed-real entries, give column j its own time slot. Present just key tooth j, the full value comb and the fixed reference in that slot. In the weak-conversion model the 64 idler fields are proportional to vᵢkⱼR*. Inject them coherently into the returning column, stored on teeth j through j+63. All 4,096 (slot, tooth) addresses are distinct. This requires key serialization, 64 deliveries of the value comb and phase matching across its band. A single shared guide needs 64 slots (1.28 ns at an assumed 20 ps pitch). Ideal passive replication of one fixed-energy packet provides 1/64 of its energy per copy (−18.1 dB) before conversion or write-tap loss. Reading this layout requires a sum over columns. A row layout can perform each row dot product within the guide and then align its outputs for the next layer. [FWM mixing law, Guo et al.](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-018-31470-x) · [Row write and read interface](#a-dense-ttt-memory-written-one-row-per-slot)

**Restricted key for a full matrix.** With `kₜ = eⱼ` and `j = t mod 64`, the increment is one copy of vₜ in column j. Store every column on the same 64 wavelengths, distinguished by slot. A fixed coupler adds a value packet on the memory band only in the scheduled slot, with the coupling amplitude absorbed in the value scale. Every column experiences the prescribed loop decay. Starting from zero, 64 successive independent value writes can yield a full-rank matrix. The clock chooses the write address. A learned one-hot address would require an optical selector. Test retrieval when later inputs collide in a slot, and budget the dense query read separately. This layout stores the matrix entries directly.

**Accumulating a generated update.** Start with one fixed field retention γ per head. Different heads retain information for different durations, while learned keys and queries select the content. This is the retention core of RetNet when its positional factors are included in the key and query. RetNet was trained through 6.7B parameters. For selective retention, a phase-trimmed coherent coupler can return `aₜ Sₜ₋₁ + √(1−aₜ²) vₜ kₜᵀ` if the incident update field already represents the outer product. This is exactly decayed dot-product attention with effective value `uₜ = √(1−aₜ²) vₜ`. The optical gate law changes the input scaling relative to standard Mamba-2. Hawk and Griffin use the same square-root coefficient on a vector state. Its use on this optical matrix state is a proposal. At a = 0.99 only 1.99% of the incident update power enters the storage port. Include optical generation and distribution of any token-dependent gate, read taps, idle loss and coherent write noise over the recall horizon. [RetNet, §2.2 and Table 2](https://arxiv.org/html/2307.08621) · [Hawk and Griffin, Eq. 4](https://arxiv.org/html/2402.19427v1#S2.SS4)

**One FWM layout for explicitly stored entries.** Encoding a whole 64 × 64 write in frequency would place vᵢ at offset i and kⱼ at 64j, giving each product its own idler i+64j. The 4,096 idlers would span 40.95 THz on a 10 GHz grid, before guard bands. An 8 × 8 tile uses 64 distinct state teeth. Number local key coordinates i and value coordinates j from 0 to 7. In separate carrier bands put k and q at offsets 8i from their respective origins and v at j, all in units of one comb spacing. The `k + v − reference` frequency family writes kᵢvⱼ at 8i+j. On read, `reference + state − query` produces offset 8i+j−8i′. Filtering for offsets 0…7 selects precisely i = i′ and yields all eight row sums on separate teeth. The equations assume signed-real fields, flat first-order coupling and separated mixing families. A full 64 × 64 head comprises 8 × 8 **tiles**, with eight partial output combs added spatially per value group. A fully parallel layout needs 64 tiles per head. The choice of which tiles share a guide determines where the read results must be added. Shared keys across model heads also need optical fan-out. [Dual-pump FWM field, Eq. 16](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6115365/) · [Measured small FWM dot products](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.22.002609)

With ideal couplers and gains calibrated out, a 256-token check computed writes by convolution and reads by correlation on those teeth. For 64-dimensional signed Gaussian values and unit-normalized Gaussian queries and keys, both the tiled read and the explicit attention sum agreed with the dense coupler recurrence to within 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ maximum relative vector error (NumPy seed 802, a drawn uniformly from 0.88 to 0.997). The read power budget is a separate constraint. At the earlier extrapolated η = 0.468 per tile (50 mW reference and 50 mW total in the stored comb at the guide), equal-power state teeth and one lit query tooth put η/8 into the eight useful idlers of one tile. For a full head, ideally splitting the query eight ways and collecting one port of each eight-way spatial sum gives η/64 (−21.4 dB) across all 64 useful outputs, or η/4,096 (−39.4 dB) per tooth relative to the launched tooth. Interference changes these numbers on dense queries. Check actual phase matching, spectral isolation, attainable state power and fresh noise per token before claiming a device gain or model accuracy. [Single-pump measurement underlying η](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447)

**Eight-guide schedule for one dense head.** Assign eight columns to each FWM guide and store successive eight-row groups in eight memory slots. During a read, replay each guide's eight query teeth across the slots. In slot r, all guides produce partials for the same eight rows on the same eight output wavelengths. A phase-aligned, balanced 8-to-1 combiner delivers their field sum divided by √8. The output streams eight completed coordinates per slot. A simultaneous 64-tooth interface would also require wavelength assignment and retiming. Assigning a row group to each guide instead would require summing eight successive partial vectors in time. For a delta write, first obtain the residual e = v − Wk using the calibrated read gain. In slot r, broadcast its eight entries $e_r$ to the guides. Guide c replays its key group $k_c$ and coherently adds $\beta e_r k_c^\mathsf{T}$ to the corresponding stored tile. All 4,096 entries retain separate addresses. Each full read or full write occupies eight slots.

For a proposed 20 ps slot pitch, delays of 20, 40 and 80 ps can generate the eight replicas. Qiu et al. demonstrated 64-fold pulse interleaving at 14 GHz. The 50 GHz comb replay proposed here requires amplitude and phase characterization. Eight equal passive copies of a fixed-energy packet each receive at most 1/8 of its energy. On a write, passive temporal replay of k and eight-way spatial broadcast of e therefore give at most 1/64 of the per-product idler power of an isolated tile supplied with both full-strength operands (−18.1 dB, equal pulse shapes, undepleted FWM, same reference power per guide). Port and propagation losses are additional. [Qiu et al., Figs. 1–3](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-59794-z)

**Practical product count per FWM guide.** For explicit, wavelength-addressed weights, N idlers on pitch Δf occupy (N−1)Δf between the outermost centers. If each address must accept a fresh product every 20 ps, ordinary Nyquist wavelength multiplexing gives an optimistic pitch of 50 GHz. At that rate 64, 512 and 4,096 products require 3.15, 25.55 and 204.75 THz of idler span alone. At a 100 ps update interval and 10 GHz pitch the spans are 0.63, 5.11 and 40.95 THz. The interval here is the time between updates to the same address. A 20 ps pulse can occur within a slower write cycle. Filters, modulation sidebands and separation from the launched bands require additional bandwidth. [Hillerkuss et al., Nyquist WDM](https://eprints.soton.ac.uk/343085/)

Guan et al. used FWM in one 5 mm AlGaAsOI waveguide to convert a time-multiplexed stream into 256 simultaneous 1 Gb/s channels on a 14 GHz grid. Lei et al. measured a 133 nm 3 dB conversion bandwidth in the 2.12 cm guide used for our power estimate by sweeping one signal past a fixed pump. A two-comb writer needs the complex conversion coefficient and crosstalk for every occupied pair under simultaneous loading. In the weak-conversion model, reducing an 8 × 64 write to 8 × 8 at fixed reference power, value-tooth power and total key power gives each equal key tooth eight times the power, or 9 dB more desired idler power per product. Covering the original columns then costs eight tile operations. Use 16 and 64 products per guide as simulation cases and compare field error at the same launched powers. [Guan et al., ECOC 2019](https://doi.org/10.1049/cp.2019.0740) · [Lei et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Kaminski et al., measured channel-loading penalty, Figs. 10–11](https://backend.orbit.dtu.dk/ws/files/192313703/HKKR_jlt_2933226_pp.pdf)

**Other published memory mechanisms.**

- For repeated-key replacement, Gated DeltaNet uses `S̄ = aₜ Sₜ₋₁`, `e = vₜ − S̄ kₜ`, `Sₜ = S̄ + βₜ e kₜᵀ`, then `yₜ = Sₜ qₜ`. It adds one forward read and a coherent subtraction. Aggregate the full prediction before broadcasting e to the write tiles. Yang et al. trained a 1.3B model on 100B tokens. [Yang et al., Table 1 and §4](https://arxiv.org/html/2412.06464v3)
- Sparse Delta Memory provides a design precedent for selecting individual slots. Its published experiments pair 64 reads and 64 writes with local attention in a hybrid model. The purely recurrent routing comparison is Mixture-of-Memories. [Mixture-of-Memories, §§3–4](https://arxiv.org/html/2502.13685) · [Sparse Delta Memory preprint, §§3–4](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.07386v1)
- H3 uses a delayed key, value products, learned linear filters and a query read. Its memory stores filtered histories. In the 125M OpenWebText experiment the purely recurrent H3 came within 0.4 perplexity of the authors' Transformer baseline. [Fu et al., Fig. 1, Algorithm 1 and Appendix E.2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2212.14052)

For the compact baseline, reducing key width from 64 to 8 cuts a head from 64 tiles to 8 and removes the sum across key groups. Mamba-2 found that raising key/state width from 16 to 64 to 256 consistently improved associative recall. [Mamba-2, Fig. 8](https://arxiv.org/html/2405.21060#S9.SS1) · [Multi-query associative recall](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.04927)

### Other forward-only optical memories

**Earlier wavelength-grouped linear-attention variant.** Route key or query teeth into disjoint fixed groups `Gᵦ = {j : j mod 8 = b}` and use one writable Hankel per group. Entry (i,j) uses coefficient hᵦ[s] for b = j mod 8 and s = i+j. Matrix updates have an exact Frobenius projection onto this fixed space. If mᵦₛ is the number of entries sharing the coefficient, add `conv(v, k restricted to Gᵦ)[s] / mᵦₛ`, keeping the masked key at its original frequency indices. Apply the common retention to each stored coefficient. This tracks the projection of the dense additive state at every token for scalar decay and matching initial states. The normalization is static. There are 960 nonempty coefficients (Σₛ min(8, length of anti-diagonal s)), with up to eight entries tied per coefficient. A reversal is full rank in this space. This variant requires wavelength routing, eight deliveries of v and coherent output summation. Its retrieval accuracy and power budget are open.

**A product of directly written Hankel factors.** Store 34 time slots of 127 weight teeth. Before the write, send the current optical query through the 34 old factors from oldest to newest. Replace the oldest factor with one comb made from the current token, then advance the starting slot by one. A pre-encoded comb on the weight band needs one timed exchange. Alternatively the existing FWM write interaction can produce `uₜ[s] ∝ R* Σᵢ₊ⱼ₌ₛ kₜ[i] vₜ[j]` in one forward visit using 64-tooth key and value packets on the two data bands and the fixed reference R. This pools products on each anti-diagonal. The write needs neither cached intermediate activations nor reverse visits. The 4,318 stored fields encode an ordered 64 × 64 operator. Foerster et al. trained input-dependent matrix transitions on language data. The window of optically stored Hankel factors is a proposed adaptation. [Foerster et al., input-switched affine networks](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v70/foerster17a.html) · [FWM write and carrier placement](#backpropagation-and-the-ttt-weight-update)

Full replacement gives the layer a direct 34-token window. With a clocked bypass on unselected visits and field retention 0.9 on each selected visit, an old contribution has a 224-token half-life from mixing alone. The query still incurs all 34 conversions. In a noiseless check of 64 programs, the median stable rank `‖S‖²_F / ‖S‖²₂` was 1.76 for independent random factors and 17.40 for `J + 0.1 H(g)`, where J is the central-tooth reversal. Each factor used a new 127-vector of independent Gaussian coefficients with variance 1/64 (NumPy generator seed 37). Stable rank measures the spread of read gain across query directions. Check sensitivity to the newly written packet as well, since the fixed central tone can dominate. [Write tap and retention assumptions](#writing-a-hankel-factor-through-a-coupler)

**A rolling key–value memory on the parallel pairs.** Store one 64-tooth optical key and one 64-tooth optical value in each of 32 branches, giving 4,096 fields. Read with the current query and overwrite just the oldest pair on a clocked schedule. Both packets must reach their respective weight carriers at the required pump power. For signed-real fields, pad each stored vector with 63 trailing zeros and define `H(a)[i,j] = a[i+j]` on zero-based indices. With a fixed diagonal intermediate passband mask D the ideal read is `Σ_b H(v_b) D H(k_b) q` up to calibrated common gain. Selecting intermediate tooth zero gives exactly `Σ_b v_b(k_bᵀq)`, the unnormalized sliding-window linear-attention numerator. Its rank is at most 32. Selecting m teeth gives up to m shifted outer products per slot with shared coefficients. A full band can give a full-rank read matrix even on one slot, with tied entries. The array sums branches spatially. Serializing this read on one guide requires a cross-slot accumulator. [Jiang et al., rolling KV cache, Fig. 2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.06825) · [Schlag et al., outer-product attention](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html)

The intermediate mask changes retrieval even with perfect optics. For one stored pair, take independent ±1/8 key and value entries and query with the stored key. The first intermediate tooth returns v exactly. Passing the first m teeth adds shifted copies of v weighted by nonzero-lag key autocorrelations. Averaging over the codes gives `E‖y−v‖² = Σ_{r=1}^{m−1}(64−r)²/64³`. The RMS error is 12.3% for two adjacent teeth and 57.1% for all 64. A check with 8,192 random pairs gave 12.2% and 57.0% (seed 38). These numbers describe a specific untrained code and ideal field gain.

**Holographic associative memory.** Danihelka et al. trained an associative LSTM on character-level language modeling. In its circular-convolution formulation the write adds `k ⊛ v` to a stored trace. Correlating the trace with q retrieves a value. A unit-modulus Fourier key retrieves its own single stored value exactly at unit retention and ideal gain. Other stored pairs contribute interference. The paper averages traces with differently permuted keys. With 64 copies of 64 teeth the bank stores 4,096 fields and each read requires combining the copies. On this FWM grid a 64-by-64 convolution produces 127 linear-convolution teeth. A circular write must coherently add bins s and s+64 where both exist, or use a periodic operand encoding with its own bandwidth and power budget. [Danihelka et al., §§2–3 and 6](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v48/danihelka16.pdf)

**A dilated RNN with optical hidden-state packets.** At dilation 64 keep 64 packets of 64 teeth. Each token releases `hₜ₋₆₄`, computes `hₜ = F(xₜ,hₜ₋₆₄)` with fixed trained optics, emits the required output and returns hₜ to that slot. This gives 4,096 state fields, a single scheduled packet access and a 64-tooth output. Shorter-dilation layers mix information between residue classes. This architecture retrieves by age. Its recurrent matrices stay fixed during inference. Include state/output tap loss and storage gain in the comparison. [Chang et al., Dilated Recurrent Neural Networks](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2017/hash/32bb90e8976aab5298d5da10fe66f21d-Abstract.html) · [Clock-only optical coupler](#recurrent-memory-with-two-optical-waveguides)

The factor window and dilated memory are controls for how much recall a clocked write can support at the same state size and encoder capacity. Count FWM visits, amplification and field error per completed token alongside the model scores.

## Recurrent memory with two optical waveguides

For one head, let Wₜ₋₁ be the 64 × 64 state just before token t. **Row i is wavelength i. Column m is one circulating packet.** At zero-based time slot j = 0,…,31, tooth i of loop A carries Wₜ₋₁[i,2j] and tooth i of loop B carries Wₜ₋₁[i,2j+1]. The two packets thus present columns a = Wₜ₋₁[:,2j] and b = Wₜ₋₁[:,2j+1] at the same instant. A stored coordinate is a signed optical field amplitude. The MZI settings select how to access those fields.

**One token with a shared read and write key.** Let hₜ be the incoming hidden vector. One proposed token interface has a fixed learned value projection vₜ = Pᵥ hₜ, delivered as a 64-tooth optical packet. A separate learned controller takes hₜ and supplies five address bits for j and one real angle θ. For example, threshold five projected control features for the bits and scale a sixth feature for the angle. The address controls the time of the exchange and the angle controls the two MZI drives. Together these encode the mathematical key kₜ = c e₂ⱼ + s e₂ⱼ₊₁, where c = cos θ, s = sin θ and eₘ is the 64-dimensional basis vector for column m. Token-derived control is electro-optic in this proposal. The numerical checks below supply the address, angles and value directly.

| Component in the selected slot | Optical inputs | Optical outputs |
| --- | --- | --- |
| First MZI | Stored packets a and b | Prediction p = ca + sb, and complementary field d = −sa + cb |
| Timed 2 × 2 exchange switch | Prediction p and the synchronized incoming value vₜ | p goes to the layer output as yₜ. The switch sends vₜ to the first input of the second MZI. |
| Second MZI, inverse rotation | vₜ and d, which bypasses the switch | a⁺ = c vₜ − s d to loop A, b⁺ = s vₜ + c d to loop B, both in slot j |

Here **yₜ = Wₜ₋₁ kₜ** is the value predicted by memory *before* this token writes. The compact recurrent layer uses yₜ as its output. After the write, Wₜ kₜ = vₜ. A TTT layer whose specified output is Wₜ qₜ for an independent query requires another access. All 64 output coordinates of yₜ travel together as one comb. This field model assumes matched group delays, a common phase reference, calibrated phases and the same rotation across the comb.

For example, select slot 3 (columns 6 and 7) with c = 0.6 and s = 0.8. On one wavelength let the stored amplitudes be 0.2 and 0.4 and the arriving value be 0.7. The circuit emits 0.44, retains d = 0.08, and writes 0.356 and 0.608 to the two loops. Reading that key after the write gives 0.6 × 0.356 + 0.8 × 0.608 = 0.700.

- **Same read and write direction.** The entire memory follows Wₜ = Wₜ₋₁ + (vₜ − Wₜ₋₁kₜ)kₜᵀ, exactly one unit-step gradient update for ½‖Wkₜ − vₜ‖². For a step size 0 < β < 1, an additional variable coupler can leave (1−β)p in the memory port and mix in √(β(2−β)) times an incident target scaled by √(β/(2−β)). This gives the exact coefficient βvₜ. The emitted port then mixes the old prediction and target. [Delta-rule fast weights](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html)
- **Independent directions in the same slot.** Let A = [a, b], with two-component unit vectors q for the first rotation and k for the second. For u = (u₁,u₂), write u⊥ = (−u₂,u₁). The output is the pre-write projection Aq. The circuit returns A⁺ = vₜ kᵀ + (Aq⊥) k⊥ᵀ, so A⁺k = vₜ and A⁺k⊥ = Aq⊥. The retained association changes basis. This defines a recurrent operator to train directly. A later read that must preserve the old association costs optical power. For instance, a 10% tap delivers −10 dB to the reader and leaves −0.46 dB on the stored projection before device losses.
- **Power.** With the full exchange and ideal components, ‖A⁺‖²_F + ‖y‖² = ‖A‖²_F + ‖v‖². Both rotation outputs do useful work. At a 45° read angle one lit memory rail sends half its power to y and half to the retained field. Other sources of loss include coupler insertion, bypasses, propagation and any phase or amplitude stabilization. Restoring circulating power with an amplifier introduces noise dependent on the number of laps since a write.

| Key coordinates in an addressed group | Groups in a 64-column state | Physical implementation | Access tradeoff |
| ---: | ---: | --- | --- |
| 1 | 64 | Timed packet exchange | Exact lookup and overwrite of a routed address. Address collisions erase previous content. |
| 2 | 32 | Two broadband rotations and timed injection | One angle each for read and write. Per-token access is confined to two columns. |
| 8 | 8 | Two seven-coupler trees, three levels each | One active group can combine and update eight columns. |
| 64 | 1 | Two 63-coupler trees, six levels each | One token may read and update all 4,096 fields with arbitrary real unit keys. All 64 columns must be available together. |

A tree exposes the selected projection at its root. Carry every complementary output to the corresponding input of the second tree with matched delays. The counts exclude the exchange, bypasses, phase trims and routing. For a uniform 64-coordinate key each individual field-to-read power coefficient is still 1/64. Sacchi et al. demonstrated a 16-channel tree with 15 thermally controlled MZIs. Takeda et al. demonstrated a variable beam splitter and coherent recirculation with 66 ns timing and about 7% round-trip loss at 860 nm. These are component precedents. A per-token router, broadband rotation and coherent analog retention at the paper's timing need independent budgets. [Pai et al., binary trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.16935) · [Sacchi et al.](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41377-025-01977-w) · [Takeda et al., Fig. 2 and Methods](https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aaw4530)

**Control and storage.** Both interferometers share one key angle in the delta-rule version. Independent directions require a second angle. Include any optical tap, detection, drive, switching guards and waiting for the addressed slot in token latency and energy. The 32 slots occupy at least 640 ps at 20 ps pitch. An archived 150 m fiber loop has roughly 0.74 µs round-trip time at group index 1.47. Collected outputs may need a whole-packet delay to reach the next layer on its clock. Keeping a stored field within 3 dB of its original power over 100 unamplified laps requires at most 0.03 dB round-trip loss.

For matched read/write keys, an update changes the answer at any other query u by (v − Wk)(kᵀu). Different banks have exactly zero interference in the ideal circuit. Within a bank, two orthogonal keys give independent associations. With uniform random banks and independent directions, two keys share a bank with probability 1/32 and have mean squared overlap 1/2 conditional on sharing it. The global mean squared overlap is 1/64, the same as for independent isotropic dense keys in 64 dimensions. Learning which associations share a bank is central to retrieval quality.

**Alternative with clock-only control.** A fixed coupler feeding a D-slot loop implements hₜ = a hₜ₋ᴅ + c uₜ, yₜ = c hₜ₋ᴅ − a uₜ, with a² + c² = 1. At D = 64 and 64 wavelengths this also holds 4,096 fields. Fixed optics give a temporal filter with a known delay. A nonlinear value projection or subsequent layer can still learn from its output. Token-dependent retention and independently addressable associations are additional capabilities. The normalized state/input coefficients have a language-model precedent in Hawk and Griffin's gated linear recurrent unit, which also uses learned channel-wise gates. Luo et al. provide an optical precedent for adaptive recurrent processing using a feedback delay and a fast diagonal operator. [De et al., §2.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2402.19427v1#S2.SS4) · [Luo et al.](https://doi.org/10.1186/s43593-026-00139-8)

**Checks.** In 1,024 random paired writes, the optical field equations matched dense arithmetic for the same sparse normalized keys to 2.3 × 10⁻¹⁶ maximum relative state error. In a separate test, two assigned orthogonal keys per group stored 64 independent value vectors. Over 8,192 random reads with replacement writes, independent Gaussian rotation errors of 1° and 3° RMS on each active MZI produced 3.44% and 10.3% RMS recall field error. This sensitivity isolates angle errors with perfect addresses and idle storage. The next comparison is learned routing versus a full-key digital DeltaNet and a clocked delay memory, all with 4,096 state values and the same value projection. Measure associative recall, repeated-query behavior and held-out language-model loss, then sweep slot age, phase drift, switch leakage and amplifier noise. [Reproduce the checks](recurrent_exchange.py) · [Arora et al., multi-query associative recall](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.04927)

### Exchanging one association in a full matrix

Store a real 64 × 64 matrix W directly in fiber. In the time-bin design, packet j holds the entire column W[:,j] on 64 wavelengths. Given a unit write key k and a new value comb v, the target is to emit the old prediction p = Wk and return W′ = W + (v − p)kᵀ to storage. The write changes the response at a different query q by exactly (kᵀq)(v − p). Thus overwrites preserve all orthogonal associations. Arbitrary nonorthogonal keys generally interfere even with ideal dense delta updates. An independent post-write read W′q requires another physical access. [Delta-rule fast weights](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/schlag21a.html)

**Six-angle time-bin interferometer.** The model supplies six angles for one token. At level j, every pair of slots differing in bit j is mixed by the same symmetric two-port matrix with rows (cos θⱼ, sin θⱼ) and (sin θⱼ, −cos θⱼ). The six levels act on different binary indices, so they commute. Their joint transform U is its own inverse. Its first column k is the tensor product of the six pairs (cos θⱼ, sin θⱼ), most significant bit first. Send the 64 packets through the device. The first outgoing packet of WU is Wk. Exchange just that packet with v, retain the other 63, and send the entire frame forward through the same device at the same settings. This yields W′. The value and old prediction cross once. In ideal lossless optics the bank energy plus the emitted prediction energy equals the old bank energy plus the incident value energy.

A soft key can have all 64 coordinates nonzero, but its six angles restrict which unit vectors the network can query. Choosing each angle to be +π/4 or −π/4 gives 64 mutually orthogonal dense Walsh keys. An arbitrary value for each key specifies an arbitrary W. A workload confined to these fixed keys is a lookup workload. The dense MVM comparison below applies to general soft reads. Using electronic controls for the angles makes this a hybrid memory. An all-optical implementation needs the token to set six calibrated broadband rotations.

**Physical costs.** One time-multiplexed implementation uses delay strides of 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 and 32 slots. At each stage a clocked router separates the halves of each group, delays the first half to overlap the second in a tunable coupler, then delays one output and reserializes both. This gives six physical mixers, two clocked routing elements per stage, and two delays of each stride, plus the frame return path and the exchange switch. At 20 ps pitch the fabricated differential delays sum to 2.52 ns, about 51 cm of fiber at group index 1.47, before return routing. Each stored field traverses the six stages twice per write. The coupler angles can be held between traversals, but the routers still switch within each frame. Cui et al. demonstrated time-bin Hadamard routing with about 20 dB net loss in their four-stage receiver. They discuss under 0.4 dB per stage as a future target. The adjustable 64-bin, 64-wavelength optical memory needs its own insertion loss, phase drift and switching-energy budget. [Cui et al., discussion and experimental design](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-59107-4)

**Recall checks.** After 64 initial writes and 128 random replacements on the Walsh keys, the largest relative error across the entire stored value bank was 8.6 × 10⁻¹⁵. For a sequence of 128 soft writes a separate soft query after every write agreed with direct dense delta to 2.5 × 10⁻¹⁵. This checks the transfer algebra. In 128 trials with 32 random keys, independent random values, and one write per key from an empty bank, the RMS recall error after the last write was 55.3% for product keys and 54.1% for random unit vectors on the full 64-dimensional sphere. Fixed orthogonal codes and unconstrained soft keys give very different recall. In a separate 2,048-overwrite Walsh test, 0.25° RMS angle jitter gave 2.1% recall error if both traversals shared the same perturbation and 12.3% if the errors were independent. This models six common rotation errors per traversal and omits wavelength dependence. [Reproduce the experiments](temporal_mode_exchange.py)

**Energy versus retention.** Count 4,096 MACs for one general read with simultaneous overwrite. Assume a full bank of 64 equal-energy columns, equal mean incoming/outgoing value energy, 1550 nm, uniform loss L across both traversals and one compensating amplifier. With column energy E, the added *signal* light costs 64E(1 − 10^(−L/10))/0.121 on the electrical rail. The 12.1% efficiency was measured under other loading conditions. For a clean write followed by A visits without overwriting that key, take G = 10^(L/10) and effective spontaneous-emission factor 1.58. Each visit adds 1.58(G − 1) uncorrelated ASE photons per matched mode. If S is the mean number of signal photons per tooth just after a clean write, the relative RMS field error from ASE alone is √[A × 1.58(G − 1)/S]. Uniform random accesses to 64 orthogonal keys have mean revisit interval 64. Other access patterns can have much longer intervals. [Amplifier measurement](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.23.022431) · [Noise convention](#amplifier-noise-between-layers)

| Column energy | Loss per full update | Signal replenishment only | ASE field RMS, 64 visits | ASE field RMS, 1,000 visits |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 0.2 pJ | 1 dB | 5.3 fJ/MAC | 3.3% | 13.0% |
| 0.2 pJ | 4.8 dB | 17.3 fJ/MAC | 9.2% | 36.2% |
| 2 pJ | 1 dB | 53.1 fJ/MAC | 1.0% | 4.1% |
| 2 pJ | 4.8 dB | 172.7 fJ/MAC | 2.9% | 11.4% |

The 4.8 dB case applies the published prospective 0.4 dB allowance to 12 stage traversals, with zero additional loss. For a 50 fJ/MAC target the full update has 204.8 pJ on the rails. At 0.2 pJ and 4.8 dB the signal replenishment uses 70.8 pJ, leaving 134.0 pJ for router drives, amplifier bias, calibration, key generation, other losses and output delivery. A 10 fJ/MAC target allows only 40.96 pJ total. These are design thresholds. Amplifier saturation, idle circulations, extra read taps, finite pulse overlap and coherence across the comb require separate budgets.

**Arbitrary optical keys with a mode-selective frequency converter.** Here each packet holds a *row* of W in its spectral modes. A shaped optical pump specifies k. An ideal two-input frequency converter swaps the amplitude of the row mode k with an auxiliary mode carrying the incoming scalar vᵢ. The old projection is emitted, the other 63 row modes propagate, and the same delta identity follows. Processing 64 serial rows reuses one guide and produces 64 serial answers. Price repeating or circulating the pump, serializing v, and repacking the read for a comb-to-comb interface. Bragg-scattering FWM has a theoretical mode-selective implementation. De et al. demonstrated programmable projections of up to 64 frequency bins using three-wave mixing. At 64 bins the pump had 0.125 pJ and the conversion was 0.1%. A later thin-film lithium-niobate preprint measured 89.6% internal conversion for one mode with 20 mW incident. Its selectivity measurements were made separately at lower gain. A direct single swap leaves a fraction √(1 − η) of the old selected field, so η > 99% is necessary for less than 10% old-field leakage. Near-unit transfer, undisturbed orthogonal modes, and low pump energy with both data ports populated are the relevant tests. [FWM proposal](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.23.023287) · [64-bin experiment, supplement](https://journals.aps.org/prresearch/supplemental/10.1103/PhysRevResearch.6.L022040/Frequency_bin_unitaries_supplement.pdf) · [High-conversion preprint](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.09346v1)

**Optically writable holographic crossbar.** Angular channels encode keys and values at a common carrier. Read by diffracting q through the stored index grating. For a write, inject coherent beams carrying k and the residual v − Wk. In the linear recording regime the cross interference writes the outer product throughout the material. Vlieg et al. describe the MVM and outer-product geometry and demonstrated an integrated photorefractive synapse. Their prototype grating diffraction efficiency was around 10⁻⁶. A competitive design would need multichannel write energy, read diffraction efficiency, speed and old-key retention under repeated exposures. This moves the recurrent state into the recording medium and requires a spatial interface for comb inputs. [Architecture](https://www.mdpi.com/2076-3417/12/9/4226) · [Integrated experiment](https://doi.org/10.1109/JSTQE.2024.3519983) · [Authors’ prototype efficiency](https://pure.tue.nl/ws/portalfiles/portal/354879050/2501.07917v2.pdf)

## Amplifier noise between layers

At 1550 nm use a 5 dB optical noise figure as a fiber-amplifier design point. Optilab specifies 4.5 dB for a C-band preamplifier with over 30 dB gain. Liu et al. measured about 7 dB at over 20 dB net gain in an integrated erbium amplifier. For an unsaturated phase-insensitive amplifier, write power gain `G` and noise factor `F` in linear units. The mean amplified spontaneous emission (ASE) per output mode is `m = (FG − 1)/2` photons. In one polarization the ASE power in optical noise bandwidth `B` is `m hν B`. At +30 dB gain and 5 dB noise figure this gives 2.53 µW at 12.5 GHz or 20.3 µW at 100 GHz, equivalent to 2.53 or 20.3 nW at the input. Collecting both polarizations doubles those powers. Use the actual filter bandwidth for each tooth. [Optilab preamplifier](https://www.optilab.com/products/c-band-msa-pre-amp-edfa-module) · [Liu et al., Fig. S11](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.abo2631) · [Baney et al., Eq. 13](https://perso.telecom-paristech.fr/gallion/documents/free_downloads_pdf/PG_revues/PG_R66.pdf)

**Noise figure at the loop gain.** Liu and Pun reported about 8 dB net gain and a 5.1 dB average noise figure over 1530–1560 nm in a packaged erbium–ytterbium glass waveguide. This supports 5 dB as a simulation assumption near the required +6.28 dB gain. The actual value depends on wavelength, pumping and total comb power. At 5 dB noise figure, +6.28 dB gain adds 6.21 ASE photons per matched output mode and polarization, versus 1,581 at +30 dB. As a sensitivity check, holding the effective spontaneous-emission factor `n_sp = m/(G−1)` at its +30 dB value of 1.58 gives a 4.24 dB noise figure at +6.28 dB. An ideal phase-insensitive amplifier has `m = G−1`, hence `F_min = 2−1/G` or 2.47 dB at the loop gain. For the balanced 34-pass example with 15,600 signal photons per mode, these three noise figures give 12.5%, 11.6% and 9.6% added RMS field error, respectively, including the FWM contribution. [Liu and Pun, packaged waveguide measurements](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.optcom.2007.01.036) · [Caves et al., Eq. 2.22](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1208.5174#page=8) · [Balanced loop calculation](#repeated-hankel-multiplication-in-a-data-loop)

**Reducing field error with gain on every pass.** For K balanced passes, useful power fraction q, linear amplifier noise factor F at gain 1/q, and S mean signal photons per matched mode, the squared added RMS field error is `ε² = K(1 + F/q)/(2S)`. A 1 dB improvement in useful conversion has the same effect as a 1 dB reduction in noise figure if the matrix shape and other parameters hold. For an illustrative 10% target at 34 passes, varying one quantity at a time from the 12.5% baseline would require a noise figure of 2.85 dB, useful conversion of 38.7% (up from 23.6%), or 24,510 signal photons per mode (up from 15,600). The last corresponds to 3.14 fJ per tooth at 1550 nm, about 314 µW in a 10 ps matched mode. Raising the signal power requires checking pump depletion, while lengthening the pulse changes the timing. [Balanced loop calculation](#repeated-hankel-multiplication-in-a-data-loop)

For an ideal energy read of one coherent pulse in a matched mode, `N = P_in τ/(hν)` is the input signal photon count. The mean output signal is `S = GN` and the photon-count variance including ASE is `S(1 + 2m) + m(m + 1)`. For a 10 ps value, 200 nW entering the amplifier gives about 46% RMS fluctuation relative to the signal, 20 µW gives 4.5%, and 26.7 µW gives 3.9%. A 1 nW tooth carries 0.078 photons in 10 ps or 5.0 photons over the archived 640 ps read. For field simulations, add independent circular complex Gaussian ASE per orthogonal mode with mean squared magnitude `m` in photon units and propagate it through later layers. At 26.7 µW per tooth, restoring 200 µW needs +8.75 dB. Applying +30 dB to 64 such teeth would require 1.71 W total signal output before ASE, so check gain compression at aggregate comb power. [Photon-number fluctuation theory](https://doi.org/10.1143/JPSJ.12.686) · [Archived read window](../archive/README.md)

**Cascaded amplifiers.** When each amplifier exactly compensates the preceding loss, each span restores the signal and the ASE from previous spans to the same powers. Independent ASE contributions add in power. For a clean input and identical unsaturated spans, optical signal-to-noise ratio (OSNR) after `K` spans is `OSNR₁/K` in a fixed bandwidth. At 1550 nm, 1 mW per channel, 20 dB span loss and gain, and 5 dB noise figure give about 33 dB OSNR after one span and 20 dB after twenty in a 12.5 GHz reference bandwidth counting both polarizations. The twenty-span value is 11 dB in 100 GHz. For an ideal single-mode analog path restoring 200 µW after each 10 dB loss, replace `m` by `K m` in the variance above. At 5 dB noise figure and 10 ps per value, the RMS pulse-energy fluctuation grows from 4.5% after one stage to 14% after ten. Actual matrix and activation stages transform the incoming noise. [Optical link noise accumulation](https://ee.stanford.edu/~jmk/pubs/mdg.pdg.ulh.jlt.20.pdf) · [2,800 km erbium-amplified digital transmission](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.20.00B217)

### Photon budget for two 34-pass banks

The reference packet is **100 ps on the 10 GHz grid**. The loop round-trip time and the shuffle also enter the layer latency. Keep all 68 balanced FWM visits, 23.6% useful conversion per visit, 5 dB amplifier noise figure and ideal routing for the table. Each visit adds 7.21 noise photons per accepted output mode at unity net signal gain. The full forward pass adds 490.2. For mean input signal occupancy S per tooth, the added RMS field error relative to the ideal vector is `ε = √(490.2/S)`. The table uses 1550 nm and one polarization. Power is averaged over the active packet.

| Target forward field error | Signal photons per tooth | Signal energy per tooth | Power per tooth for a 100 ps mode |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 10% | 49,000 | 6.28 fJ | 62.8 µW |
| 5% | 196,000 | 25.1 fJ | 251 µW |
| 2% | 1.23 million | 157 fJ | 1.57 mW |
| 1% | 4.90 million | 628 fJ | 6.28 mW |

**100 ps power sweep.** At 200, 300 and 400 µW per input tooth the respective forward errors are 5.60%, 4.58% and 3.96% with an ideal shuffle, or 6.86%, 5.60% and 4.85% with an uncompensated 3 dB shuffle. The 5% crossings are 251.3 µW with ideal routing, 252.7 µW with a 5 dB-noise-figure amplifier restoring the shuffle loss, and 376.3 µW with uncompensated loss. The earlier 17.7% figure used 200 µW for 10 ps. On the 10 GHz grid, 100 ps spans one orthogonal rectangular Fourier window.

At 400 µW, each tooth carries 40 fJ (312,000 photons) and a 64-tooth lane carries 25.6 mW of mean signal power during the packet. At 50 mW each, the reference and total weight comb supply 10 pJ combined per lane per visit. The balanced model predicts 6.03 mW of selected idlers per visit at this lane power, requiring at least 12% of the reference photons before discarded idlers. Check both pump depletion and the resulting matrix distortion across the power sweep. The 1% row would require 402 mW of signal per lane and about 95 mW of selected idlers per visit, exceeding the photon flux available from the 50 mW reference. [Steffensen et al., two-pump photon balance and depletion](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.19.006648)

Keep the pumps and weights coherent over the packet, allow for it in the slot schedule, and select the matching temporal mode before the optical activation or gradient write. At fixed optical noise bandwidth a longer observation admits more ASE modes. Out-of-mode light reaching an instantaneous nonlinearity needs its own noise model. [Optical subcarrier spacing and symbol duration](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.23.003886) · [Baney et al., ASE spectral density](https://perso.telecom-paristech.fr/gallion/documents/free_downloads_pdf/PG_revues/PG_R66.pdf)

At the original 2 fJ, a quantum-limited phase-insensitive amplifier at +6.28 dB gives 13.6% over both banks. Doubling useful conversion to 47.1% at a fixed 5 dB noise figure gives 13.0% if the matrix shape is preserved. For extra transmission t between the converter and amplifier on every visit, restoring each visit requires `G = 1/(qt)`. At 40 fJ per tooth and fixed noise figure a 5% forward budget allows 2.14 dB of this additional loss per visit with an ideal shuffle, 0.28 dB with the uncompensated shuffle, or 2.12 dB if a 3 dB, 5 dB-noise-figure boundary amplifier restores that shuffle loss. These budgets use the 64-mode conversion model and exclude extra phase-matched vacuum modes, technical noise and gain compression. [Quantum-limited amplifier](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1208.5174#page=8) · [Shuffle calculation](#connecting-two-loop-banks-through-the-monarch-shuffle)

For TTT let r be the clean residual RMS divided by the clean prediction RMS. A noiseless target gives `ε/r` relative error in the residual before backpropagation. At r = 0.10 the 400 µW, 100 ps point gives about 40% with ideal routing or 49% with the uncompensated shuffle. A 10% residual-error budget at that boundary requires 1% forward error, corresponding to the pump-limited row above. If the unscaled residual then traverses 68 equally noisy, balanced adjoints with ideal routing, independent noise gives `√2 ε/r` at the original key, before gradient-write noise. Rescaling the error field and compensating the learning rate can reduce the relative noise added on the return. Include reverse-replay tap loss, noise accumulated by stored weights and activations, and coherent writeback in the test of whether one update reduces the clean inner loss. [TTT update and replay schedule](#backpropagation-and-the-ttt-weight-update)

#### 20 ps candidate at 400 µW per tooth

At 400 µW during a 20 ps packet, each input tooth carries 8 fJ, or 62,400 photons at 1550 nm. Assuming independent matched modes and the same conversion and gain, the added RMS field error is **6.27% after one 34-pass bank**, **8.86% after both with ideal routing**, **10.84% with an uncompensated 3 dB shuffle**, and **8.89% if a 5 dB-noise-figure boundary amplifier restores that loss**. At a clean TTT residual of 10% of prediction RMS, the ideal-shuffle forward noise is already 89% of the residual RMS. A 5% two-bank forward target at 20 ps requires about 1.26 mW per tooth with ideal routing, or 1.88 mW with the uncompensated shuffle, assuming conversion holds at the higher signal power.

For a fresh vector on every 20 ps rectangular window, the magnitude of the normalized overlap of adjacent tooth waveforms is `|sin(π Δf T)/(π Δf T)|`. At 10 GHz it is 0.935. The first zero is at 50 GHz spacing. That grid spans 3.15 THz for 64 data teeth and 6.30 THz for 127 weights. Keeping the present band starts would put 14 A and B carriers on identical frequencies. The 20 ps error budget therefore requires a compatible carrier plan, phase matching, gain bandwidth and optical filters. A shorter pulse within a longer data symbol uses the full symbol interval for independent-vector throughput. The propagation estimate for one dependent vector still comes from the physical loop. [Lowery, optical subcarrier waveforms and matched filtering](https://ecse.monash.edu/staff/lowery/AJL%20Papers%20PDFs/Lowery_Photonic_Circuit_Topologies_JLT2618388.pdf) · [Loop timing](#useful-throughput-at-100-ps)

#### 20 ps with 100 mW of data per lane

At the guide input, 100 mW of data across 64 teeth gives 1.5625 mW per tooth, 31.25 fJ and 244,000 photons per matched mode. The 50 mW reference and 50 mW weight comb bring the total to 200 mW during the packet (4 pJ per lane visit). Holding the useful conversion at 23.6% and the amplifier noise figure at 5 dB gives **3.17%** added RMS field error over 34 balanced passes and **4.48%** over 68 with ideal routing. The two-bank error becomes **5.49%** with an uncompensated 3 dB shuffle or **4.50%** with its loss restored. If 100 mW includes all three beams, an equal three-way split gives 0.521 mW per data tooth, 10.5% useful conversion under quadratic pump scaling, and 11.4% ideal-shuffle noise over 68 passes.

The undepleted model predicts 23.6 mW in the 64 selected idlers. With uncorrelated input teeth and flat conversion across all idlers in the intended FWM family, it predicts 46.8 mW including discarded frequencies. Each generated idler consumes one reference photon and one weight photon. With empty idlers on entry and a common power transmission `t`, the reference and total idler photon fluxes obey `Φ_R(out) + Σ Φ_idler(out) = t Φ_R(in)` for this process. Using the measured 0.8 dB/cm over 2.12 cm gives `t = 0.677`, allowing about **33–34 mW across all exit idlers** at nearby telecom frequencies even with complete reference depletion. If the useful share stays at 64/127, at most about 17 mW reaches the selected outputs. [Lei et al., measured waveguide loss and weak-signal calibration](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Steffensen et al., two-pump photon balance](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.19.006648)

With ideal routing and gain restoring each balanced pass, useful output powers of 18.7, 4.47 and 1.97 mW per pass correspond to 5%, 10% and 15% added RMS field noise after 68 passes. At 17 mW the noise estimate is 5.2%. The 5–15% range can be used to test task sensitivity. Total field error can exceed 15% even with ample output power if depletion changes the relative amplitudes or phases. To quantify this, compare the noiseless, pump-depleted output with the target on held-out vectors using a fixed calibration, then repeat with amplifier noise.

For hardware scale, Pu et al. coupled a 400 mW CW pump into a 3 mm AlGaAs-on-insulator guide. In a multichannel converter, Kaminski et al. found that a 100 mW pump gave the best central-channel SNR near 20 mW total signal for up to seven channels. They attributed the declining optimum per channel to cross-phase modulation and interchannel mixing. [Pu et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1002/lpor.201800111) · [Kaminski et al., Figs. 10–11](https://backend.orbit.dtu.dk/ws/files/192313703/HKKR_jlt_2933226_pp.pdf#page=7)

#### FWM with the archived binary delay lattice

Compare one completed 64 × 64 bank at **2 pJ of launched data per lane** (100 mW × 20 ps), 1550 nm and a 5 dB amplifier noise figure. The loop reuses one packet for 34 visits. The temporal reader divides the same data energy among 64 copies, applies two independently pumped FWM conversions per slot, and reads the complete sum over 64 × 20 ps = 1.28 ns. The archived phase-ramp reference puts slot `m` in Fourier mode `u_m`, so different slot vectors are orthogonal. For slot fields `z_m`, the 128-tap lattice produces `Σ z_m/128` throughout the read. It therefore collects `64/128² = 1/256` of the burst energy in the held output. The archived 7 dB excess-loss assumption reduces this to **0.0779%**. Sampling a single 20 ps output slot collects 1/64 of that held energy. [Archived reader and source bundle](../archive/README.md#original-notes-code-and-results) · [Measured delay-interferometer topology](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-59794-z)

| Same dense real orthogonal matrix, one bank | Added RMS field error at equal launched data energy |
| --- | ---: |
| 34 balanced FWM visits, gain after every visit | 3.16% including modeled FWM and amplifier noise |
| Two FWM conversions per slot, one amplifier after the binary lattice | ≥241% from amplifier noise alone |
| Same reader, amplifier immediately before the lattice, perfectly gated to the 64 occupied slots | ≥53.7% from amplifier noise alone |

Both noiseless programs reproduce the same target up to one common field gain. To match the loop's 3.16% with a single output amplifier, the accepted mode needs about **1,580 coherent photons per mean output tooth** before gain. The two temporal estimates use the full held mode and omit spontaneous FWM noise, additional optical losses, depletion and distortion. Keeping 100 mW of data on throughout the 64-slot burst raises input energy to 128 pJ and gives respective amplifier-only errors of 30.1% and 6.7% if the transfer stays linear. Full restoration by the pre-lattice amplifier alone would then require 128 W of coherent signal across one lane during the burst (2.0 W at equal input energy). With the assumed FWM pump powers, the loop uses 68 pJ per bank and the temporal program uses 222 pJ in populated slots, before amplifier pumping or delivery losses. [FWM calibration](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Amplifier noise model](https://doi.org/10.1006/ofte.2000.0327)

To reproduce the target, for `d = 0, 1` set `θ_k = 2π(k+d/2)/64` and `h_d[s] = mean_k cos(sθ_k + 8 sin((d+1)θ_k))` over the 64 teeth. Form `U_d[i,j] = h_d[i+j]` and `W = (U₁ U₀)^17`. Both factors are real orthogonal, and normalizing the 127 weight teeth to fixed total power gives useful fractions `η/‖h_d‖²` of 0.2405 and 0.2338 at `η = 0.4677`. For the same W, the archived `simulation_notes/layer_cascade_screen.py` function `phase_ramp_encoding(W)` gives a peak adaptive coefficient norm `p = 1.54174` before rescaling. With 50 mW fixed reference per guide and 50 mW shaped-comb cap, the temporal reader's matched-energy transfer is `T = 10^(−7/10) [η/(128p)]² = 1.12 × 10⁻⁶`. This gives 0.273 coherent photons per mean output tooth before postgain. A restoring amplifier of gain `1/T` adds `(F/T−1)/2` photons per mode. Placing it before the lattice with ideal burst gating multiplies that noise contribution by `10^(−7/10)(64/128)²`, the squared overlap of the input noise slots with the held read mode. Delay copies of the same noise sample are correlated. The results use the weak-conversion transfer for both designs. The multitone response and pump depletion at these powers still require physical simulation.

### Amplified FWM in one 10 ps operation

The model uses 200 µW per active tooth at 1550 nm (15,600 photons per 10 ps), one matched mode and polarization, quiet pumps, and 743 µW final signal power per output compared. An unsaturated erbium amplifier with a 5 dB noise figure follows the guide. The RMS values are modeled pulse-energy fluctuations relative to the signal.

| Operation | Signal entering amplifier | Applied gain | Relative RMS |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| FWM Hankel, one input lit, one selected idler | 0.743 µW | +30.0 dB | 25.2% |
| FWM Hankel, 64 inputs lit, output at the mean power of an equal-magnitude scaled unitary | 47.6 µW | +11.94 dB | 3.1% |
| Single-tone FWM, using Lei et al.’s measured −7.3 dB conversion | 37.2 µW | +13.0 dB | 3.5% |

At −24.3 dB the selected idler contains 58 signal photons. The erbium amplifier adds 1,581 noise photons per output mode. Treating the idler as coherent gives 23.5% RMS. The phase-conjugating Hankel term also generates spontaneous pairs. The 64 modeled data-band modes contribute `m_FWM = Σᵢ |Hⱼᵢ|² ≈ 0.238` photons per output mode, with H in photon-flux units. Use `G m_FWM + m_EDFA` for m in the variance above. The single-tone row includes one coupled pair. Other phase-matched modes and pump fluctuations can raise the noise. Interference can leave individual matrix outputs dark. For a field-encoded MVM, normalize vector field error to the ideal vector norm. A known-phase quadrature has variance `(1 + 2m)/4` in units where the coherent signal amplitude is `√S`. [Lei et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [McKinstrie and Marhic, parametric fluctuation theory](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.21.019437)

## Multiplexed MVM benchmarks

### Useful throughput at 100 ps

For a fixed real matrix and one input vector, count a multiply and an add as two operations. An electronic implementation can precompute each 64 × 64 block from its 34 Hankel factors. On each visit the optical lane evaluates 64 dot products of length 64. Their 4,096 MACs correspond to 81.92 TOPS at continuous 100 ps spacing. Completing one programmable 64 × 64 block takes 34 visits for 4,096 useful MACs, giving **2.41 useful TOPS per lane** at full occupancy. Two banks of eight parallel blocks with the permutation between them perform `2 × 8 × 64² = 65,536` useful MACs per 512-element vector. This credits the block structure on both platforms. For workloads requiring complex arithmetic, report complex MAC/s separately.

| Schedule at one occupied 100 ps visit slot per guide | Complete vectors/s | Useful real TOPS |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| Eight lanes, banks processed serially with no overlap between vectors | 147 million | 19.3 |
| Two dedicated eight-lane banks overlapping independent vectors after fill | 294 million | 38.6 |

The packet-rate ceiling scales inversely with slot duration. A single dependent vector also traverses 72.08 cm of nonlinear guide per bank (34 × 2.12 cm). As a group-index estimate, Chang et al.’s 100 µm-radius AlGaAs-on-insulator ring with 118 GHz free spectral range gives `n_g = c/(2πR·FSR) ≈ 4.04`. Applying that value to Lei et al.’s 2.12 cm spiral gives 286 ps propagation per visit, 9.72 ns for one bank, and 19.44 ns for the forward layer before filters, switches, amplifiers, return paths and shuffle. The actual spiral group delay needs verification. At that propagation time, one packet at a time in each lane gives at most 0.84 useful TOPS per lane, or 6.74 TOPS for one dependent stream across eight lanes. Filling consecutive 100 ps slots in a longer loop requires interleaved packets with aligned weight slots. [Lei et al., nonlinear guide length](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Chang et al., ring radius and FSR](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1909.09778#page=10)

For a same-shape electronic comparison, Vishwakarma et al. report 1.48 TOPS for a 64 × 64 SRAM compute-in-memory macro in 65 nm CMOS schematic simulation with 4-bit unsigned inputs and weights. Five clocks at their quoted 724.63 MHz give about 6.9 ns to a result with weights already loaded. Matching that local latency with 34 serial optical visits would require about 203 ps per visit including overhead. Compare task accuracy in addition to dimensions. [Digital macro timing and Table I](https://doi.org/10.1109/ICEE67165.2025.11409700)

For whole-chip context, NVIDIA specifies 242 dense INT8 TOPS for L4 and 1,979 for H100 SXM. Batch-one bandwidth and data residency matter for a direct comparison. The two banks contain 64 KiB of precomputed real INT8 weights, small enough to fit in the H100’s 50 MB L2 cache. Fetching them anew from HBM at the rated 3.35 TB/s would take at least 19.6 ns for the weights alone. Benchmark the same two-bank MVM with resident weights at matched output accuracy. For per-token TTT, include weight updates and any recomputation in both implementations. [L4, Appendix D](https://images.nvidia.com/aem-dam/Solutions/Data-Center/l4/nvidia-ada-gpu-architecture-whitepaper-V2.02.pdf#page=40) · [H100 architecture](https://resources.nvidia.com/en-us-hopper-architecture) · [H100 memory bandwidth](https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/h100/) · [NVIDIA matrix–vector performance guide](https://docs.nvidia.com/deeplearning/performance/dl-performance-matrix-multiplication/index.html)

### Optical measurements

For the multiplexed core, report how many input and output modes one waveguide couples, how many inputs contribute appreciably to each output, and which coefficients share a weight. A 2 × 2 test can characterize an individual cross coefficient. For an arbitrary-matrix claim, also test whether the coefficients can be varied independently. With 200 µW on input 1 and a unit weight to output 2, the −10 dB threshold is 20 µW in the data-bearing output mode. Account for any independent read carrier. Drive multiple inputs together and report the RMS error on held-out vectors, modulation bandwidth and total control/read power at the same operating point. For an intended nonlinear activation, compare directly with its specified curve.

Digitally encoded inputs can also drive analog optical weights. Meng et al. transmitted bit-serial inputs, summed signed weighted contributions at a photodiode, and reconstructed the result by digital shifts and additions. This trades time slots and electronic readout for lower precision per analog read. Include the optical re-encoding cost if another optical layer follows. [Meng et al., Fig. 2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-62586-0)

## Optically controlled transmission and gain

Put a nonnegative input on a weak probe and a weight on a separate control beam. If the control sets a reproducible transmission or gain `T(P_w)`, the output power is `P_x T(P_w)`. Encoding each weight by its calibrated control level implements multiplication over the accessible transmission range.
