A live weight arriving as an ordinary analog intensity may need optical remapping. Measure leakage at zero weight and error with both operands varying on consecutive slots.

| Interaction | What hardware has shown |
| --- | --- |
| Kerr cross-phase modulation (XPM) | A weight beam changes the probe phase. A cavity or interferometer converts the phase shift to transmission on the original probe wavelength. Chen et al. switched a CW probe with a 7.5 mW peak on-chip control, 63 fJ per “1”, a 9 ps window and 8.3 dB on/off contrast at 40 Gbit/s. The passive transmission peak of the probe resonance had 2.2 dB insertion loss. The dynamic contrast leaves about 15% of the bright output at the dark level. The experiment used binary control and a fixed probe. [Chen et al., Figs. 2–4](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-73285-9) |
| Semiconductor optical amplifier (SOA) cross-gain modulation | The optical control depletes the carrier population, reducing the gain of a weak probe. Electrical bias supplies power. Matsuura et al. measured over 25 dB small-signal gain at 2 A, with 85% gain recovery in under 10 ps and the remainder in about 100 ps. Their filtered 320 Gbit/s binary converter delivered −6.36 dBm from a 6.9 dBm CW probe and 6.7 dBm modulated control (−13.26 dB relative to the input probe, using average powers). Relevant analog limits include gain memory, amplified spontaneous emission and gain shared by simultaneous channels. [Matsuura et al., Fig. 1 and §4.3](https://pure.tue.nl/ws/portalfiles/portal/3604162/703727992841251.pdf) |
| Cross-saturable absorption | A weight pulse bleaches an absorber, increasing transmission for a separate probe. Ono et al. measured 260 fs switching with a 35 fJ control in a graphene plasmonic guide. They report 19 dB total on-state insertion loss at 3 dB contrast. [Ono et al., Fig. 5 and Discussion](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1907.01764) |

**Nonresonant Kerr estimate.** Take the 2.12 cm AlGaAs guide parameters reported by Lei et al. (γ ≈ 437 W⁻¹m⁻¹, propagation loss 0.8 dB/cm). In an ideal balanced Mach–Zehnder, inject the control into one arm and take the probe port that is dark at zero weight. For matched arm loss and full temporal overlap, `P_out/P_x = exp(−αL) sin²(γ P_w L_eff)`, with power attenuation α = 18.4 m⁻¹ and `L_eff = (1 − exp(−αL))/α`. A 200 µW probe gives 19 µW (−10.2 dB) at 50 mW control in the arm or 27 µW (−8.7 dB) at 60 mW. This is a single-gate estimate from waveguide parameters. Routing and coupler excess losses, walk-off and parasitic mixing consume margin. A shared control also weights multiple probes together. Independent matrix rows require separately addressed weights and optical summation. A single-arm interferometer adds a weight-dependent output phase, which matters for coherent summation. [Lei et al., Fig. 2 and Table 1](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447)

**Kerr interaction length and footprint.** At 60 mW in the control arm, the model above gives −31.8 dB at 1 mm, −18.5 dB at 5 mm, and −13.3 dB at 10 mm. It first reaches −10 dB at 16.8 mm. At a fixed 1 mm length the same threshold needs about 0.75 W peak. Lei et al. fit their roughly 2 cm traveling-wave path in a 600 × 300 µm spiral. Dolgaleva et al. directly measured broadband XPM in a 9 mm AlGaAs waveguide. Chen et al.'s 7.5 mW resonant switch occupied 27 × 1.3 µm and used two specific cavity resonances for the control and probe. [Lei et al., Fig. 1](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447) · [Dolgaleva et al.](https://doi.org/10.1364/OL.35.004093) · [Chen et al., Figs. 2–3 and Discussion](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-73285-9)

**Arithmetic precedents.** Babaeian et al. varied two optical input energies and measured an approximate product using separate two-photon absorbers for the logarithms, optical addition, and a saturable absorber for the exponential. Their logarithmic fit covered 0.5–1.1 µJ per input pulse. If weights can be written before the data arrive, Ríos et al. demonstrated scalar multiplication with an optically programmed phase-change cell, and Feldmann et al. demonstrated a 9 × 4 matrix with stored phase-change weights and photodetector readout. [Babaeian et al., Fig. 4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-018-04578-x) · [Ríos et al., Fig. 3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6377270/) · [Feldmann et al., Methods](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-020-03070-1)

## Multiplication across a frequency comb

One optical control can modulate many weak probe teeth in one Kerr waveguide. After the interferometer, tooth `j` carries `P_j T_j(P_control)`. Matched transmission curves give scalar–vector multiplication. Two arbitrary combs need a way to select corresponding pairs for an elementwise product. For example, with only data tooth 1 and control tooth 2 illuminated, broadband XPM still shifts data tooth 1. Coherent control tones can also mix signal teeth separated by the same frequency difference through phase-matched four-wave mixing. Hui and Zhang demonstrated one optical modulation on four probe wavelengths in one fiber using XPM and sideband filters. [Hui and Zhang](https://doi.org/10.1134/S1054660X11050094) · [Wang et al., Fig. 1 and Eq. 2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41377-020-0299-7)

**One matrix column per slot.** Put nonnegative weights `W[:, i]` on the probe-tooth powers and calibrate the shared control for transmission proportional to input `x_i`. One slot gives all products in `x_i W[:, i]`. A 64 × 64 MVM takes 64 weight columns and an optical sum over the 64 slots for each output. Xu et al. demonstrated the shared-input encoding with an electro-optic modulator and 90 weighted comb lines. For the proposed optical control, the single-tooth estimate of 26.7 µW out is −8.7 dB relative to a 200 µW weight probe and −33.5 dB relative to the 60 mW full-scale data control. Check transfer, dispersion and mixing with all teeth present. For 64 equal, co-polarized 200 µW tones at the input, the mean probe power is 12.8 mW and the instantaneous peak can reach 819 mW if their phases align. [Xu et al., Fig. 1](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-020-03063-0) · [Lei et al., waveguide parameters](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.4c02447)

**Brillouin spectral shaping.** Redding et al. used a train of frequency-shifted control pulses and SBS to address a 50-tooth comb at 200 MHz spacing in fiber, achieving 30 dB modulation depth. This is line-by-line spectral shaping. [Redding et al.](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.457796)

## Stimulated Brillouin scattering

A frequency-matched pump gives a probe selective optical gain through an acoustic wave. At fixed pump power, a weak probe sees a programmable positive weight. Pump depletion changes the transfer curve. Frequency selectivity helps address neighboring channels. A separate SBS approach writes an acoustic grating and reads it with another beam. Time integration has been demonstrated this way, making it a possible route to optical summation. [Dynamic Brillouin grating experiment](https://doi.org/10.1038/srep01594)

**Two beams in one waveguide.** Kittlaus et al. measured +5.2 dB net on-chip gain on a seeded signal at 62 mW pump power in a traveling-wave silicon amplifier. In a separate intermodal device they measured +2.3 dB net small-signal gain at 88 mW pump power and, with two equally strong inputs of 75 mW each, 50% pump-to-Stokes energy transfer. Multiplication precision at the latter operating point depends on how pump depletion changes the response across input pairs. [Traveling-wave amplifier, Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1038/nphoton.2016.112) · [Intermodal transfer, Figs. 3–4](https://doi.org/10.1038/ncomms15819)

**Two inputs summed on a separate optical receiver.** Kim et al. drove a common acoustic mode from two emitter waveguides. The optical beat in each emitter drives the sound in proportion to the product of its two optical fields. An interferometer on the receiver turned the combined phase modulation into intensity modulation. They measured a 3.3 MHz envelope bandwidth around a 3.03 GHz beat using about 20 mW pump and 5 mW probe on chip. In a weak-signal design, assigning data and weight to the two fields could give one row sum. Reading the same single acoustic amplitude several times gives scaled copies of that row. Independent rows require new weights in successive slots or independently driven acoustic modes. [Kim et al., Figs. 1–5](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.2c00253)

**Speed of the acoustic sum.** The measured rise and decay time constants were 68 ns and 96 ns. With the drive off, about 98.6% of the acoustic amplitude would remain after 1.36 ns. This memory could integrate terms belonging to one row. Independent successive rows at that period would need a way to clear the previous sum or read a separate acoustic mode. A different geometry can hold a weight in a continuously driven acoustic grating and reflect faster read pulses from it. Antman et al. used 260 ps read pulses on such a grating. Pulsed SBS storage has reached 150 ps data pulses with about 20 W peak write/read controls. [Kim et al., Fig. 4](https://doi.org/10.1021/acsphotonics.2c00253) · [Antman et al., Fig. 4](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.20.007807) · [Stiller et al., Fig. 3 and Methods](https://doi.org/10.1038/s44310-024-00004-x)

**One data tooth to one generated sideband.** In a related emitter–receiver experiment, Shin et al. measured sideband power of about 1% of the receiver probe with two 36 mW emitter tones. Put data and weight on the two emitter tones. In the weak-signal model the sideband power is proportional to the product of data, weight, and receiver probe powers. With 50 mW each for weight and probe, the estimated sideband/data ratio is `0.01 × (50/36)² ≈ 0.019`. One 200 µW data tooth then gives about 3.9 µW (−17.1 dB). This one-coefficient extrapolation assumes the same effective interaction strength at the redistributed powers and excludes additional routing losses. [Shin et al., Fig. 3 and Supplementary Note 4](https://doi.org/10.1038/ncomms7427)

Slinkov et al. measured leaky-ReLU-like, sigmoid-like, and quadratic responses with **up to +2.4, +8.8, and +21.9 dB** gain respectively. Their two-stage fiber system also operated on two channels 3 GHz apart. The single-channel pump-amplifier settings were about 1.1–1.7 W. A narrowband silicon resonator separately achieved **over +20 dB net scalar gain with about 15 mW CW**. Independent gains supply diagonal weights. Producing different weighted sums at different outputs requires channel mixing. [Two-channel nonlinearities, Figs. 4–6](https://doi.org/10.1515/nanoph-2024-0513) · [Silicon Brillouin amplifier](https://doi.org/10.1364/OPTICA.6.001117)

## Stimulated Raman scattering

The Raman gain measurements, candidate matrices and noise estimates are archived for a possible floating-point encoding study. [Raman investigation](../archive/srs.md)

## Demonstrated matrix sizes and precision

A fixed amplitude range limits the smallest resolvable coefficient. A separate limit comes from the output range and noise. Even with binary weights and inputs, an exact 4,096-term dot product has 4,097 possible sums. Signed products can also cancel to a result below the output noise. Approximate inference can tolerate errors judged against the layer outputs and task. [Analysis of accumulator headroom and noise](https://doi.org/10.1145/3400302.3416344)

| Hardware | Demonstrated scale and scope |
| --- | --- |
| Lightmatter photonic processor | Four programmable 128 × 128 cores. Each 128-entry weight-row segment and input segment used its own maximum absolute value as a bfloat16 scale. The core used 7-bit weights and 10-bit inputs. After digitization, the electronics restored the product of the two scales and accumulated the partial sums. [Ahmed et al., Fig. 2c](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-08854-x) |
| IBM phase-change memory | 512 × 512 weight tiles. Two tiles could integrate up to 2,048 input terms in analog. The recurrent workload mapped dense 1,024-input, 4,096-output matrices across tiles, with some partial sums combined digitally. [Ambrogio et al., Fig. 1 and Methods](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-023-06337-5) |
| Coherent free-space optics | Optical matrix–matrix products of two 50 × 50 matrices. Tests used 50 random pairs per size and reported normalized RMS error below 0.1. [Zhang et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41566-025-01799-7) |
| Free-space optical dot product | 505,521 nonnegative products summed to one output. Normalized RMS error fell from about 6% at 0.001 detected photons per multiplication to 0.2% at 10. [Wang et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-021-27774-8) |

For scale, LightOn reports up to a million input and output modes with a fixed random scattering matrix. The direct intensity readout gives `|Rx|²`. An arbitrary N × N weight bank requires N² independent real coefficients. [LightOn architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2107.11814)

## Floating-point encodings

For one Hankel stage, a single scale for the 127 weight teeth preserves the shared anti-diagonals. With one scale for the 64 input teeth, every output field has the same scale product. Independent row normalization generally changes the Hankel structure. Fixed scales can be included in interstage gain calibration. If blocks use different scales, their partial fields need relative weighting before coherent addition. An adaptive scale also changes the physical intensity presented to a fixed-threshold saturable absorber.

Sobhanan et al. encoded the significand in the amplitude of a radio-frequency subcarrier and the exponent in its frequency. Two electrically driven modulators in series multiplied the significands and added the offsets on one optical carrier. A balanced detector read the product against a copy of the laser. Ten scalar tests gave about 1% to under 10% significand error and no exponent errors. Their proposed MVM uses a comb line per input and filters the detector output by exponent. If Aₖ is the signed mantissa sum in bin k, the dot product is Σₖ bᵏAₖ for base b. [Sobhanan et al., Figs. 1, 4 and 6](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.531813)

The bench launched 13 dBm (20 mW) at 1550 nm and used two signal-path amplifiers plus a third for the local oscillator. Fig. 3 measures spectral purity. From Eq. 3, an illustrative peak drive of 0.1 Vπ gives 2.47% useful sideband power per modulator (−16.1 dB before insertion loss). Their 512 × 512 model projects 2.3–3.4 fJ/MAC at 16 GBd. Its nominal 12-bit-range example uses three exponent bins and a four-bit signed significand, so a single tone has at most 48 input codes. A comparison at matched signed dot-product error would answer a different precision question. [Experimental setup, Fig. 2](https://www.researchgate.net/figure/Experimental-setup-of-cascaded-SSB-SC-modulation-TLS-Tunable-Laser-Source_fig2_382353483) · [Energy model, Fig. 7 and Appendix A.2](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.531813)

Klein et al. modulated two interferometer arms independently and selected the sum-frequency component after balanced detection. Six base-three exponents gave a 243-fold increase in representable range. These experiments encoded operands while computing. For persistent optical storage, Feldmann et al. stored decimal ones and tens in separate nonvolatile phase-change cells and demonstrated a carry between them. [Klein et al., Figs. 1–2](https://doi.org/10.1063/5.0216999) · [Feldmann et al., Fig. 3](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-017-01506-3)

Islam et al. demonstrated real-time five-bit floating-point vector multiplication with accumulation across wavelengths. A shared-exponent alternative is LightMat-HP. Each weight row and input vector has its own scale, so every term in their dot product has the same product exponent. Its prototype multiplied mantissa slices optically and accumulated and rescaled digitally. A shared scale simplifies summation, but the smallest coefficient in a row still has to fit the available mantissa precision. [Islam et al., CLEO 2026](https://doi.org/10.1364/CLEO_SI.2026.STU1E.6) · [Gong et al., 2026 preprint, Fig. 6 and §4.2.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12278)

**Cascading.** Sobhanan’s product exists as an optical field before detection. In principle a later driven modulator can scale every occupied exponent bin by the next mantissa and shift all bins by the next exponent. Linear layers can retain these bins if paths contributing to each output share a carrier and phase reference and the shifted spectrum fits the bandwidth. For an ordinary saturable absorber, an optical circuit could shift the bins to a common frequency and combine their fields with weights proportional to bᵏ. The exponent span then sets the required range of field weights. Giving the input vector and weight matrix one fixed scale each puts all outputs on a common scale for the absorber. Two related experiments demonstrated cascaded frequency-mode processing and, separately, a multilayer fiber loop with amplitude encoding, optical accumulation and a saturating semiconductor optical amplifier. [Sobhanan et al., Eq. 6](https://doi.org/10.1364/OE.531813) · [Rübeling et al., Fig. 2](https://doi.org/10.1515/nanoph-2025-0125) · [Sun, Zhu and Azaña, Figs. 1 and 4](https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aee9649)
